Difference operators and difference equations on lattices, or grids, up to the
elliptic hypergeometric case.

Abstract




I unwittingly daubed with a tar-brush the edges of a neatly-folded studding-sail which lay near me on a barrel. The studding-sail is now bent upon the ship, and the thoughtless touches of the brush are spread out into the word DISCOVERY.

E. Allan Poe,
Ms. found in a bottle.

Abstract.
It is shown how to define difference operators and
equations on particular lattices
\{x_{n}\}
, such that the divided difference operator
(\mathcal{D}f)(x_{n+1/2})=(f(x_{n+1})-f(x_{n}))/(x_{n+1}-x_{n})
has the property that
\mathcal{D}f
is a
rational function of degree
2d
when
f
is a rational function of
degree
d
. It is then shown that the
x_{n}
s are in the most general case values of an elliptic function at a sequence of arguments in
arithmetic progression (
elliptic lattice
). Many
special and limit cases, down to the most elementary ones, are
considered too. First and second order difference operators and equations are constructed, up to the simplest
elliptic hypergeometric ones. One also shows orthogonality and biorthogonality properties of rational solutions to some of these difference equations.

Keywords: Elliptic difference operators, Elliptic difference equations, Elliptic hypergeometric expansions, Interpolatory continued fraction, Biorthogonal rational functions.

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification:

33C05 Classical hypergeometric functions,
{}_{2}F_{1}

33D15 Basic hypergeometric functions in one variable,
{}_{r}\varphi_{s}

33E05 Elliptic functions and integrals
39A13 Difference equations, scaling (
q-
differences)
39A70 Difference operators
41A05 Interpolation

1.
Introduction: old and new difference calculus.

1.1.
Differences and sums

Discrete calculus is much older than calculus itself!

The Pythagoreans knew triangular numbers
T_{n+1}-T_{n}=n+1
, and squares as sums
of odd numbers (
gnomons
)
[
20
, chap. 4]

[
74
, §3.5]

[
16
, vol.1, p.161]
.

Archimedes established
N^{3}/3<1+2^{2}+\dotsb+N^{2}<(N+1)^{3}/3

[
19
, §17]
.

Boole wrote the first treatise on the calculus of finite differences in 1860
[
18
]
,
even if much had already been done earlier by Newton, Euler, the Bernoullis, or also
by Abel in this identity

\sum_{0}^{N}u_{n}(v_{n+1}-v_{n})=-u_{-1}v_{0}-\sum_{0}^{N}(u_{n}-u_{n-1})v_{n}+u_{N}v_{N+1},

(1)

introduced in 1826
[
1
]
, often called ”Abel’s sum formula”, or
partial summation, and which is a discrete rule of integration by parts
[
102
, §2.64]
.
This is just calculus without infinitesimals, and very elementary!
Why give great names to such easy identities? However, the formula
(
1
) appeared incidentally in a much deeper study of convergence of series,
as in recent works
[
31
,
32
]
,
and Boole is mainly concerned by the foundation of the theory of difference equations
[
18
, chap. VII- chap. X]
.

Discrete Calculus
is a field of contemporary applied mathematics, dealing with graph, or network, theory, algorithmic signal analysis
[
48
, chap. 1]
.

1.2.
Difference operators

Difference calculus considered initially arithmetic progressions, but more instances have been introduced, see Table
1
.

d

df(x)

\Delta

f(x+h)-f(x)

\nabla

f(x)-f(x-h)

\delta

f(x+h/2)-f(x-h/2)

G

f(qx)-f(x)

H

f(qx+\omega)-f(x)

W

f((t+i/2)^{2})-f((t-i/2)^{2})
,
x=t^{2}

AW

f(\cos(\theta+\lambda))-f(\cos(\theta-\lambda))
,
x=\cos\theta

NSU

f(x(s+1))-f(x(s))
,
x(s)=c_{1}q^{s}+c_{2}q^{-s}+c_{3}

or
x(s)=c_{1}s^{2}+c_{2}s+c_{3}

E

f(\mathcal{E}(s+1))-f(\mathcal{E}(s))
, where
\mathcal{E}
is an elliptic function.

Table 1.
Some difference operators. First column gives the name,
or the context: G is for geometric (why this name? see §
2
) or Heine, Jackson
[
68
]
,
H for Hahn
[
52
, eq. (1.3)]
, W for Wilson
[
8
, p.34]
, AW for Askey-Wilson
[
8
, (5.2)-(5.5)]
with
q=\exp(2i\lambda)
, NSU, equivalent to AW, for Nikiforov-Suslov-Uvarov
[
103
]

[
104
, §3.4]
, E for elliptic, Baxter
[
12
]
, Rosengren
[
119
]
,
Spiridonov & Zhedanov
[
123
,
124
,
126
]
(to be explained in full detail in the next sections).

Each row is a special case of the next rows.

\Delta
is found in Boole 1860
[
18
]
,
\nabla
(
nabla
) in Nörlund
[
105
]
,
\nabla
and
\delta
in Steffensen
[
127
]
and Milne-Thomson
[
102
]
.

Thiele
[
128
]
uses the notation
\delta(a,b,\dotsc)
for general divided differences.

We will encounter things as strange as the difference operator
f(x+\sqrt{x})-f(x-\sqrt{x})
, see also §
3.7
.

Why just these formulas, why not
f(\log x)
or
f(x^{3})
?

All the particular difference operators of table
1
give rise to
the
same
super class of difference equations solved by special functions

\phi_{m}
satisfying simultaneously

•

linear combinations relating several instances
\phi_{m}(x),\phi_{m\pm 1}(x),\dotsc
, for a given
x
;

•

a differential or difference equation relating
\phi_{m}(x),\phi_{m}(x\pm h),\dotsc
, for a given
m
.

These relations have sometimes a neat spectral form, called bispectral form
[
110
]
,
as
with Hermite polynomials
\phi_{m}(x)=H_{m}(x)
:
[
3
, §22.6,7]
,
[
108
, §18.8, 9]

\phi_{m+1}(x)+2m\phi_{m-1}(x)=2x\phi_{m}(x)
with eigenvalue
2x

-\phi^{\prime\prime}_{m}(x)+2x\phi_{m}^{\prime}(x)=2m\phi_{m}(x)
with eigenvalue
2m

or, to show a difference equation, with Charlier orthogonal polynomials
\phi_{m}(x)=C_{m}(x;a)

[
76
, 9.14]

a\phi_{m+1}(x)-(m+a)\phi_{m}(x)+m\phi_{m-1}(x)=-x\phi_{m}(x)
,
[
108
, §18.22]
,

a\phi_{m}(x+1)-(x+a)\phi_{m}(x)+x\phi_{m}(x-1)=-m\phi_{m}(x)
.

See also
[
56
,
130
]
.

The recurrence relations and difference equations to be seen here in §
3.5.6
, §
7.2.3
will
normally not be so beautiful!

1.3.
Hypergeometric expansions

The first rows of Table
1
, from
\Delta
to H, lead
to hypergeometric expansions as a method to solve difference equations. Later on, clever transformations
of such expansions showed the new difference operators
W
to
NSU

[
6
,
8
,
76
,
78
,
89
,
90
,
103
,
104
]
.
Completely new expansions are needed in the E case
[
117
,
119
,
121
,
123
,
124
,
126
]
.

Hypergeometric expansions
F(a,b;c;x)=\sum\dfrac{a(a+1)\dotsb(a+j-1)b(b+1)\dotsb(b+j-1)}{j!c(c+1)\dotsb(c+j-1)}x^{j}
(the name goes back to Wallis 1655
[
141
, chap. 14]
)

Gauss’s ratio of hypergeometric functions
[
109
, 1913, 1929 §59, 1957 §24]
;
[
136
, chap. XVIII §89]
.

f(x)=\dfrac{F(\alpha,\beta;\gamma;x}{F(\alpha,\beta+1;\gamma+1;x}=1+\dfrac{a_{1}x}{1+\dfrac{a_{2}x}{1+\ddots}},a_{2m}=-\dfrac{(\beta+m)(\gamma-\alpha+m)}{(\gamma+2m-1)(\gamma+2m)},a_{2m+1}=-\dfrac{(\alpha+m)(\gamma-\beta+m)}{(\gamma+2m)(\gamma+2m+1)}.

(2)

This ratio happens to be a solution to the
Riccati
equation

(1-x)xf^{\prime}(x)=[(\beta-\alpha)x+\gamma]f(x)-\gamma f^{2}(x)+\beta(\alpha/\gamma-1)x.

(3)

[
73
, chap. II, §10]

The present study is NOT based on hypergeometric expansions. Instead
it starts with a very elementary construction, and hypergeometric
expansions will be recovered at the very end.

1.4.
Orthogonality, Padé approximation, biorthogonality, and rational interpolation

Many special functions
\phi_{m}(x)
that will follow satisfy recurrence relations typical
of
orthogonal polynomials
, with or without the bispectrality property.

Relation to Padé
1
1

1

Henri Eugène Padé, 1863-1953,
[
21
, p. 249]
.
approximation:
if
\displaystyle\phi_{m}(t)=\sum_{0}^{m}\phi_{m}^{(j)}t^{j}
is orthogonal to all polynomials of degree
<m

with respect to a (formal) bilinear form
\langle f,g\rangle=\mathscr{L}(fg)
, one has

\mathscr{L}(t^{r}\phi_{m}(t))=\sum_{0}^{m}\phi_{m}^{(j)}\mathscr{L}(t^{j+r})=0
for
r=0,1,\dotsc,m-1
, showing that
the product of
\phi_{m}(x)
and
f(x)=\sum\mathscr{L}(t^{r})x^{-r-1}=\mathscr{L}((x-t)^{-1})
has a Laurent expansion
with vanishing coefficients of
x^{-1},\dotsc,x^{-m}
, the numerator polynomial is made of
the nonnegative powers

[
9
, chap. 7]

[
71
, §5.5]

Example of Hermite polynomials (actually
i^{-m}H_{m}(ix)
) in the error function
[
3
, §7.1.14, 15]
,

\displaystyle f(x)=2e^{x^{2}}\int_{x}^{\infty}e^{-t^{2}}dt=\dfrac{i}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\dfrac{e^{-t^{2}}\,dt}{ix-t}\sim\dfrac{1}{x}-\dfrac{1/2}{x^{3}}+\dfrac{3/4}{x^{5}}-\dfrac{15/8}{x^{7}}+\dfrac{105/16}{x^{9}}-\dotsb\\
=\frac{1}{x+\frac{1/2}{x+\frac{2/2}{x+\frac{3/2}{x+\ddots}}}}

[
38
, 13.2.20]
. Here,
\displaystyle\mathscr{L}(fg)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-t^{2}}f(t)g(t)\,dt
.

Other example, the exponential integral and Laguerre (actually
L_{m}(-x)
)
[
3
, §5]
,

\displaystyle e^{x}E_{1}(x)=e^{x}\int_{x}^{\infty}e^{-t}dt/t=\int_{0}^{\infty}\dfrac{e^{-t}dt}{x+t}

[
3
, §5.1.28]
,

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{x+\frac{1}{1+\frac{1}{x+\frac{2}{1+\frac{2}{x+\ddots}}}}}

[
3
, §5.1.22]
,
[
38
, 14.1.16]

\displaystyle\sim\sum_{0}^{\infty}\dfrac{(-1)^{m}m!}{x^{m+1}}

[
3
, 5.1.51]
. Here,
\mathscr{L}(fg)=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}f(t)g(t)dt
.

\dfrac{1}{x}

\dfrac{1}{x}

\dfrac{1}{x+1}

\dfrac{1}{x}-\dfrac{1}{x^{2}}+\dotsb

\dfrac{x}{x^{2}+1/2}

\dfrac{1}{x}-\dfrac{1/2}{x^{3}}+\dotsb

\dfrac{x+3}{x^{2}+4x+2}

\dfrac{1}{x}-\dotsb-\dfrac{6}{x^{4}}+\dotsb

\dfrac{x^{2}+1}{x^{3}+3x/2}

\dfrac{1}{x}-\dfrac{1/2}{x^{3}}+\dfrac{3/4}{x^{5}}-\dotsb

\dfrac{x^{2}+8x+11}{x^{3}+9x^{2}+18x+6}

\dotsb-\dfrac{120}{x^{6}}+\dotsb

\dfrac{x^{3}+5x/2}{x^{4}+3x^{2}+3/4}

\dfrac{1}{x}-\dfrac{1/2}{x^{3}}+\dfrac{3/4}{x^{5}}-\dfrac{15/8}{x^{7}}+\dotsb

\dfrac{x^{3}+15x^{2}+58x+50}{x^{4}+16x^{3}+72x^{2}+96x+24}

\dotsb-\dfrac{5040}{x^{8}}+\dotsb

Table 2.
Padé effect of Hermite and Laguerre polynomials. Denominator of degree
m
leads to matching
2m
power coefficients. The numerator
polynomials satisfy the same recurrence relations.

See
[
21
, §4.5]
for Padé history,
[
21
, §5.2.3]
for orthogonal polynomials , see also
[
24
]

for a wonderful summary of Padé and orthogonal polynomials activity during the 1970s and 1980s.

We will also encounter the following case of
biorthogonality
:

if the rational function
\phi_{m}(x)=\dfrac{P_{m}(x)}{(x-a_{0})\dotsb(x-a_{m})}
is orthogonal to
rational functions of the form
\dfrac{Q_{n}(x)}{(x-b_{0})\dotsb(x-b_{n})},n\neq m
, i.e. if
P_{m}

is orthogonal to any polynomial of lower degree with respect to
\displaystyle\mathscr{L}\left(\dfrac{f(t)g(t)}{(t-a_{0})\dotsb(t-a_{m})(t-b_{0})\dotsb(t-b_{m-1})}\right)
leading to

\displaystyle P_{m}(x)x^{r}f(x)=P_{m}(x)x^{r}\mathscr{L}\left(\dfrac{1}{x-t}\right)dt=\text{\ polynomial\ of\ degree\ }m+r<2m+\mathscr{L}\left(\dfrac{t^{r}P_{m}(t)}{x-t}\right)dt

and we perform the divided difference of order
2m
at
x=a_{0},\dotsc,a_{m},b_{0},\dotsc,b_{m-1}
giving

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}\left(\dfrac{t^{r}P_{m}(t)}{(t-a_{0})\dotsb(t-a_{m})(t-b_{0})\dotsb(t-b_{m-1})}\right)=0
from the biorthogonality conditions, meaning that there is a polynomial
N_{m}
interpolating
P_{m}(x)f(x)
at the given
2m+1
points, and that
N_{m}
has degree
\leqslant m

, as
x^{r}N_{m}(x)
has a vanishing divided difference of order
2m
.

Interpolation by
N_{m}/P_{m}
is called multipoint Pad é approximation in
[
47
,
85
,
86
]
,
[
71
, §5.5.2]

Example of Psi function
[
105
]

f(x)=\Psi(x)-\Psi(1)=0,1,3/2,11/6,25/12,
at 1, 2, 3…

f(x)=\dfrac{x-1}{1+\dfrac{(x-2)/3}{1+\dfrac{\ddots}{1+\dfrac{(m-1)(x-2m+1)/(9m^{2}-9m+3)}{1+\dfrac{m(x-2m)/(9m^{2}-9m+3)}{1+\ddots}}}}}
.

Successive rational interpolants are
\dfrac{3x-3}{x+1},\dfrac{9x^{2}+21x-30}{2x^{2}+18x+4},\dfrac{11x^{3}+162x^{2}+37x-210}{2x^{3}+60x^{2}+166x+12}
. The curious continued fraction above (see also
[
105
, §244]
)
will be explained in §
7.3.8
.

See E. Rains
[
113
]
for a very extensive highbrow relation to algebraic geometry.

2.
The lattices.

2.1.
Curves

Simplest difference equations relate two values of the unknown
function
f
, for
instance
f(x)
and
f(x+h)
, or the more
symmetric
f(x-h/2),f(x+h/2)
, or also
f(x),f(qx)
in
q-
difference
equations
[
50
,
76
,
78
]
.

Some of these difference operators allow to formulate, and solve, curious difference equations
such as:
find
f
such that
f(x+\sqrt{x})-f(x-\sqrt{x})=1
, starting from

f(0)=0
.
Answer:
f(n^{2}+n)=n,n=0,1,\dotsc
, see the last exercise of §
2.2.2
. Or also:
find
f
such that

f(x+x/2)-f(x-x/2)=1,withf(1)=1
.Answer:
f(3^{n})=n+1,n=0,1,\dotsc

Solution of difference equations asks for values of the solution on particular
lattices, or grids, of various kinds given in §
2.3
.

From W onward, arguments of
f
are given in parametric form, suggesting
a
CURVE. A Cartesian form of the W case is
y=t^{2}\pm it-1/4\Rightarrow(y-x+1/4)^{2}+x=0
, a parabola.

Figure 1.
Examples of values involved in the solution of a
difference equation. Why are the axes drawn without variables names?

We show that the difference operators seen up to now
involve
f
at the two
roots
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
of a quadratic polynomial in
y
. Indeed, we
already saw
(y-x+1/4)^{2}+x=0
for the parabola of the third
example of Fig.
1
. The two easier examples are

(y-x-h/2)(y-x+h/2)=0
and
(y-x)(y-qx-\omega)=0
and do again enter
the quadratic scheme. For the more difficult AW case,

y_{1},y_{2}=\cos\theta\cos\lambda\pm\sin\theta\sin\lambda\Rightarrow(y-x\cos\lambda)^{2}+(x^{2}-1)\sin^{2}\lambda=0
, an ellipse if
\lambda
is real,
a hyperbola if
\lambda
is pure imaginary, showing how
conics can be discovered from other
rows of table
1
.

For NSU, where is the curve?
Actually, the NSU formula gives immediately the relevant lattice,
or grid,
x(s),x(s+1),x(s+2),\dotsc
The next sections follow
with more on lattices and recurrence relations.

2.2.
Difference equations and lattices

A first order difference equation relates the
values of the unknown function
f
at two ordinates on the given curve, say

y_{1}
and
y_{2}
. If
f(y_{1})
is given (Cauchy problem), the
equation gives
f(y_{2})
, and we search another point on the curve of ordinate

y_{2}
, the difference equation gives then
f
at a new point, etc. The solution
of the equation is then described by the values at a sequence of values

y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\dotsc
This rather ridiculous choice of the name
y_{k}

for the fundamental lattice, or grid, of arguments of the function
f

(used by the author in
[
92
,
93
]
)
is avoided by (re)turning to the
x-
axis containing the relevant

x_{0},x_{1},\dotsc
values and the
y-
axis contains now intermediate data
[
126
, eq. (1.2)]
. One could consider that the pictures
of fig.
1
have a horizontal axis
y
and a vertical
axis
x
.

2.2.1.
The biquadratic polynomial.

So, let
(x_{n},y_{n})
be a current point of a relevant sequence on the curve

F(x,y)=0
. Then,

x_{n+1}
is the second
x-
root of
F(x,y_{n})=0
(Fig.
2
).
To be sure that the equation
F(x,y)=0
has always exactly two roots in
x
for
a given
y
, we choose
F
to be a quadratic polynomial in
x
:

\hskip 65.0pt\ \ \ \ \ F(x,y)=Y_{0}(y)+Y_{1}(y)x+Y_{2}(y)x^{2}=0,

(4a)

where
Y_{0},Y_{1}
, and
Y_{2}
are given functions.

But difference equations must allow the recovery of
f
on a whole set
of points! An initial-value problem for a first order difference equation
starts with a value for
f(x)
at some
x=x_{0}
. We then get
y_{0}
as a
y-
root of
F(x_{0},y)=0
. The difference equation relates then

f(x_{0})
to
f(x_{1})
, where
x_{1}
is the second root of (
4a
) at
y=y_{0}
, found by (
6
) as
x_{1}=-Y_{1}(y_{0})/Y_{2}(y_{0})-x_{0}
.
We now need
y_{1}
,
the
y-
root of
F(x_{1},y)=0
which is not
y_{0}
. Here again, the
simplest case is when
F
is of degree 2 in
y
:

F(x,y)=X_{0}(x)+X_{1}(x)y+X_{2}(x)y^{2}=0.

(4b)

Both forms (
4a
) and (
4b
) hold simultaneously
when
F
is
biquadratic
:

F(x,y)=\sum_{i=0}^{2}\sum_{j=0}^{2}c_{i,j}x^{i}y^{j}.

(5)

Remark that the sum and the product of the two roots are
the quadratic rational functions

\begin{split}x_{n}+x_{n+1}&=-Y_{1}(y_{n})/Y_{2}(y_{n}),\ \ \ x_{n}x_{n+1}=Y_{0}(y_{n})/Y_{2}(y_{n}),\\
y_{n-1}+y_{n}&=-X_{1}(x_{n})/X_{2}(x_{n}),\ \ \ y_{n-1}y_{n}=X_{0}(x_{n})/X_{2}(x_{n}).\end{split}

(6)

We also have the two factorizations of
F

At some
x=x_{n}
, the two
y-
roots of
F(x_{n},y)=0
are
y_{n-1}
and

y_{n}
,

so
F(x_{n},y)=X_{2}(x_{n})(y-y_{n-1})(y-y_{n})
; also,

F(x,y_{n})=Y_{2}(y_{n})(x-x_{n})(x-x_{n+1})
.

\begin{split}F(x_{m},y_{n})&=X_{2}(x_{m})y_{n}^{2}+X_{1}(x_{m})y_{n}+X_{0}(x_{m})=X_{2}(x_{m})(y_{n}-y_{m-1})(y_{n}-y_{m})\\
&=Y_{2}(y_{n})x_{m}^{2}+=Y_{1}(y_{n})x_{m}+Y_{0}(y_{n})=Y_{2}(y_{n})(x_{m}-x_{n})(x_{m}-x_{n+1}).\end{split}

(7)

Il eût été si facile … de faire précéder et suivre chaque proposition
d’un cortège redoutable d’exemples particuliers!

(It would have been so easy … to precede and to follow each statement by a
formidable parade of particular examples!)

É. Galois, who hated losing time on examples and exercises.

2.2.2.
Exercises.

Show that
\dfrac{x_{n+1}-x_{n-1}}{y_{n}-y_{n-1}}

is a rational function of
x_{n}
. Hint: use
x_{n+1}-x_{n-1}=x_{n}+x_{n+1}-(x_{n-1}+x_{n})
and use (
6
). This curious ratio will be used in §
3.3.5
and
6.1
.

Remove the indetermination at
n=0
of
\dfrac{y_{n}-y_{0}}{x_{n}-x_{0}}
. Answer: multiply
the two terms of the fraction by
x_{n+1}-x_{0}
. The denominator is now

(x_{n}-x_{0})(x_{n+1}-x_{0})=F(x_{0},y_{n})/Y_{2}(y_{n})=X_{2}(x_{0})(y_{n}-y_{0})(y_{n}-y_{-1})/Y_{2}(y_{n})

from (
7
), and the result is
\dfrac{X_{2}(x_{0})(x_{n+1}-x_{0})}{Y_{2}(y_{n})(y_{n}-y_{-1})}
. Note that the indetermination is now at
n=-1
…

How can we relate this to the difference operator
f(x+\sqrt{x})-f(x-\sqrt{x})
?

x
is a parameter not to be confused with the
x
of
F(x,y)
, so, there is
a parameter
s_{n}
such that
x_{n}=s_{n}-\sqrt{s_{n}}
, and
x_{n+1}=s_{n}+\sqrt{s_{n}}
,

x_{n}+x_{n+1}=2s_{n}
and
x_{n}x_{n+1}=s_{n}^{2}-s_{n}
. By (
6
),

Y_{0}(y_{n})/Y_{2}(y_{n})=Y_{1}^{2}(y_{n})/(4Y_{2}^{2}(y_{n}))+Y_{1}(y_{n})/(2Y_{2}(y_{n}))
. Let
Y_{2}(y)\equiv 1
,
and the degrees of
Y_{0}
and
Y_{1}
be 2 and 1, with
Y_{1}(y)=2\alpha y+2\beta
, we have

Y_{0}(y)=(\alpha y+\beta)^{2}+\alpha y+\beta
,
F(x,y)=(\alpha y+\beta)^{2}+\alpha y+\beta+2(\alpha y+\beta)x+x^{2}=(x+\alpha y+\beta+1/2)^{2}-x-1/4=0
at
x=x_{n}
when

-s_{n}=\alpha y_{n}+\beta=-\sqrt{x_{n}+1/4}-x_{n}-1/2=-(1/2+\sqrt{x_{n}+1/4})^{2}
,

x_{n+1}=2s_{n}-x_{n}=x_{n}+1+2\sqrt{x_{n}+1/4},\sqrt{x_{n+1}+1/4}=1+\sqrt{x_{n}+1/4}
,

the sequence is
x_{n}=(n+c)^{2}-1/4
,
c=\sqrt{x_{0}+1/4}
, the Wilson (W) lattice, see below.

2.3.
Lattices formulas

All the examples already seen enter the (
5
) form.

Besides
f(x_{n+1})-f(x_{n})=0
, the most ridiculously simple difference
equation is
f(x_{n+1})-f(x_{n})=1
, of course solved by
f(x_{n})=f(x_{0})+n
.
The interesting feature here is not given by the values of
f
, but by the
points
x_{n}
where
f
can be computed. Examples follow.

Most people do not use this
(x,y)
system, but a particular parametric representation
such that
x_{n}=x(s_{0}+n)
. The simplest example is
x(s)=sh
.
The formulas for the various curves
F(x,y)=0
follow now.

(1)

Two parallel lines, first entries of table
1
,

F(x,y)=(y-ax-b)(y-ax-c)
, we start with
x_{0}
and

y_{0}=ax_{0}+b
, then,
x_{1}
is such that
ax_{1}+c=y_{0}=ax_{0}+b
so
x_{n}=x_{0}+nh,y_{n}=y_{0}+nah
follow, where
h=(b-c)/a
.

(2)

Two lines
F(x,y)=(y-ax-b)(y-cx-d)
,
a\neq c
, then

F(x,y_{n})=0
is solved by
x_{n}=(y_{n}-b)/a
and
x_{n+1}=(y_{n}-d)/c
,
so,
cx_{n+1}-ax_{n}=b-d
, or
x_{n+1}-x^{*}=q(x_{n}-x^{*})
with
q=a/c

and
x^{*}=(b-d)/(c-a)
,
whence
x_{n}=x^{*}+q^{n}(x_{0}-x^{*}),y_{n}=y^{*}+q^{n}(y_{0}-y^{*})
,
where
y^{*}=ax^{*}+b=(bc-ad)/(c-a)
, a
geometric
sequence, rows G and H of table
1
, whence the name.

Note that
q
is the ratio of the slopes
of the two lines.

When
a
and
c
are very close,
a=c(1+\varepsilon),q=1+\varepsilon,x^{*}=(d-b)/(c\varepsilon),x_{n}=(1+\varepsilon)^{n}x_{0}+((d-b)/c)[1-(1+\varepsilon)^{n}]/\varepsilon\to x_{0}-n(d-b)/c
when
\varepsilon\to 0
.

(3)

Parabola
2
2

2

Parametric description of a parabola used in mathematical
typography, the
TrueType
system
[
41
,
129
]
.
, line W of table
1
,

x_{n}=as_{n}^{2}+bs_{n}+c,y_{n}=ds_{n}^{2}+es_{n}+f
,

or

F(x,y)=x-a\left(\dfrac{ay-dx-\gamma}{ae-bd}\right)^{2}-b\dfrac{ay-dx-\gamma}{e-bd}-c
, where

\gamma=af-cd
. The two
s-
roots of
y=y_{n}
are
s_{n}
and
-e/d-s_{n}
; the two
s-
roots
of
x=x_{n+1}
are
-e/d-s_{n}
and
s_{n+1}=-b/a-(-e/d-s_{n})
, so

s_{n}=s_{0}+n(ae-bd)/(ad)
, and
x_{n}
and
y_{n}
are quadratic polynomials in
n
, that is
the second NSU-line of table
1
.

(4)

General centered conic
x_{n}=a\cos(\theta_{n}-\theta^{*})+b,y_{n}=c\cos\theta_{n}+d
,

or
F(x,y)=\left(\dfrac{x-b}{a\sin\theta^{*}}-\dfrac{y-d}{c\tan\theta^{*}}\right)^{2}+\left(\dfrac{y-d}{c}\right)^{2}-1
,
where the
\theta
s need not be real if the
x
and
y
s are real.
Then
x_{n+1}
is the second
x-
root of
F(x,y_{n})=0
, so,

x_{n+1}=b+a\sin\theta^{*}(\cos\theta_{n}/\tan\theta^{*}-\sin\theta_{n})
, and

\theta_{n+1}=\theta_{n}+2\theta^{*}
follows, or

y_{n}=d+c\cos(\theta_{0}+2n\theta^{*})=d+c(e^{i\theta_{0}}q^{n}+e^{-i\theta_{0}}q^{-n})/2,x_{n}=b+a\cos(\theta_{0}+(2n-1)\theta^{*})=b+a(e^{i\theta_{0}}q^{n-1/2}+e^{-i\theta_{0}}q^{1/2-n})/2
,
with
q=\exp(2i\theta^{*})
,
basically the NSU formulas of table
1
.

Note that
q
is now the ratio of the slopes
of the two asymptotes
[
89
, p. 264]

[
90
, p. 255]
.

(5)

The general biquadratic polynomial will of course be the subject matter
of all the following sections.

Figure 3.
An instance of a biquadratic curve
F(x,y)=X_{2}(x)y^{2}+X_{1}(x)y+X_{0}(x)=0
, with

P(x)=42.26667(x+2)(x^{2}-1)(x-1.5),X_{2}(x)=(x+5.5)(x-4)
,
and where
X_{1}(x)=-0.7333x^{2}+27.49x-0.6882
interpolates
\sqrt{P}
at the zeros of
X_{2}
(vertical
asymptotes).

near
(x^{\prime}_{1},y^{\prime}_{0})
,
0=F(x^{\prime}_{1}+dx,y^{\prime}_{0}+dy)=dx\partial F/\partial x+dy\partial F/\partial y+o(dy)
,

dy/dx=-(2x^{\prime}_{1}Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{0})+Y_{1}(y^{\prime}_{0}))/(2y^{\prime}_{0}X_{2}(x^{\prime}_{1})+X_{1}(x^{\prime}_{1}))=-(x^{\prime}_{1}-x^{\prime}_{0})Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{0})/((y^{\prime}_{0}-y^{\prime}_{1})X_{2}(x^{\prime}_{1}))

(8)

3.
Definition of elliptic lattice, or grid.

3.1.
Definition 1.

A sequence
\{\dotsc,x_{-1},x_{0},x_{1},\dotsc\}
,
is an elliptic lattice if there exists
a biquadratic polynomial (
5
)
such that
F(x_{n},y)=0
and
F(x_{n+1},y)=0
have a common root in
y
.

A very awkward definition! I am probably the only one person using it.

3.1.1.
Exercise.

Are
\{an+b\},\{n^{2}\}
special
cases of elliptic sequences? And
\{\sqrt{n}\}
? Impossible to answer with
the present definition, see §
3.3.4
.

3.1.2.
Direct formulas.

We have

y_{n}\ \text{and\ }y_{n-1}=\dfrac{-X_{1}(x_{n})\pm\sqrt{P(x_{n})}}{2X_{2}(x_{n})},\ \ x_{n}\ \text{and\ }x_{n+1}=\dfrac{-Y_{1}(y_{n})\pm\sqrt{Q(y_{n})}}{2Y_{2}(y_{n})},

(9)

where

P=X_{1}^{2}-4X_{0}X_{2},\ \ Q=Y_{1}^{2}-4Y_{0}Y_{2}

(10)

are polynomials of degree
\leqslant 4
.

Of course, the sequence
\{y_{n}\}
is elliptic too, consider
G(x,y)=F(y,x)
.
The construction above is called “T-algorithm” in
[
126
, Theorem 6]
.

Given
x_{0}
, we choose
y_{0}
as one of the two
y-
roots of
F(x_{0},y)=0
,
and
y_{1},x_{1},y_{2}
etc. follow without having to solve any new quadratic
equation, by using (
6
) repeatedly.

If we choose the other root at
x_{0}
, say,
y^{\prime}_{0}=y_{1}
, then
y^{\prime}_{1}=y_{0},y^{\prime}_{2}=y_{-1}
etc.

Special cases.
We already encountered the usual difference operators
involving
(x,x+h)
or
(x-h,x)
or
(x-h/2,x+h/2)
, which must
now be written
(y+\alpha h,y+\beta h)
with various
(\alpha,\beta)
,
corresponding to
F(x,y)=(x-y-\alpha h)(x-y-\beta h)
, so,

X_{2}(x)\equiv 1
,
X_{1}
of degree 1,
X_{0}
of degree 2 with
P=X_{1}^{2}-4X_{0}X_{2}
of degree 0.
For the geometric difference operator,
P
is the square of a first degree polynomial.
For the Askey-Wilson operator
[
6
,
8
,
65
,
76
,
89
,
90
]
,
P
is an arbitrary second degree
polynomial (
=
a fourth degree polynomial with a double zero at
\infty
).

3.1.3.
A glance at the numerical
F(x,y)
laboratory

.

Choosing the
c_{i,j}
s at random will not yield a readable result.
The most important feature is
P(x)=\epsilon(x-z_{1})(x-z_{2})(x-z_{3})(x-z_{4})

whose square root will be needed in many expressions.
Why this symbol
\epsilon
? This coefficient controls the
step
,
the
x_{n}
s are close together if
\epsilon
is small, as the square root is
small in (
9
), see fig.
4
.

Figure 4.
Influence of
\epsilon
on the step.

How to
reconstruct
the
X
s in (
10
):
P=X_{1}^{2}-4X_{0}X_{2}
? We could give
X_{1}

and recover
X_{0}
and
X_{2}
by factoring the quartic
X_{1}^{2}-P
.

The preferred choice is to give
X_{2}(x)=c_{2,2}(x-u)(x-v)
(
u
and
v

are the vertical asymptotes in fig.
3
), then
X_{1}

interpolates some determinations of the square root of
P
at
u

and
v
, and
X_{0}
is the (exact) quotient of the division of

X_{1}^{2}-P
by
4X_{2}
.

See here a test with the gp-pari software
[
35
]
:

GP/PARI CALCULATOR

comes WITHOUT ANY WARRANTY WHATSOEVER.

nd=15;yv=vector(nd+5);xv=vector(nd+5);fv=vector(nd+1);fv1=vector(nd+1);

\\ F(x,y)=X2(x)y2+X1(x)y+X0(x) = Y2(y)x2+Y1(y)x+Y0(y) P=(X1)^2-4X0X2
epsi=42.26667;zerP=[-2,-1,1,1.5];
P=epsi*prod(k=1,4,x-zerP[k]);

\\ given X2 u,v = vertical asymptotes

3.2.
Essential and secondary parameters: modulus and step.

F(x,y)=\sum_{0}^{2}\sum_{0}^{2}c_{i,j}x^{i}y^{j}
has 9 parameters in homogeneous
form, leaving 8 degrees of freedom.

The first degree rational transformations

\xi=\dfrac{\alpha x+\beta}{1+\gamma x},\ \ \ \eta=\dfrac{\alpha^{\prime}y+\beta^{\prime}}{1+\gamma^{\prime}y}

(11)

still lead to biquadratic polynomials:

\mathcal{F}(\xi,\eta)=(1+\gamma^{\prime}y)^{-2}(1+\gamma x)^{-2}\sum_{0}^{2}\sum_{0}^{2}\gamma_{i,j}(\alpha^{\prime}y+\beta^{\prime})^{i}(1+\gamma^{\prime}y)^{2-i}(\alpha x+\beta)^{j}(1+\gamma x)^{2-j}

=(1+\gamma^{\prime}y)^{-2}(1+\gamma x)^{-2}F(t,x)
.

As each rational function carry 3 coefficients, there remains two
parameters invariant with respect to the transformations of (
11
),
they are called the
essential
parameters.

The cross ratio of any set of 4
x-
points, say,
z_{1},\dotsc,z_{4}

corresponding to
\zeta_{1},\dotsc,\zeta_{4}
has this invariance
property:

\lambda=\dfrac{(\zeta_{3}-\zeta_{1})/(\zeta_{3}-\zeta_{2})}{(\zeta_{4}-\zeta_{1})/(\zeta_{4}-\zeta_{2})}=\dfrac{(z_{3}-z_{1})/(z_{3}-z_{2})}{(z_{4}-z_{1})/(z_{4}-z_{2})}
.

We choose the four zeros of
P
sent to the symmetric Jacobi
configuration
\{\zeta_{1},\dotsc,\zeta_{4}\}=\{-1/k,-1,1,1/k\}
, then

\lambda=\dfrac{(1+1/k)/2}{(2/k)/(1/k+1)}=\dfrac{(1+k)^{2}}{4k}

defining the
modulus

k=\left(\sqrt{\lambda}-\sqrt{\lambda-1}\right)^{2}
from the cross-ratio
of the 4 zeros of
P
. Permutations of the 4 zeros lead
to 6 possible values of the cross-ratio

\lambda,1/\lambda,1-\lambda,1/(1-\lambda),1-1/\lambda,1-1/(1-\lambda)=\lambda/(\lambda-1)
, the anharmonic group
( Maillard
[
87
, p.300]
).
If
\lambda=\cos^{2}\theta
, the other values are
\sec^{2}\theta,\sin^{2}\theta,\csc^{2}\theta,-\tan^{2}\theta,-\cot^{2}\theta
.

When
z_{1},\dotsc,z_{4}
are real numbers in ascending order,

\lambda=\dfrac{(z_{3}-z_{1})/(z_{3}-z_{2})}{(z_{4}-z_{1})/(z_{4}-z_{2})}=\dfrac{1+(z_{2}-z_{1})/(z_{3}-z_{2})}{1+(z_{2}-z_{1})/(z_{4}-z_{2})}>1

and
0<k<1
.

With the numerical example of §,
[z1,\dotsc,z4]=[-2,-1,1,1.5]
, one finds

\lambda=15/14=1.0714,k=0.58957;\xi=\dfrac{x+\gamma}{1+\gamma x}
with

\gamma=(z_{j}-\xi_{j})/(z_{j}\xi_{j}-1)=-0.12702
.

\zeta_{j}=\dfrac{\alpha z_{j}+\beta}{1+\gamma z_{j}}\Rightarrow\zeta_{j}-\zeta_{m}=\dfrac{\beta-\alpha/\gamma}{1+\gamma z_{j}}-\dfrac{\beta-\alpha/\gamma}{1+\gamma z_{m}}=\dfrac{(\alpha-\beta\gamma)(z_{j}-z_{m})}{(1+\gamma z_{j})(1+\gamma z_{m})}

(12)

The other essential parameter is the
step
,

x_{n+1}-x_{n}
in the uniform case, but should be in general related
to the width of the figure (fig.
4
). The ratio

(x_{n+1}-x_{n})/(z_{2}-z_{1})
is independent of dilations of the
x_{n}
s.
The nonuniformity is almost canceled when one considers that the
computation of
x_{n+1}
from
x_{n}
involves the square root of

P(x_{n})=\epsilon\prod_{1}^{4}(x_{n}-z_{k})
(from (
6
)-(
9
)), and we
may consider
(x_{n+1}-x_{n})/\sqrt{P(x_{n})}
.

What works is the definition of the step

h=\dfrac{1}{1-k}\int_{x_{n}}^{x_{n+1}}\dfrac{\sqrt{(z_{1}-z_{2})(z_{3}-z_{4})}\;dx}{\sqrt{(x-z_{1})(x-z_{2})(x-z_{3})(x-z_{4})}},

(13)

on any set
x_{n},x_{n+1}
of the
x-
lattice. More on the numbering
z_{1},z_{2}
, etc. is
given at the end of the present subsection.

We show that the definition (
13
) is invariant under transformations (
11
).

\xi-\zeta_{j}=\dfrac{\alpha x+\beta}{1+\gamma x}-\dfrac{\alpha z_{j}+\beta}{1+\gamma z_{j}}=(x-z_{j})\dfrac{\alpha-\beta\gamma}{(1+\gamma x)(1+\gamma z_{j})}
,

(\xi-\zeta_{1})\dotsb(\xi-\zeta_{4})=\dfrac{(x-z_{1})\dotsb(x-z_{4})(\alpha-\beta\gamma)^{4}}{(1+\gamma x)^{4}(1+\gamma z_{1})\dotsb(1+\gamma z_{4})}

and, with

\dfrac{dx}{d\xi}=-\dfrac{(1+\gamma x)^{2}}{\beta\gamma-\alpha}
, we have

\dfrac{d\xi}{\sqrt{(\xi-\zeta_{1})\dotsb(\xi-\zeta_{4})}}=C\dfrac{dx}{\sqrt{(x-z_{1})\dotsb(x-z_{4})}}
, with

C=-\dfrac{\beta\gamma-\alpha}{1+\gamma x)^{2}}\sqrt{\dfrac{(1+\gamma x)^{4}(1+\gamma z_{1})\dotsb(1+\gamma z_{4})}{(\alpha-\beta\gamma)^{4}}}=\sqrt{\dfrac{(z_{1}-z_{2})(z_{3}-z_{4})}{(\zeta_{1}-\zeta_{2})(\zeta_{3}-\zeta_{4})}}
,
using (
12
).

This shows that (
13
) is independent of rational transformations. The independence
with respect to
n
is of course much more important and is the main subject of §
3.6
.

With a rational map (
11
) sending
z_{1},\dotsc,z_{4}
to

\zeta_{1},\dotsc,\zeta_{4}=-1/k,-1,1,1/k,
(
13
) becomes

h=\int_{\xi_{n-1}}^{\xi_{n}}\dfrac{d\xi}{\sqrt{(1-\xi^{2})(1-k^{2}\xi^{2})}},

(14)

indeed,
(\zeta_{1}-\zeta_{2})(\zeta_{3}-\zeta_{4})=(1/k-1)^{2}
, and

(\xi^{2}-1)(\xi^{2}-1/k^{2})=(1-\xi^{2})(1-k^{2}\xi^{2})k^{2}
.

With the numerical example of §, a crude check of (
13
)
with
\sqrt{(z_{1}-z_{2})(z_{3}-z_{4})}/(1-k)=\sqrt{(-2+1)(1-1.5)}/0.4104=1.7229
,
by

h\approx 1.7229(x_{n+1}-x_{n})/\sqrt{(x-z_{1})\dotsb(x-z_{4})}
at
x=(x_{n}+x_{n+1})/2
:

n 3 4 5 6 7 8 9 10
xn -0.8607 -0.1752 0.5380 0.9309 0.9843 0.7197 0.0942 -0.6257
h 0.720 0.737 0.689 0.252? -0.641?? -0.727 -0.735
Simpson 0.771 0.766 0.785 0.271? -0.850 -0.768 -0.766

Now we proceed to an accurate computation of
h=\int_{\xi_{0}}^{\xi_{1}}dx/\sqrt{(1-\xi^{2})(1-k^{2}\xi^{2})}\\
=\int_{\varphi_{0}}^{\varphi_{1}}d\varphi/\sqrt{1-k^{2}\sin^{2}\varphi}=F(\varphi_{1},k)-F(\varphi_{0},k)
, where
\xi=\sin\varphi
.

Landen:
F(\varphi,k)=(\lim\varphi^{(n)}/2^{n})(1+k_{1})(1+k_{2})\dotsb

where
k_{0}=k,k_{n+1}=\dfrac{1-\sqrt{1-k_{n}^{2}}}{1+\sqrt{1-k_{n}^{2}}},\varphi^{(n+1)}=\varphi^{(n)}+\arctan(\sqrt{1-k_{n}^{2}}\,\tan\varphi^{(n)})

[
3
, 15.7]

Here,
k=0.5896,k_{1}=0.1064,k_{2}=0.0028,k_{3}=2.02\;10^{-6}
.
With
\xi_{0}=(\alpha x_{0}+\beta)/(1+\gamma x_{0})=-0.12702=\sin(-0.12736),\varphi_{0}^{(1)}/2=-0.11521,\varphi_{0}^{(2)}/4=-0.11490
, etc. Elliptic integrals
from
\varphi_{0}
to
\varphi_{1}
and from
\varphi_{1}
to
\varphi_{2}
yield the
same value of
h=-0.76411
. We have
\xi_{n}={\text{sn}}(nh+g)
, where
g=
arcsn
(\xi_{0})=F(\varphi_{0},k)=-0.11490(1+k_{1})(1+k_{2})\dotsb=-0.12748

We also have
nh=\int_{\xi_{0}}^{\xi_{n}}\dfrac{d\xi}{\sqrt{(1-\xi^{2})(1-k^{2}\xi^{2})}}
,
giving an explicit relation between
n
and
x_{n}
,
nh
being an

elliptic integral
of
x_{n}
, which is its inversion, i.e., an
elliptic
function
of
n
:
\xi_{n}={\text{sn}}(nh+g)
. Much more in §
3.6
.

The simplest
F
for given
k
and
h
will be given in (
24
).

R. Baxter uses
the Jacobi canonical setting in the form
t_{n}=k^{1/2}{\text{sn}}(nh+g)

[
12
, § 15.10]
,so,
nh=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{n}}\dfrac{dt}{\sqrt{(k-t^{2})(1-kt^{2})}}.

A third essential parameter depending on the two first ones is the
period
.
The
n-
period is the (interpolated) index where
(x_{n},y_{n})
reproduce
(x_{0},y_{0})
.
On our example, could it be between 4 and 5?

.

Simple limit cases are the obvious

\to\infty
, so that we have

,

x^{2}
,and

gives

(the log in some basis).

) gives

3.3.
Definition 2. Symmetry.

An elliptic lattice, or grid, is a sequence
satisfying a symmetric biquadratic relation

[
126
, Theorem 5]

E(x_{n},x_{n+1})=\sum_{0}^{2}\sum_{0}^{2}e_{i,j}x^{i}_{n}x^{j}_{n+1}=0,\ \ e_{i,j}=e_{j,i}.

(15)

As
E
does not depend on
n
, we also have
E(x_{n-1},x_{n})=0
, so,

E(x_{n},x_{n-1})=0
by the symmetry of
E
.

The two
z-
roots of
E(x_{n},z)=0
are therefore
x_{n\pm 1}

One has

x_{n-1}+x_{n+1}=-\dfrac{e_{1,2}x_{n}^{2}+e_{1,1}x_{n}+e_{0,1}}{e_{2,2}x_{n}^{2}+e_{1,2}x_{n}+e_{0,2}},\ \ \ x_{n-1}x_{n+1}=\dfrac{e_{0,2}x_{n}^{2}+e_{0,1}x_{n}+e_{0,0}}{e_{2,2}x_{n}^{2}+e_{1,2}x_{n}+e_{0,2}}.

(16)

One also has
E(x,z)=e_{0,0}+e_{0,1}(x+z)+e_{0,2}(x^{2}+z^{2})+e_{1,1}xz+e_{1,2}xz(x+z)+e_{2,2}x^{2}z^{2}

=e_{0,0}+e_{0,1}\Sigma+e_{0,2}\Sigma^{2}+(e_{1,1}-2e_{0,2})\Pi+e_{1,2}\Sigma\Pi+e_{2,2}\Pi^{2}
.

where
\Sigma=x+z
and
\Pi=xz
.

3.3.1.
1\Rightarrow 2
.

Let
\{x_{n},y_{n}\}
be sequences satisfying the definition 1.
A relation involving only
x_{n}
and
x_{n+1}
is obtained by
the elimination of
y_{n}
through the resultant
[
17
, § 8]
,

[
46
]
,
(bigradient in Aitken
[
4
]
) of the two
polynomials in
y_{n}
from (
6
)

P_{1}(y_{n})=(x_{n}+x_{n+1})Y_{2}(y_{n})+Y_{1}(y_{n})

and

P_{2}(y_{n})=x_{n}x_{n+1}Y_{2}(y_{n})-Y_{0}(y_{n})
.

\begin{vmatrix}\Sigma c_{2,0}+c_{1,0}&\Sigma c_{2,1}+c_{1,1}&\Sigma c_{2,2}+c_{1,2}&0\\
0&\Sigma c_{2,0}+c_{1,0}&\Sigma c_{2,1}+c_{1,1}&\Sigma c_{2,2}+c_{1,2}\\
\Pi c_{2,0}-c_{0,0}&\Pi c_{2,1}-c_{0,1}&\Pi c_{2,2}-c_{0,0}&0\\
0&\Pi c_{2,0}-c_{0,0}&\Pi c_{2,1}-c_{0,1}&\Pi c_{2,2}-c_{0,0}\end{vmatrix}=0,

were
\Sigma
and
\Pi
are here
x_{n}+x_{n+1}
and
x_{n}x_{n+1}
.
It is not obvious that
the result is biquadratic in
x_{n}
and
x_{n+1}
,
that the expression is quadratic in
\Sigma
and
\Pi
.
The coefficient of
\Sigma^{2}\Pi^{2}
is

\begin{vmatrix}c_{2,0}&c_{2,1}&c_{2,2}&0\\
0&c_{2,0}&c_{2,1}&c_{2,2}\\
c_{2,0}&c_{2,1}&c_{2,2}&0\\
0&c_{2,0}&c_{2,1}&c_{2,2}\end{vmatrix}=0
, and
the
\Sigma^{2}\Pi
and
\Sigma\Pi^{2}
terms are still determinants with
at least one pair of identical rows.

The final quadratic formula in
\Sigma,\Pi
is

\mathcal{R}(\Sigma,\Pi):=e_{0,0}+e_{0,1}\Sigma+e_{0,2}\Sigma^{2}+(e_{1,1}-2e_{0,2})\Pi+e_{1,2}\Sigma\Pi+e_{2,2}\Pi^{2}=0,

(17)

which is
E(x,z)=0
where
\Sigma=x+z
and
\Pi=xz
, as above,
(
17
) is the equation of a
conic
3
3

3

Called the
\mathcal{R}-
conic.
The importance of this conic
has been stressed by A.Ronveaux
[
115
]
.
.

3.3.2.
2\Rightarrow 1
.

We try to arrive at
\Sigma=-Y_{1}(y)/Y_{2}(y),\Pi=Y_{0}(y)/Y_{2}(y)
(we drop the index
n
) considered
as a parametric representation of the curve (
17
) with rational
functions (
unicursal
curve)
[
33
]
of degree
\leqslant 2
, which therefore holds for a conic, as
well known (
[
25
,
26
,
27
]
,
[
40
, § 58]
).

Let
(\Sigma^{*},\Pi^{*})
be a particular point of the conic
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma,\Pi)=0
, and solve
\Pi=\Pi^{*}+t(\Sigma-\Sigma^{*})
for
t
:

0=\mathcal{R}(\Sigma,\Pi)-\mathcal{R}(\Sigma^{*},\Pi^{*})

=e_{0,1}(\Sigma-\Sigma^{*})+e_{0,2}(\Sigma^{2}-(\Sigma^{*})^{2})+(e_{1,1}-2e_{0,2})(\Pi-\Pi^{*})+e_{1,2}(\Sigma\Pi-\Sigma^{*}\Pi^{*})+e_{2,2}(\Pi^{2}-(\Pi^{*})^{2}),

=(\Sigma-\Sigma^{*})\{e_{0,1}+e_{0,2}(\Sigma+\Sigma^{*})+(e_{1,1}-2e_{0,2})t+e_{1,2}\Pi^{*}+e_{1,2}t\Sigma+e_{2,2}t[2Pi^{*}+t(\Sigma-\Sigma^{*})]\}
, so

\Sigma=-\dfrac{e_{0,1}+e_{0,2}\Sigma^{*}+(e_{1,1}-2e_{0,2})t+e_{1,2}\Pi^{*}+e_{2,2}t[2\Pi^{*}-t\Sigma^{*}]}{e_{0,2}+e_{1,2}t+e_{2,2}t^{2}}\\
=\Sigma^{*}-\dfrac{e_{0,1}+2e_{0,2}\Sigma^{*}+(e_{1,1}-2e_{0,2})t+e_{1,2}\Pi^{*}+e_{1,2}t\Sigma^{*}+2e_{2,2}t\Pi^{*}}{e_{0,2}+e_{1,2}t+e_{2,2}t^{2}}
,

\Pi=\Pi^{*}-t\dfrac{e_{0,1}+2e_{0,2}\Sigma^{*}+(e_{1,1}-2e_{0,2})t+e_{1,2}\Pi^{*}+e_{1,2}t\Sigma^{*}+2e_{2,2}t\Pi^{*}}{e_{0,2}+e_{1,2}t+e_{2,2}t^{2}}

give a parametric representation
-Y_{1}(y)/Y_{2}(y),Y0(y)/Y_{2}(y)
with
y=t
, so build a valid

F(x,y)=x^{2}Y_{2}(y)+xY_{1}(y)+Y_{0}(y)
.

End of
2\Rightarrow 1
. ∎

3.3.3.
Direct formula.

We have
x_{n+1}
in terms of
x_{n}

by (
6
)
x_{n+1}=-x_{n}-Y_{1}(y_{n})/Y_{2}(y_{n})
,
where

y_{n}
is replaced by (
9
). This yields
x_{n+1}=R(x_{n})+S(x_{n})\sqrt{P(x_{n})}
,
with the same
P
as in (
9
), and with rational
R
and
S
. Note that the second
determination of the square root of
P
yields
x_{n-1}
, as
E
is symmetric.
We see that
R
and
S
are rational functions of degree 2,

R(x)=-\dfrac{e_{1,2}x^{2}+e_{1,1}x+e_{0,1}}{2(e_{2,2}x^{2}+e_{1,2}x+e_{0,2})}
,

S(x)=\dfrac{\ \ \sigma\ \ }{e_{2,2}x^{2}+e_{1,2}x+e_{0,2}}
,
by (
15
) and (
16
), where
\sigma
is a constant, knowing that
P(x)
is a constant times

(e_{1,2}x^{2}+e_{1,1}x+e_{0,1})^{2}-4(e_{0,2}x^{2}+e_{0,1}x+e_{0,0})(e_{2,2}x^{2}+e_{1,2}x+e_{0,2})
.

3.3.4.
Answers to §
3.1.1
.

\{an+b+cn^{2}\}
is an elliptic lattice, or grid, (a very special one). Indeed, the
sum
a(n-1)+b+c(n-1)^{2}+a(n+1)+b+c(n+1)^{2}=2(an+b+cn^{2})+2c

and the product
[a(n-1)+b+c(n-1)^{2}][a(n+1)+b+c(n+1)^{2}]=a^{2}n^{2}+2abn+2ac(n^{3}-n)+2bcn^{2}+c^{2}(n^{4}-2n^{2})-a^{2}+b^{2}+2bc+c^{2}=(an+b+cn^{2})^{2}-2c(an+b+cn^{2})-a^{2}+b^{2}+4bc+c^{2}

are rational functions of
an+b+cn^{2}
. By the way, if
x_{n}
is
elliptic, does the same hold for
x_{n}+an+b
? Normally, no, but

x_{an+b}
is, see Definition 4, where
x_{s}
may be defined for any real or
complex
s
.

\sqrt{n}
is not elliptic:
\sqrt{n-1}+\sqrt{n+1}
is not a rational function
of
\sqrt{n}
.

3.3.5.
Answer to §
2.2.2
exercise.

One immediately has
\chi_{n}=\dfrac{x_{n+1}-x_{n-1}}{y_{n}-y_{n-1}}=\dfrac{2\sigma\sqrt{P(x_{n})}}{e_{2,2}x^{2}+e_{1,2}x+e_{0,2}}\dfrac{X_{2}(x_{n})}{\sqrt{P(x_{n})}}\\
=
a constant times

\dfrac{X_{2}(x_{n})}{e_{2,2}x_{n}^{2}+e_{1,2}x_{n}+e_{0,2}}

from (
9
) and the formula for
S_{n}
above. How sloppy! we do not
know the signs of the two square roots of
P
.
For a more complete, but tedious, explanation, we follow the suggestion of
§
2.2.2
and computations already done in , so,

\chi_{n}=\dfrac{-Y_{1}(y_{n})/Y_{2}(y_{n})+Y_{1}(y_{n-1})/Y_{2}(y_{n-1})}{y_{n}-y_{n-1}}
,which is
a symmetric rational function of
y_{n}
and
y_{n-1}
, so, of
x_{n}
. By the simple
fractions expansions
Y_{1}(y)/Y_{2}(y)=c_{1,2}/c_{2,2}+Y_{1}(c)/(Y^{\prime}2(c)(y-c))+Y_{1}(d)/(Y^{\prime}_{2}(d)/(y-d))
,where
c
and
d
are the roots of
Y_{2}(y)=0
(horizontal asymptotes).
Remark that
Y^{\prime}_{2}(c)=c_{2,2}(c-d)
and
Y^{\prime}_{2}(d)=c_{2,2}(d-c)
are opposite. Then,

\chi_{n}=\dfrac{Y_{1}(c)/((y_{n-1}-c)(y_{n}-c))-Y_{1}(d)/((y_{n-1}-d)(y_{n}-d))}{c_{2,2}(c-d)}=\dfrac{Y_{1}(c)/F(x_{n},c)-Y_{1}(d)/F(x_{n},d)}{c_{2,2}(c-d)/X_{2}(x_{n})}\\
=\dfrac{1/(x_{n}+Y_{0}(c)/Y_{1}(c))-1/(x_{n}+Y_{0}(d)/Y_{1}(d))}{c_{2,2}(c-d)/X_{2}(x_{n})}\\
=\dfrac{(Y_{0}(d)/Y_{1}(d)-Y_{0}(c)/Y_{1}(d))X_{2}(x_{n})}{c_{2,2}(c-d)(x_{n}+Y_{0}(c)/Y_{1}(c))(x_{n}+Y_{0}(d)/Y_{1}(d))}

is the full answer.

A near fixed point and a true one.

3.4.
Chasing fixed points.

It seems obvious that there are always 4 fixed points, the 4 roots of
E(x,x)=0
, but
no: we simply have a mirror configuration
x_{n+1}=x_{n},x_{n+2}=x_{n-1}
, etc. see
fig.
5
.

Proposition.

There is a fixed point
\dotsb=x_{n-1}=x_{n}=x_{n+1}=\dotsb=x_{\infty}
only if the equation
P(x)=0
has a double root, i.e., in the zero-genus case
.

Indeed, as
x_{n\pm 1}=R(x_{n})\pm S(x_{n})\sqrt{P(x_{n})}
, one must have

P(x_{\infty})=0
, the tangent of the curve
E(x,y)=0
is vertical at
x=y=x_{\infty}
,
so,
\partial E(x,y)/\partial y=0
at this point, but
\partial E/\partial x=0
as well,
from symmetry of
E
. So,
(x_{\infty},x_{\infty})
is a double point of the
curve
E=0
, which looks like two directions
y-x_{\infty}=
constant
times
x-x_{\infty}
near
(x_{\infty},x_{\infty})
, see fig.
5
. ∎

In the simple geometric case,
E(x,y)=(y-qx)(x-qy)
, the fixed
points are 0 and
\infty
. For ANSUW, the fixed point is
\infty
.

3.5.
Definition 3. Continued fraction.

3.5.1.
Continued fraction.

Square roots and continued fractions share a long history,
simplest example is
A=\sqrt{a^{2}+1}=a+\dfrac{1}{a+\sqrt{a^{2}+1}}=a+\dfrac{1}{2a+\dfrac{1}{2a+\ddots}}
, going back to Greek mathematics

[
21
, §1.2]
,
[
77
, §2.]
.

Similar constructions with the square root of a function

P
with a known Taylor-Maclaurin expansion have been imagined, such as

\sqrt{P(x)}=\sqrt{P(0)}+\dfrac{x}{\ \ \ \dfrac{x}{\sqrt{P(x)}-\sqrt{P(0)}}=\dfrac{\sqrt{P(x)}+\sqrt{P(0)}}{(P(x)-P(0))/x}\ \ \ }
. If
P
is a linear or quadratic polynomial,
one reproduces the same form and we get a simple periodic continued fraction. Continued fraction expansions built as
f(x)=
two first terms of
Taylor-Maclaurin expansion
+\dfrac{x^{2}}{g(x)}
are called

associated
continued fractions
[
77
,
109
,
136
]
etc.

For application to solid-state physics, see
[
58
]
.

When the degree of
P
is higher, we encounter again our subject matter:

3.5.2.
The definition.

An elliptic lattice, or grid,
is made of coefficients
x_{0},x_{1},\dotsc,x_{m},\dotsc
involved in the continued fraction expansion of

f(x)=\dfrac{\sqrt{P(x)}-V(x)}{(x-x_{0})(x-v)}=\dfrac{x-u}{\alpha_{0}(x-u)+\beta_{0}(x-v)-\dfrac{(x-u)(x-v)}{\alpha_{1}(x-u)+\beta_{1}(x-v)-\dfrac{(x-u)(x-v)}{\ddots}}},

(18)

containing the square root of a polynomial
P
of degree

\leqslant 4
,
where
V
is a quadratic polynomial interpolating a square root of
P
at
u
,
v
, and
x_{0}
. At the
m^{\text{th}}
step,

f_{m}(x)=\dfrac{\sqrt{P(x)}-V_{m}(x)}{\gamma_{m}(x-x_{m})(x-v)}=\dfrac{x-u}{\alpha_{m}(x-u)+\beta_{m}(x-v)-(x-v)f_{m+1}(x)},,

(19)

where
\alpha_{m}(x-u)+\beta_{m}(x-v)
is the linear interpolant to
(x-u)/f_{m}
about
x=u
and
x=v
, and
f_{m+1}(x)
is the remainder divided by
-(x-v)
.

We only need the square root of
P
as an analytic function in a neighborhood of
u
, and an independent determination in a neighborhood of
v
. Then,
V_{m}
interpolates the chosen determinations, and some (unknown) determination at
x_{m}
,
and
\alpha_{m}(x-u)+\beta_{m}(x-v)
is the linear interpolant to
(x-u)/f_{m}(x)
. Of course, as
f_{m}(u)=0
, we already need the derivative in
\beta_{m}=1/(f_{m}^{\prime}(u)(u-v))=(u-x_{0})/(P^{\prime}(u)/(2\sqrt{P(u)})-V^{\prime}_{m}(u)),\alpha_{m}=1/f(v)
which is itself a limit:
\alpha_{0}=(v-x_{0})/(P^{\prime}(v)/(2\sqrt{P(v)})-V^{\prime}_{0}(v))
.

We show that
f_{m}
and
f_{m+1}
have the same form in (
19
), with
\rho_{m}

the fourth root of
V_{m}^{2}(x)-P(x)=\delta_{m}(x-u)(x-v)(x-x_{m})(x-\rho_{m})
:

f_{m}(x)=\dfrac{1}{\ \ \dfrac{\gamma_{m}(x-x_{m})(x-v)\ \ \ }{-V_{m}(x)+\sqrt{P(x)}}\ \ \ }=\dfrac{V_{m}^{2}(x)-P(x)=\delta_{m}(x-u)(x-v)(x-x_{m})(x-\rho_{m})}{\ \ \ \gamma_{m}(x-x_{m})(x-v)[-V_{m}(x)-\sqrt{P(x)}]}

=\dfrac{x-u}{\alpha_{m}(x-u)+\beta_{m}(x-v)-(x-v)\left(\dfrac{V_{m}(x)+\sqrt{P(x)}}{\delta_{m}(x-v)(x-\rho_{m})/\gamma_{m}}+\dfrac{\alpha_{m}(x-u)}{x-v}+\beta_{m}]\right)}

So,
V_{m+1}(x)=-V_{m}(x)-\delta_{m}(x-\rho_{m})[\alpha_{m}(x-u)+\beta_{m}(x-v)]/\gamma_{m}
, and
\gamma_{m+1}=\delta_{m}/\gamma_{m},x_{m+1}=\rho_{m}
.

More on
\delta_{m}
, coefficient of
x^{4}
of the quartic
V_{m}^{2}-P
: as
V_{m}
interpolates a square root of
P
at
u,v
and
x_{m}
, let
V_{m}(x)=X(x)+z_{m}(x-u)(x-v)
, where the quadratic (or linear) polynomial
X
performs the interpolation
at
u
and
v
, so,

V_{m}^{2}(x)-P(x)=(x-u)(x-v)[Y(x)+2z_{m}X+z_{m}^{2}(x-u)(x-v)],\\
\text{\ where\ }Y\text{\ is\ the\ polynomial\ }(X^{2}(x)-P(x))/((x-u)(x-v)).

(20)

\delta_{m}(x-x_{m})(x-x_{m+1})=\gamma_{m}(x-x_{m})\gamma_{m+1}(x-x_{m+1})=Y(x)+2z_{m}X+z_{m}^{2}(x-u)(x-v)

This identity appears as
R=r_{m}^{2}+s_{m}s_{m-1}
in Abel’s famous work on the integral of
\rho(x)/\sqrt{R(x}

[
2
, §12]
, see also Houzel
[
63
, §4, pp. 73-77]
, Zannier
[
143
]
.

Formulas for quadratic irrationals are strikingly similar, see fig.
6
.

Figure 6.
Formula (3) in §20 of Perron 1913
[
109
]
on
quadratic irrationals, and where it is added that
P_{\nu},Q_{\nu}
, AND
(D-P_{\nu}^{2})/Q_{\nu}
are integers.

3.5.3.
M-
and
T-
continued fraction.

The
M-
or
T-
continued fraction expansion
[
38
, § 6.6, 6.7]

[
70
]

[
71
, § 7.3]
of (at least formal) power
expansions about
x=u
and
x=v
is

f(x)=f(u)+\dfrac{z}{\alpha_{0}z+\beta_{0}-\dfrac{z}{\alpha_{1}z+\beta_{1}-\dfrac{z}{\ddots}}},

with
z=\dfrac{x-u}{x-v}
,

f(x)-f(u)=f_{0}(x)=\dfrac{x-u}{\alpha_{0}(x-u)+\beta_{0}(x-v)-(x-v)f_{1}(x)}
,
where
\alpha_{0}(x-u)+\beta_{0}(x-v)
is the linear interpolant to
(x-u)/f_{0}
about
x=u
and
x=v
,
and where the
operation is repeated on
f_{1}
etc.
The existence of the
\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}
s depends on the nonvanishing
of relevant determinants
[
38
, § 6.6, 6.7]

[
71
, § 7.3]
.

The
T-
fractions were itroduced in 1948 by W.J. Thron, and their two-point Padé property
was established in 1976 by J.H. McCabe and J.A. Murphy
[
36
,
100
,
99
]
, see below in §
3.5.6
for
more on the two-point Padé property.

3.5.4.
Proof of
1\Rightarrow 3
.

Let
{x_{n},y_{n}}
be elliptic grids determined by a biquadratic curve
F(x,y)=X_{0}(x)+X_{1}(x)y+X_{2}(x)y^{2}=0
, let
V_{m}(x)=X_{1}(x)+2y_{m}X_{2}(x),u
and
v
be the roots of
X_{2}(x)=(x-u)(x-v)=0
(same notation as in §
3.1.3
) , and we see
that this
V_{m}
interpolates indeed a square root of
P
at
u,v,x_{m},x_{m+1}
:

V_{m}^{2}(x)-P(x)=X_{1}^{2}(x)+4y_{m}X_{1}(x)X_{2}(x)+4y_{m}^{2}X_{2}^{2}(x)-X_{1}^{2}(x)+4X_{0}(x)X_{2}(x)\\
=4X_{2}(x)\left[\underbrace{X_{0}(x)+y_{m}X_{1}(x)+y_{m}^{2}X_{2}(x)}_{\displaystyle F(x,y_{m})=Y_{2}(y_{m})(x-x_{m})(x-x_{m+1})}\right]
, so
\delta_{m}=4Y_{2}(y_{m})
. ∎

Halphen
[
57
, chap. XIV, (48), p. 603]
gives the equivalent of our
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta
s in terms of the Weierstrass
\wp
function.

3.5.5.
Proof of
3\Rightarrow 1
.

We have the same form for
f
and its transformations, but the same symbols may have different meanings. Now,
P,u,v,x_{0}
are given. From
V_{0},X,Y
in (
20
) at
m=0
, we make
X_{0}=Y/4,X_{1}=X,X_{2}(x)=(x-u)(x-v)
, so we have
F(x,y)
. At the
m^{\text{th}}
step, again written as

f_{m}(x)=\dfrac{-V_{m}(x)+\sqrt{P(x)}}{\gamma_{m}(x-x_{m})(x-v)}=\dfrac{x-u}{\alpha_{m}(x-u)+\beta_{m}(x-v)-(x-v)f_{m+1}(x)}
, where
z_{m}
in
V_{m}(x)=X(x)+z_{m}(x-u)(x-v)
is such that

x_{m}
is a root of
V_{m}^{2}(x)-P(x)=0
(together with
u
and
v
),
x_{m+1}
is
defined

here as the fourth root, so that
x_{m}
and
x_{m+1}
solve
Y(x)+2z_{m}X(x)+z_{m}^{2}X_{2}(x)=4F(x,z_{m}/2)=0
, and
z_{m}/2
is a valid
y_{m}
. ∎

3.5.6.
Approximants

.

Approximants of
f_{0}
are
\dfrac{A_{-1}(x)}{B_{1}(x)}=\dfrac{-1/(x-v)}{0},\dfrac{A_{0}(x)}{B_{0}(x)}=\dfrac{0}{1},\dfrac{A_{1}(x)}{B_{1}(x)}=\dfrac{x-u}{\alpha_{0}(x-u)+\beta_{0}(x-v)},\\
\dfrac{A_{2}(x)}{B_{2}(x)}=\dfrac{(x-u)(\alpha_{1}(x-u)+\beta_{1}(x-v))}{(\alpha_{0}(x-u)+\beta_{0}(x-v))(\alpha_{1}(x-u)+\beta_{1}(x-v))-(x-u)(x-v)}
.

A_{m}
and
Bm
satisfy the same recurrence relation, as well as any
linear combination, the most useful being

B_{n+1}(x)f_{0}(x)-A_{n+1}(x)=(\alpha_{n}(x-u)+\beta_{n}(x-v))(B_{n}(x)f_{0}(x)-A_{n}(x))-(x-u)(x-v)(B_{n-1}(x)f_{0}(x)-A_{n-1}(x))
,

\dfrac{B_{n+1}(x)f_{0}(x)-A_{n+1}(x)}{B_{n}(x)f_{0}(x)-A_{n}(x)}=\alpha_{n}(x-u)+\beta_{n}(x-v)-\dfrac{(x-u)(x-v)}{\ \ \ \dfrac{B_{n}(x)f_{0}(x)-A_{n}(x)}{B_{n-1}(x)f_{0}(x)-A_{n-1}(x)}\ \ \ }
, or

\dfrac{B_{n}(x)f_{0}(x)-A_{n}(x)}{B_{n-1}(x)f_{0}(x)-A_{n-1}(x)}=\dfrac{(x-u)(x-v)}{\alpha_{n}(x-u)+\beta_{n}(x-v)-\dfrac{B_{n+1}(x)f_{0}(x)-A_{n+1}(x)}{B_{n}(x)f_{0}(x)-A_{n}(x)}}
, so

\dfrac{B_{n}(x)f_{0}(x)-A_{n}(x)}{(x-v)(B_{n-1}(x)f_{0}(x)-A_{n-1}(x))}=f_{n}(x)
and

B_{n}(x)f_{0}(x)-A_{n}(x)=(x-v)^{n}f_{0}(x)\dotsb f_{n}(x)

showing that
A_{n}(x)/B_{n}(x)-f_{0}(x)=O((x-u)^{n+1})
about
x=u
, and

O((x-v)^{n})
about
x=v
(
two-point Padé approximation
).
If
u=v
, the approximants of the associated continued fraction are
Padé approximants
[
38
, (2.3.5), (6.5.1)]

(
[
108
, 3.10.11]
where it is called Jacobi)

[
109
, §61, §77 (1913) §25, §44 (1957)]
.
When
u=v=\infty
, one must expand in series of
1/x
, and
we have a Jacobi continued fraction
[
38
,
108
,
109
,
136
]
.

(x-v)^{n}f_{0}(x)\dotsb f_{m}(x)=B_{m}(x)f_{0}(x)-A_{m}(x)=[B_{m}(x)\sqrt{P(x)}-\tilde{A}_{m}(x)]/(\gamma_{0}(x-x_{0})(x-v))

with
\tilde{A}_{m}=V_{0}(x)B_{m}(x)+\gamma_{0}(x-x_{0})(x-v)A_{n}(x)

from
f_{0}(x)=\dfrac{-V_{0}(x)+\sqrt{P(x)}}{\gamma_{0}(x-x_{0})(x-v)}

Outside a system of cuts,
f_{m}(x)=\dfrac{-V_{m}(x)+\sqrt{P(x)}}{\gamma_{m}(x-x_{m})(x-v)}
is a meromorphic function with a possible pole at
x_{m}
. With the conjugate
function
f_{m}^{(\text{conj})}(x)=\dfrac{-V_{m}(x)-\sqrt{P(x)}}{\gamma_{m}(x-x_{m})(x-v)}
,
the product
f_{m}(x)f_{m}^{\text{conj})}(x)=\dfrac{\delta_{m+1}(x-u)(x-x_{m+1})}{\delta_{m}(x-v)(x-x_{m})}
, only one of
f_{m},f_{m}^{(\text{conj})}

has a pole at
x_{m}
. This is an elementary way to cope with a
two-sheeted Riemann surface.

Product of the
f
s:

(x-v)^{n}f_{0}(x)\dotsb f_{n-1}(x)\;f_{0}^{(\text{conj})}(x)\dotsb f_{n-1}^{(\text{conj})}(x)=(x-u)^{n}\delta_{n}(x-x_{n})/(\delta_{0}(x-x_{0}))\\
=(x-v)^{-n+2}\dfrac{B^{2}_{n-1}(x)P(x)-\tilde{A}^{2}_{n-1}(x)}{4\delta_{0}^{2}(x-v)^{2}(x-x_{0})^{2}}
.

Pell
property:
x_{n}
is such that there exists polynomials

\tilde{A}_{n-1}
and
B_{n-1}
such that

B^{2}_{n-1}(x)P(x)-\tilde{A}^{2}_{n-1}(x)=4\delta_{0}\delta_{n}(x-u)^{n}(x-v)^{n}(x-x_{0})(x-x_{n})

(21)

See
[
83
]
for a modern treatment of Pell’s equation.

3.6.
Definition 4. Elliptic functions

An elliptic grid is a sequence

x_{n}=\mathcal{E}(nh+t_{0})
, where
\mathcal{E}
is any elliptic function of order 2 (i.e., bivalent in a fundamental parallelogram of periods)
.

3.6.1.
Proof of
1\Rightarrow 4
.

How on earth to find elliptic functions out of
F(x_{n},y_{n})=0
and
F(x_{n+1},y_{n})=0
with the biquadratic polynomial
F
of §
2.2.1
?

One may establish that the biquadratic curve
F(x,y)=0
in (
5
)
has genus 1 and a parametric representation

x=\mathcal{E}_{1}(t),\ \ y=\mathcal{E}_{2}(t),

with
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
elliptic functions of order 2.

Indeed, a birational transformation
(x,y)\leftrightarrow(r,s)
sending the biquadratic
curve (
5
)
F(x,y)=0
to the canonical cubic curve, known to have genus 1,
s^{2}=R(r)
, where
R
is a
polynomial of third degree, see Appell & Goursat
[
7
, p.292]
: from (
9
), choose

w=
a square root of
P(x)
, so that
y=(-X_{1}(x)+w)/(2X_{2}(x))\leftrightarrow w=X_{1}(x)+2yX_{2}(x)
, and
x=z_{1}+1/r
, where
z_{1}
is one of the four roots
of
P(x)=0
, and take
s=wr^{2}
.

\left\{\begin{matrix}x&=&z_{1}+1/r,&\ \ &r&=&1/(x-z_{1}),\\
y&=&(-X_{1}(z_{1}+1/r)+s/r^{2})/(2X_{2}(z_{1}+1/r))&\ \ &s&=&(X_{1}(x)+2yX_{2}(x))/(x-z_{1})^{2}\end{matrix}\right.

(22)

Then, with
P(z_{1}+1/r)=R(r)/r^{4}
,
s^{2}=R(r)
of third degree,
the Weierstrass representation holds
r=A+B\wp(at+b),s=C\wp^{\prime}(at+b)
.

Note that
R(r)=r^{4}P(z_{1}+1/r)=P^{\prime}(z_{1})r^{3}+P^{\prime\prime}(z_{1})r^{2}/2+P^{\prime\prime\prime}(z_{1})r/6+\epsilon
.

So,
x=z_{1}+1/(A+B\wp),y=(-X_{1}+C\wp^{\prime}/(A+B\wp)^{2})/(2X_{2})
, which are our elliptic functions
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
! See also
[
33
]
. But how to show that the arguments of
x_{0},x_{1},\dotsc
make an arithmetic progression?

Let
(r_{m},s_{m})
on the curve
s^{2}=R(r)
correspond to
(x_{m},y_{m})=(\mathcal{E}_{1}(t_{m}),\mathcal{E}_{2}(t_{m}))
through (
22
). The addition theorem of elliptic functions in this cubic curve setting tells that the line joining a point
(r^{*},s^{*})
of argument
t^{*}
on the cubic curve to
(r_{m},s_{m})
meets the curve at a point of argument
-(t^{*}+t_{m})
(up to integer combinations of periods)
[
7
, chap. XII, §226]
,
[
120
, §III.2.]
.

One must show that the line joining
(r_{m+1},-s_{m+1})
to
(r_{m},s_{m})
must meet the cubic curve at
(r^{*},s^{*})
independent of
m
, representing the step.

\psset

xunit=10cm,yunit=0.5cm

{pspicture}
(-0.2,-5)(1,7.5)

\psline
-¿(-0.2,0)(1.05,0)
\psline
-¿(0,-5)(0,5)

\uput
[90](1.05,0)

Figure 7.
Successive grid points
(r,s)
on the cubic curve.

Numerical confirmation has been tried with the biquadratic
F
of §
3.1.3
,
(x_{0},y_{0})=(0,-0.2716),(x_{1},y_{1})=(-0.6955,-0.6220)
etc. giving by (
22
)
(r_{0},s_{0})=(0.5,2.815),(r_{1},s_{1})=(0.7666,4.646)
, using here
z_{1}=-2,R(r)=r^{4}P(-2+1/r)=-443.8(r-2/7)(r-1/3)(r-1)
.

On fig.
7
, the lines joining
(r_{m+1},-s_{m+1})
to
(r_{m},s_{m})
seem almost parallel! but the calculations show indeed a single point
(-1.4126,56.345)
of course too far. However, looking for the double step, joining now
(r_{m+2},-s_{m+2})
to
(r_{m},s_{m})
, we see the meting point
(0.00181,6.4572)
, the dashed lines in fig.
7
.

Proof that the line joining
(r_{m+1},-s_{m+1})
to
(r_{m},s_{m})

does meet the cubic curve at
(r^{*},s^{*})
independent of
m
.

We enter
s=\mu_{m}(r-r_{m})+s_{m}
in
s^{2}-R(r)=P^{\prime}(z_{1})(r-r_{m})(r-r_{m+1})(r-r^{*})=0
, where
r^{*}
is the unknown.

r_{m}+r_{m+1}+r^{*}=(\mu^{2}_{m}-P^{\prime\prime}(z_{1})/2)/P^{\prime}(z_{1})
, with
\mu_{m}=\dfrac{-s_{m+1}-s_{m}}{r_{m+1}-r_{m}}\\
=\dfrac{r_{m+1}^{2}(X_{1}(z_{1}+1/r_{m+1})+2y_{m}X_{2}(z_{1}+1/r_{m+1}))-r_{m}^{2}(X_{1}(z_{1}+1/r_{m})+2y_{m}X_{2}(z_{1}+1/r_{m}))}{r_{m+1}-r_{m}}\\
=(r_{m+1}+r_{m})w(z_{1},y_{m})\\
+(\partial w(x,y_{m})/\partial x)(x=z_{1})=\dfrac{-4X_{2}(z_{1})(\partial F(x,y_{m})/\partial x)_{x=z_{1}}}{w(z_{1},y_{m})}+(\partial w(x,y_{m})/\partial x)_{x=z_{1}}
,
where
w(x,y)=X_{1}(x)+2yX_{2}(x)=\partial F/\partial y
, and
using from (
9
)

P(z_{1})=X_{1}^{2}-4X_{0}X_{2}=0\Rightarrow F(z_{1},y)=\dfrac{[2X_{2}(z_{1})y+X_{1}(z_{1})]^{2}}{4X_{2}(z_{1})}=\dfrac{w^{2}(z_{1},y)}{4X_{2}(z_{1})},\\
r_{m+1}+r_{m}=\dfrac{1}{x_{m+1}-z_{1}}+\dfrac{1}{x_{m}-z_{1}}=\dfrac{-(\partial F(x,y_{m})/\partial x)_{x=z_{1}}}{F(z_{1},y_{m})=w^{2}(z_{1},y)/(4X_{2}(z_{1}))}
, and also

y_{m}+y_{m+1}=-X_{1}(x_{m+1})/X_{2}(x_{m+1})
from (
6
), so that
-s_{m+1}=(X_{1}(x_{m+1})+2y_{m}X_{2}(x_{m+1}))/(x_{m+1}-z_{1})^{2}
,

P^{\prime}(z_{1})r^{*}+-P^{\prime\prime}(z_{1})/2=-P^{\prime}(z_{1})(r_{m}+r_{m+1})+\mu^{2}_{m}\\
=4X_{2}(z_{1})P^{\prime}(z_{1})\dfrac{(\partial F(x,y_{m})/\partial x)_{x=z_{1}}}{w^{2}(z_{1},y_{m})}+\left[4X_{2}(z_{1})\dfrac{-(\partial F(x,y_{m})/\partial x)_{x=z_{1}}}{w(z_{1},y_{m})}+(\partial w(x,y_{m})/\partial x)_{x=z_{1}}\right]^{2}

=4X_{2}(z_{1})(\partial F(x,y_{m})/\partial x)_{x=z_{1}}\dfrac{P^{\prime}(z_{1})+4X_{2}(z_{1})(\partial F(x,y_{m})/\partial x)_{x=z_{1}}}{w^{2}(z_{1},y_{m})}\\
-8X_{2}(z_{1})\dfrac{(\partial F(x,y_{m})/\partial x)_{x=z_{1}}(\partial w(x,y_{m})/\partial x)_{x=z_{1}}}{w(z_{1},y_{m})}+(\partial w(x,y_{m})/\partial x)^{2}_{x=z_{1}}=4X_{2}(z_{1})(X^{\prime}_{0}+X^{\prime}_{1}y+X^{\prime}_{2}y^{2})_{x=z_{1}}\\
\times\dfrac{2X^{\prime}_{1}+X^{\prime}_{2}(2X_{2}y_{m}-X_{1})/X_{2}-2(X^{\prime}_{1}+2y_{m}X^{\prime}_{2})=X^{\prime}_{2}[-2y-X_{1}/X_{2}]=-X^{\prime}_{2}w/X_{2}}{w(z_{1},y_{m})}\\
+(X^{\prime}_{1}+2yX^{\prime}_{2})^{2}_{x=z_{1}}=-4X^{\prime}_{0}(z_{1})X^{\prime}_{2}(z_{1})+(X^{\prime}_{1})^{2}(z_{1})
,

using
P^{\prime}(z_{1})+4X_{2}(z_{1})(\partial F(x,y_{m})/\partial x)_{x=z_{1}}=2X_{1}X^{\prime}_{1}-4X_{0}X^{\prime}_{2}-4X^{\prime}_{0}X_{2}+4X_{2}(X^{\prime}_{2}y^{2}+X^{\prime}_{1}y+X^{\prime}_{0})=2X^{\prime}_{1}\left(X_{1}+2yX_{2}=w\right)+4X^{\prime}_{2}[X_{2}y^{2}-X_{0}=(2X_{2}y-X_{1})w/(4X_{2})]
, as
X_{0}(z_{1})=X_{1}^{2}(z_{1})/(4X_{2}(z_{1}))
.

This ends the proof that
r,s
of the step is independent of
m
on the cubic curve. A very tedious and uninspiring proof, sorry.

3.6.2.
Proof of
4\Rightarrow 1
.

Let the elliptic function
\mathcal{E}_{1}
:
x_{n}=\mathcal{E}_{1}(nh)
and consider the function
\mathcal{E}_{2}(t)=\mathcal{E}_{1}(t+h/2)
. These two elliptic functions of order 2 and sharing same periods must be related by a biquadratic equation
F(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2})=0

[
141
, §20.54]
, so,
F(x_{n},y_{n})=0
, with
y_{n}=\mathcal{E}_{2}(nh)
.
This short proof is incomplete, as it does not establish
F(x_{n+1},y_{n})=0
…

We have
x_{m}=\mathcal{E}(mh)
with
\mathcal{E}
of order 2. Let
P
be the quartic polynomial vanishing at the four values
z_{1},\dotsc,z_{4}
of
\mathcal{E}
when the derivative
d\mathcal{E}/dt
vanishes in a fundamental parallelogram. The ratio
(d\mathcal{E}(t)/dt)^{2}/P(\mathcal{E}(t))
has no singularity, so is a constant, so

t=\text{\ constant\ }+\text{\ constant\ }\int^{x=\mathcal{E}}\dfrac{du}{\sqrt{P(u)}}

First, we find
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
in
\xi=\dfrac{\alpha x+\beta}{1+\gamma x}
of (
11
) so to send the four
zeros of
P
on
\{-k^{-1},-1,1,k^{-1}\}
as in §
3.2
,

mh=\int_{\xi_{0}}^{\xi_{m}}\dfrac{du}{\sqrt{(1-u^{2})(1-k^{2}u^{2})}}
, i.e.,
\xi_{m}=\xi_{0}+{\text{sn}}(mh)
with the Jacobian ellliptic sine function, and we
define
here
\eta_{m}={\text{sn}}((m+h/2)
.

Consider now these Jacobi functions identities

{\text{sn}}(s+a)+{\text{sn}}(s-a)=\dfrac{2{\text{cn}}a\,{\text{dn}}a\;{\text{sn}}s}{1-k^{2}{\text{sn}}^{2}a\,{\text{sn}}^{2}s},{\text{sn}}(s+a){\text{sn}}(s-a)=\dfrac{{\text{sn}}^{2}s-{\text{sn}}^{2}a}{1-k^{2}{\text{sn}}^{2}a\,{\text{sn}}^{2}s},

(23)

[
5
, ell table XIV p.208]
, and take
a=h/2,s=(m+1/2)h
, so

\xi_{m}+\xi_{m+1}=2\dfrac{2{\text{cn}}a\,{\text{dn}}a\;\eta_{m}}{1-k^{2}{\text{sn}}^{2}a\,\eta_{m}^{2}},\xi_{m}\xi_{m+1}=\dfrac{\eta_{m}^{2}-{\text{sn}}^{2}a}{1-k^{2}{\text{sn}}^{2}a\,\eta_{m}^{2}}

reproducing (
6
) (with
\xi_{0}=0
),

and we build the biquadratic
\mathcal{F}(\xi,\eta)=\xi^{2}\mathcal{Y}_{2}(\eta)+\xi\mathcal{Y}_{1}(\eta)+\mathcal{Y}_{0}(\eta)

with
\mathcal{Y}_{0}(\eta)=\eta^{2}-{\text{sn}}^{2}a
,
\mathcal{Y}_{1}(\eta)=-2{\text{cn}}a\,{\text{dn}}a\;\eta
,

\mathcal{Y}_{2}(\eta)=1-k^{2}{\text{sn}}^{2}a\,\eta^{2}
, or

\mathcal{F}(\xi,\eta)=-k^{2}{\text{sn}}^{2}a\xi^{2}\eta^{2}+\xi^{2}+\eta^{2}-2{\text{cn}}a\,{\text{dn}}a\;\xi\eta-{\text{sn}}^{2}a=0

(24)

cf. Baxter
[
12
, eq. 15.10.3]
, Maillard
[
87
, eq. (13) p.294]
.

Given the symmetric
\mathcal{F}(\xi,\eta)
of above, starting with

\xi_{0}=0,\eta_{0}=\pm{\text{sn}}a,\\
\xi_{1}=\pm 2{\text{cn}}a\,{\text{dn}}a\;{\text{sn}}a/(1-k^{2}{\text{sn}}^{4}a)={\text{sn}}h
etc.

If we start with the other
\eta-
root
-{\text{sn}}a
, the step is
-h
,

{\text{cn}}a\,{\text{dn}}a
is a square root of
(1-{\text{sn}}^{2}a)(1-k^{2}{\text{sn}}^{2}a)
. What happens if the wrong square
root is used? Answer: add a half period to
h\to h+2K

[
5
, table XII p. 206]
.

This is the fast and learned proof of
4\Rightarrow 1
. ∎

Figure 8.
Appropriate contour.

On the other hand,
we look at the integral of
\dfrac{\ \ \log\dfrac{\tilde{A}_{m-1}(x)+B_{m-1}(x)\sqrt{P(x)}}{\tilde{A}_{m-1}(x)-B_{m-1}(x)\sqrt{P(x)}}dx\ \ }{2\pi i\sqrt{P(x)}}
on a big contour, where the polynomials
\tilde{A}_{m-1},B_{m-1}

come from the Pell equation (
21
).
The integral must vanish, as we integrate
O(x^{-2})
on the big contour
\to\infty
, and the singularities which are the zeros of
P
and
\tilde{A}_{m-1}\pm B_{m-1}\sqrt{P}
, i.e.
z_{1},\dotsc,z_{4},x_{0},x_{m},u,v
are in the bounded complex plane. We define the square root of
P
as continuous far from the singular points just described,
outside two arcs joining
[z_{1},z_{2}]
and
[z_{3},z_{4}]
.

We now make the contour shrink about these arcs and the singular points, one finds

0=-(m+1)\int_{z_{1}}^{u}\frac{dx}{\sqrt{P(x)}}-m\int_{z_{1}}^{v}\frac{dx}{\sqrt{P(x)}}-\int_{z_{1}}^{x_{0}}\frac{dx}{\sqrt{P(x)}}\pm\int_{z_{1}}^{x_{m}}\frac{dx}{\sqrt{P(x)}}+N\int_{z_{2}}^{z_{3}}\frac{dx}{\sqrt{P(x)}},

(25)

where the first terms account for the zero of multiplicity
m+1,m
at
u,v

of
\tilde{A}_{m-1}(x)-B_{m-1}(x)\sqrt{P(x)}
, so that
the logarithm of the ratio on the lower path from
z_{0}
to
u
is the value
on the upper path minus
m+1
and
m
;
the
\pm
signs tell if
x_{m}
is a zero of
\tilde{A}_{m-1}+
or
-B_{m-1}\sqrt{P}
; and where

N
is an unknown integer related to the increase of the imaginary part of the logarithm on the two sides. There is only
1
integral

\displaystyle{\int_{z_{2}}^{z_{3}}\frac{dx}{\sqrt{P(x)}}}
to
consider, in the “gap” between the arcs. Integrals on the two sides of the arcs
vanish, as
\sqrt{P}_{+}=-\sqrt{P}_{-}
, so that the logarithm takes
opposite values on the sides.

I learned this technique in Nuttall & Singh
[
107
, lemma 5.2]

allowing polynomials
P
of degree
2l
(hyperelliptic case if
l>2
).
The full Abelian case is considered by Nuttall too
[
106
]
.

3.7.
A brief history
[
25
,
26
,
87
,
126
]

Elliptic lattices were developed by Baxter in the solution
of special problems of statistical physics, they appear in works
by Fritz John, in many treatments of a Poncelet problem

[
25
,
26
]

[
126
, § 6]
, and go back to
pioneering work by Euler
4
4

4

and perhaps even to Fermat
[communicated by R. Askey]!
on the addition formulas of elliptic
functions, that’s why the symmetric biquadratic polynomial
(
15
) has been called the Euler polynomial in

[
126
, p. 294]
.

Even the name of our subject is not easy to choose: “elliptic
sequences” seems perfect, but this name is used by other
sequences related in another way to elliptic functions (sequences

\{A_{n}\}
where
A_{n-1}A_{n+1}/A_{n}^{2}
is our
x_{n}
,
[
133
]
),
“elliptic lattice” may by used for the repetitions of the
periods parallelogram of an elliptic function, “elliptic grid”
means a convenient mesh for discretizing over ellipses, and
“elliptic difference operator” is a partial difference operator
extending partial differential operator of elliptic type.

4.
The divided difference operator.

We define
\mathcal{D}
of a function
f
as

\dfrac{f(x^{+})-f(x^{-})}{x^{+}-x^{-}}
, where
x^{-}
and
x^{+}

are the two
x-
roots of
F(x,y)=0
for some
y
. The value at
this
y

of
\mathcal{D}f
will often be noted as the
somewhat awkward
(\mathcal{D}f)(y)
.

Considering the preceding section on lattices, or grids,
we see that
f
must be known on at least a relevant
sequence
\{x_{n}\}
, so that
\mathcal{D}f
is automatically
defined on the companion lattice
\{y_{n}\}
:

(\mathcal{D}f)(y_{n})=\dfrac{f(x_{n+1})-f(x_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}.

f(x)

(\mathcal{D}f)(y)

1

0

x

1

x^{2}

-Y_{1}(y)/Y_{2}(y)

x^{3}

Y_{1}^{2}(y)/Y_{2}^{2}(y)-Y_{0}(y)/Y_{2}(y)

\dfrac{1}{x-A}

-\dfrac{Y_{2}(y)}{F(A,y)}

Table 3.
Some instances of the difference operator
\mathcal{D}
.

The simplest nontrivial example if
f(x)=x^{2}
, then
\mathcal{D}f
at
y_{n}

is
x_{n}+x_{n+1}=-Y_{1}(y_{n})/Y_{2}(y_{n})
from (
6
)

Exercise Find
\mathcal{D}(x-x_{\alpha})(x-x_{\alpha+1})
. The result has the factor
y-y_{\alpha}
.
What is the second factor?The author has no elegant solution.

Exercise. Find
f
such that
\mathcal{D}f=y
. Answer: not elementary! See §
6.1.3
.

The difference operator applied to a simple rational function is of
special interest. Let
\displaystyle f(x)=\dfrac{1}{x-A}
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}\dfrac{1}{x-A}
at
y
is
\dfrac{1}{x^{+}-x^{-}}\left[\dfrac{1}{x^{+}-A}-\dfrac{1}{x^{-}-A}\right]=-\dfrac{1}{(x^{+}-A)(x^{-}-A)}=-\dfrac{Y_{2}(y)}{Y_{0}(y)+AY_{1}(y)+A^{2}Y_{2}(y)}
,

and let

\{(x^{\prime}_{n},y^{\prime}_{n}),(x^{\prime}_{n},y^{\prime}_{n+1}),\dotsc\}
be the elliptic sequence on the
biquadratic curve
F(x,y)=0
such that
x^{\prime}_{0}=A
, then

(\mathcal{D}\dfrac{1}{x-x^{\prime}_{0}})(y)=-\dfrac{Y_{2}(y)}{X_{2}(A)(y-y^{\prime}_{0})(y-y^{\prime}_{-1})}

(26)

The divided difference operator useful up to the ANSUW
case can be recovered from the simple property that polynomials
are sent to polynomials of lower degree
[
89
]
. But
polynomials are not distinguished rational functions, as
\infty

is now an ordinary point.

Proposition.
Let
\{x_{n}\}
and
\{y_{m}\}
be two
sequences related by
F(x_{n},y_{n})=0
and
F(x_{n},y_{n-1})=0

for all
n
. Let
\dfrac{f(x_{n+1})-f(x_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}

be a rational function of degree
\leqslant 2\delta
of the
variable
y_{n}
when
f
is a rational function of degree
\delta
.
Then,
F
must be biquadratic and the sequences must be elliptic
lattices
.

The proof is easier than the statement.We try
f(x)=(x-A)^{-1}

as before, so,
(x_{n}-A)(x_{n+1}-A)
is a rational function
of degree
\leqslant 2
of
y_{n}
for any
A
, say,

\dfrac{N(y_{n},A)}{D(y_{n})}=\dfrac{Y_{0}(y_{n})+AY_{1}(y_{n})+A^{2}Y_{2}(y_{n})}{Y_{2}(y_{n})}
, so,

x_{n}x_{n+1}
and
x_{n}+x_{n+1}
are two different rational
functions of
y_{n}
, and we recover
F(x,y)=Y_{0}(y)+xY_{1}(y)+x^{2}Y_{2}(y)

of (
4a
). ∎

Special cases.
We already encountered the usual difference operators

(x,x+h)
or
(x-h,x)
or
(x-h/2,x+h/2)
corresponding to

Y_{2}(y)\equiv 1
,
Y_{1}
of degree 1,
Y_{0}
of degree 2 with
Q=Y_{1}^{2}-4Y_{0}Y_{2}
of degree 0.
For the geometric difference operator,
Q
is the square of a first degree polynomial.
For the Askey-Wilson operator
[
6
,
8
,
65
,
76
,
89
,
90
]
,
Q
is an arbitrary second degree
polynomial.

5.
Rational interpolatory elliptic expansions.

5.1.
Construction of the interpolant

Let
f
be a function defined on a sequence
x_{0},x_{1},\dotsc
, which need NOT be elliptic
at the present stage.
Rational interpolants of
f
at
x_{0}
,
x_{1},\dotsc,
with poles at

x^{\prime}_{0}
,
x^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,
are successive sums

c_{0}=f(x_{0}),\ c_{0}+c_{1}\dfrac{x-x_{0}}{x-x^{\prime}_{0}},\dotsc,\qquad\sum_{0}^{\infty}c_{m}\dfrac{(x-x_{0})\dotsb(x-x_{m-1})}{(x-x^{\prime}_{0})\dotsb(x-x^{\prime}_{m-1})},

(27)

where

c_{0}=f(x_{0}),c_{1}=(x_{1}-x^{\prime}_{0})\dfrac{f(x_{1})-f(x_{0})}{x_{1}-x_{0}},\\
c_{2}=(x_{2}-x^{\prime}_{0})(x_{2}-x^{\prime}_{1})\dfrac{f(x_{2})-f(x_{0})-c_{1}(x_{2}-x_{0})/(x_{2}-x^{\prime}_{0})}{(x_{2}-x_{0})(x_{2}-x_{1})}=(x_{2}-x^{\prime}_{0})(x_{2}-x^{\prime}_{1})\dfrac{f(x_{2})-f(x_{0})}{(x_{2}-x_{0})(x_{2}-x_{1})}\\
-c_{1}\dfrac{x_{2}-x^{\prime}_{1}}{x_{2}-x_{1}}=\dfrac{x_{2}-x^{\prime}_{1}}{x_{2}-x_{1}}\left[(x_{2}-x^{\prime}_{0})\dfrac{f(x_{2})-f(x_{0})}{x_{2}-x_{0}}-(x_{1}-x^{\prime}_{0})\dfrac{f(x_{1})-f(x_{0})}{x_{1}-x_{0}}\right],

The sum
\sum_{0}^{k}c_{m}(x-x^{\prime}_{m})\dotsb(x-x^{\prime}_{k-1})(x-x_{0})\dotsb(x-x_{m-1})

is the polynomial interpolant

of degree

5.3.1.
Theorem.

Let

\begin{split}\mathcal{D}\dfrac{(x-x_{r})\dotsb(x-x_{r+m-1})}{(x-x^{\prime}_{s})\dotsb(x-x^{\prime}_{s+m-1})}&=C_{m,r,s}Y_{2}(y)\dfrac{(y-y_{r})\dotsb(y-y_{r+m-2})}{(y-y^{\prime}_{s-1})\dotsb(y-y^{\prime}_{s+m-1})},\\

.

Let the rational function
P_{m,r,s}(x)=\dfrac{(x-x_{r})\dotsb(x-x_{r+m-1})}{(x-x^{\prime}_{s})\dotsb(x-x^{\prime}_{s+m-1})}
,
the two left-hand sides of (
34
) are the difference and the sum
of
P_{m,r,s}(x)
at
x=x_{n}
and at
x=x_{n+1}
for some
y=y_{n}
, so,

P_{m,r,s}(x_{n+1})=(D_{m,r,s}(y_{n})+(x_{n+1}-x_{n})C_{m,r,s}Y_{2}(y_{n})/2)\dfrac{(y_{n}-y_{r})\dotsb(y_{n}-y_{r+m-2})}{(y_{n}-y^{\prime}_{s-1})\dotsb(y_{n}-y^{\prime}_{s+m-1})},\\
P_{m,r,s}(x_{n})=(D_{m,r,s}(y_{n})-(x_{n+1}-x_{n})C_{m,r,s}Y_{2}(y_{n})/2)\dfrac{(y_{n}-y_{r})\dotsb(y_{n}-y_{r+m-2})}{(y_{n}-y^{\prime}_{s-1})\dotsb(y_{n}-y^{\prime}_{s+m-1})},

(40)

the right-hand sides are not rational functions of
y_{n}
, as

Y_{2}(y_{n})(x_{n+1}-x_{n})
is a square root of
Q(y_{n})
, from (
9
).

The equations (
39a
) and (
39c
) also show that
D_{m,r,s}
is the linear interpolant
at
y_{r-1}
and
y^{\prime}_{s-1}
augmented by a constant times

(y-y_{r-1})(y-y^{\prime}_{s-1})

D_{m,r,s}(y)/C_{m,r,s}=R_{r,s}(y)+\rho_{m,r,s}(y-y_{r-1})(y-y^{\prime}_{s-1}),

(41)

where

R_{r,s}(y)=\dfrac{(y-y_{r-1})Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{s-1})(x^{\prime}_{s}-x^{\prime}_{s-1})+(y-y^{\prime}_{s-1})Y_{2}(y_{r-1})(x_{r}-x_{r-1})}{2(y^{\prime}_{s-1}-y_{r-1})},

(42)

and
\rho_{m,r,s}
is the coefficient of
y^{2}
of
D_{m,r,s}(y)/C_{m,r,s}
.

\begin{split}\rho_{n,r,s}&=\dfrac{(x_{n+r}-x_{n+r-1})Y_{2}(y_{n+r-1})/2-R_{r,s}(y_{n+r-1})}{(y_{n+r-1}-y_{r-1})(y_{n+r-1}-y^{\prime}_{s-1})}\\
&=\dfrac{-(x^{\prime}_{n+s}-x^{\prime}_{n+s-1})Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{n+s-1})/2-R_{r,s}(y^{\prime}_{n+s-1})}{(y^{\prime}_{n+s-1}-y_{r-1})(y^{\prime}_{n+s-1}-y^{\prime}_{s-1})},\ \ n>0.\end{split}

(43)

and we have, from (
40
) and (
41
),

\dfrac{P_{m,r,s}(x_{n+1})}{P_{m,r,s}(x_{n})}=\dfrac{R_{r,s}(y_{n})+\rho_{m,r,s}(y_{n}-y_{r-1})(y_{n}-y^{\prime}_{s-1})+(x_{n+1}-x_{n})Y_{2}(y_{n})/2}{R_{r,s}(y_{n})+\rho_{m,r,s}(y_{n}-y_{r-1})(y_{n}-y^{\prime}_{s-1})-(x_{n+1}-x_{n})Y_{2}(y_{n})/2}.

(44)

6.
Linear 1
{}^{\text{st}}
order difference equations.

We look for linear equations involving
\mathcal{D}f
and

(\mathcal{M}f)(y)
, so of the form

A(y)(\mathcal{D}f)(y)=B(y)(\mathcal{M}f)(y)+C(y)

(45)

This already allows elliptic exponentials (
B(y)\equiv A(y)
) or
logarithms (
B(y)\equiv 0
).

We now try to expand a solution to (
45
) as an interpolatory
series. This has been done in
[
93
]
for polynomials
A,B,C
.
Only two special cases allowing hypergeometric expansions are given here

6.1.
”Elliptic logarithm” or elliptic Psi function

We investigate the solution of

\mathcal{D}f(y)=\dfrac{Y_{2}(y)}{y-A}

(46)

on
some
x_{n}
lattice, starting with a given value of
f(x_{0})
.

On the linear lattice
F(x,y)=(y-x)(y-x-1)
where
Y_{2}(y)\equiv 1
, we have the Psi, or digamma, function
\Psi(x)=d\log\Gamma(x)/dx

[
3
, chap. 6]
,
[
108
, chap. 5]
etc.

The Psi (
\Psi
) function is the meromorphic function
with poles at
0,-1,\dotsc,
satisfying
\Psi(x+1)=\Psi(x)+1/x

and
\Psi(x)-\log x\to 0
when
x\to+\infty
.

[
3
, chap. 6.3]

[
102
, chap. IX]

This function is sometimes found in probability calculus identities, for
instance
[
147
]
, let
0<\alpha<1
and

\displaystyle M(t)=\sum_{n}\dfrac{w_{n}}{1-x_{n}t}
which is here
\dfrac{\Gamma(\alpha+1)\Gamma(1-t)}{\Gamma(\alpha-t+1)}=\sum_{1}^{\infty}\Gamma(\alpha+1)\dfrac{(-1)^{n-1}}{(n-1)!(n-t)\Gamma(\alpha+1-n)}=\dfrac{\alpha}{1-t}-\dfrac{\alpha(\alpha-1)}{2-t}+\dotsb=\sum_{1}^{\infty}\dfrac{\alpha(1-\alpha)(2-\alpha)\dotsb(n-1-\alpha)}{n!(1-t/n)}
. The expectation is then
\sum x_{n}w_{n}=dM(t)/dt
at
t=0
, so,
-\Psi(1)+\Psi(\alpha+1)
.

We return to (
46
):
f(x_{1})=f(x_{0})+\dfrac{(x_{1}-x_{0})Y_{2}(y_{0})}{y_{0}-A},f(x_{2})=f(x_{1})+\dfrac{(x_{2}-x_{1})Y_{2}(y_{1})}{y_{1}-A}=f(x_{0})+\dfrac{(x_{1}-x_{0})Y_{2}(y_{0})}{y_{0}-A}+\dfrac{(x_{2}-x_{1})Y_{2}(y_{1})}{y_{1}-A}
, etc.

6.1.1.
Proposition

Let
f
be a solution of the difference equation (
46
)
on the elliptic sequence
\{x_{n},y_{n}\}
related to the biquadratic polynomial
F(x,y)=x^{2}Y_{2}(y)+xY_{1}(y)+Y_{0}(y)
,
and
\{x^{\prime}_{n},y^{\prime}_{n}\}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, another elliptic sequence
related to the same biquadratic polynomial, such that
y^{\prime}_{-1}=A
.

Then
f(x)
has the expansion

f(x)=\sum_{0}^{\infty}c_{m}\dfrac{(x-x_{0})\dotsb(x-x_{m-1})}{(x-x^{\prime}_{0})\dotsb(x-x^{\prime}_{m-1})},

(47)

where
c_{0}=f(x_{0})
may be chosen at will, and where

c_{m}=\dfrac{y_{m-1}-y^{\prime}_{m-1}}{C_{m,0,0}}

when
m>0
, with the
C_{m,0,0}
of thm
5.3.1
.

6.1.2.

A simple product (
hypergeometric
) formula for
c_{m}
holds as, from
(
38
)

\dfrac{c_{m+1}}{c_{m}}=\dfrac{y_{m}-y^{\prime}_{m}}{y_{m-1}-y^{\prime}_{m-1}}\ \dfrac{C_{m,0,0}}{C_{m+1,0,0}}=\dfrac{y_{m}-y^{\prime}_{m}}{y_{m-1}-y^{\prime}_{m-1}}\ \dfrac{(y_{-1}-y_{m-1})(x_{-1}-x^{\prime}_{m})}{(y_{-1}-y^{\prime}_{m})(x_{-1}-x_{m})}\\
=\dfrac{y_{m}-y^{\prime}_{m}}{y_{m-1}-y^{\prime}_{m-1}}\ \dfrac{(x^{\prime}_{0}-x^{\prime}_{m})(y^{\prime}_{-1}-y_{m-1})}{(x^{\prime}_{0}-x_{m})(y^{\prime}_{-1}-y^{\prime}_{m})}
.

.

Proof:
put the expansion of
f
in (
46
) and use thm
5.3.1
:

\dfrac{1}{y-A}=(Y_{2})^{-1}\mathcal{D}f=\sum_{1}^{\infty}c_{m}C_{m,0,0}\dfrac{(y-y_{0})\dotsb(y-y_{m-2})}{(y-y^{\prime}_{-1})\dotsb(y-y^{\prime}_{m-1})}
,
multiply by
(y-y^{\prime}_{-1})(y-y^{\prime}_{0})
:
(y-y^{\prime}_{-1})(y-y^{\prime}_{0})/(y-A)=

y-y^{\prime}_{0}=\sum_{1}^{\infty}c_{m}C_{m,0,0}\dfrac{(y-y_{0})\dotsb(y-y_{m-2})}{(y-y^{\prime}_{1})\dotsb(y-y^{\prime}_{m-1})}
, to compare to the
expansion (
33
) with
y^{\prime}_{0}
etc. shifted to
y^{\prime}_{1}
etc.

y-y^{\prime}_{0}=-y^{\prime}_{0}+y_{0}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(y_{n}-y^{\prime}_{n})\dfrac{(y-y_{0})\dotsb(y-y_{n-1})}{(y-y^{\prime}_{1})\dotsb(y-y^{\prime}_{n})}
with
m=n+1
. Check
m=1
:

c_{1}=(x_{1}-x^{\prime}_{0})(f(x_{1})-f(x_{0}))/(x_{1}-x_{0})=(x_{1}-x^{\prime}_{0})Y_{2}(y_{0})/(y_{0}-A=y_{0}-y^{\prime}_{-1}),c_{1}C_{1,0,0}=y_{0}-y^{\prime}_{0}
from (
35
).
∎

6.2.
Exponential-like functions.

Let

\mathcal{D}f=a\mathcal{M}f.

(48)

f(x_{0})
given,
f(x_{n+1})=\dfrac{1+a(x_{n+1}-x_{n})/2}{1-a(x_{n+1}-x_{n})/2}f(x_{n})

on the arithmetic lattice, still an exponential function
\displaystyle f(x)=\left[\dfrac{1+ah/2}{1-ah/2}\right]^{(x-x_{0})/h}f(x_{0})

on the
q-
lattice,
x_{n+1}=qx_{n}
,
\displaystyle f(x)=\dfrac{e_{q}(ax/2)}{e_{q}(-ax/2)}f(x_{0})
(see
[
76
, §1.14]
for
e_{q}(x)
).

We try an expansion

f(x)=\sum_{0}^{\infty}c_{n}\dfrac{(x-x_{0})\dotsb(x-x_{n-1})}{(x-x^{\prime}_{0})\dotsb(x-x^{\prime}_{n-1})},

(49)

with given
c_{0}\neq 0
. Then,
c_{1},c_{2},\dotsc
are found from interpolation
at
x=x_{0},x_{1},\dotsc

c_{1}=\dfrac{(f(x_{1})-f(x_{0}))(x_{1}-x^{\prime}_{0})}{x_{1}-x_{0}}=\dfrac{a(x_{1}-x^{\prime}_{0})}{1-a(x_{1}-x_{0})/2}c_{0}
,

c2=\dfrac{\overbrace{\ \ \ f(x_{2})\ \ \ }^{\frac{1+a(x_{2}-x_{1})/2}{1-a(x_{2}-x_{1})/2}\left(c_{0}+c_{1}\frac{x_{1}-x_{0}}{x_{1}-x^{\prime}_{0}}\right)}-c_{0}-c_{1}(x_{2}-x_{0})/(x_{2}-x^{\prime}_{0})}{(x_{2}-x_{0})(x_{2}-x_{1})/((x_{2}-x^{\prime}_{0})(x_{2}-x^{\prime}_{1}))}=\dotsc

=\dfrac{a(x_{2}-x^{\prime}_{1})(1+a(x_{1}-x^{\prime}_{0})/2)}{(1-a\Delta x_{0}/2)(1-a\Delta x_{1}/2)}c_{0}

Proposition.
If the
x^{\prime}
s are such that
x^{\prime}_{0}-x^{\prime}_{-1}=2/a
,

c_{n}=\dfrac{x_{n}-x^{\prime}_{n-1}}{x_{0}-x^{\prime}_{-1}}\eta_{0}\dotsb\eta_{n-1}c_{0},n=0,1,\dotsc
, where

\eta_{n}=\dfrac{(x_{n}-x^{\prime}_{-1})(1+a(x^{\prime}_{n}-x^{\prime}_{n-1})/2)}{(x^{\prime}_{n}-x^{\prime}_{-1})(1-a(x_{n+1}-x_{n})/2)}

Check
\eta_{0}=\dfrac{(x_{0}-x^{\prime}_{-1})(1+a(x^{\prime}_{0}-x^{\prime}_{-1})/2=2)}{(x^{\prime}_{0}-x^{\prime}_{-1}=2/a)(1-a(x_{1}-x_{0})/2)}

For a simpler way to get further coefficients, we put (
49
) in (
48
) to have

-ac0+\sum_{1}^{\infty}c_{n}C_{n,0,0}(Y_{2}(y)-aD_{n,0,0}(y)/C_{n,0,0})\dfrac{(y-y_{0})\dotsb(y-y_{n-2})}{(y-y^{\prime}_{-1})\dotsb(y-y^{\prime}_{n-1})}\equiv 0
,
from (
34
).

R_{n}(y)=Y_{2}(y)-aD_{n,0,0}(y)/C_{n,0,0}
is a second degree polynomial.

Let also

P_{n}(y)=\dfrac{(y-y_{0})\dotsb(y-y_{n-1})}{(y-y^{\prime}_{0})\dotsb(y-y^{\prime}_{n-1})}
.
One has

-ac_{0}+\displaystyle\sum_{1}^{\infty}c_{n}C_{n}R_{n}(y)\dfrac{P_{n-1}(y)}{(y-y^{\prime}_{-1})(y-y^{\prime}_{n-1})}=0
.

Particular values of
R_{n}
are

R_{n}(y_{-1})=Y_{2}(y_{-1})(1+a(x_{0}-x_{-1})/2)
,

R_{n}(y_{n-1})=Y_{2}(y_{n-1})(1-a(x_{n}-x_{n-1})/2
,

R_{n}(y^{\prime}_{-1})=Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{-1})(1-a(x^{\prime}_{0}-x^{\prime}_{-1})/2
,

R_{n}(y^{\prime}_{n-1})=Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{n-1})(1+a(x^{\prime}_{n}-x^{\prime}_{n-1})/2
,

from (
39a
-
39d
) at
r=s=0
.

A big simplification occurs if
R_{n}(y^{\prime}_{-1})=0
, or
x^{\prime}_{0}-x^{\prime}_{-1}=2/a
which is an equation for
y^{\prime}_{-1}
, as
Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{-1})(x^{\prime}_{0}-x^{\prime}_{-1})
is a square
root of
Y_{1}^{2}(y^{\prime}_{-1})-4Y_{0}(y^{\prime}_{-1})Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{-1})
from (
6
)

So, let
R_{n}(y)=(y-y^{\prime}_{-1})S_{n}(y)
with
S_{n}
of first degree interpolated
at
y_{n-1}
and
y^{\prime}_{n-1}
as

S_{n}(y)=(S_{n}(y^{\prime}_{n-1})(y-y_{n-1})-(S_{n}(y_{n-1})(y-y^{\prime}_{n-1}))/(y^{\prime}_{n-1}-y_{n-1})
,

we have

S_{n}(y^{\prime}_{n-1})=Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{n-1})(1+a(x^{\prime}_{n}-x^{\prime}_{n-1})/2)/(y^{\prime}_{n-1}-y^{\prime}_{-1})
,

S_{n}(y_{n-1})=Y_{2}(y_{n-1})(1-a(x_{n}-x_{n-1})/2)/(y_{n-1}-y^{\prime}_{-1})
,

and

-ac_{0}\displaystyle+\sum_{1}^{\infty}\dfrac{c_{n}C_{n}}{y^{\prime}_{n-1}-y_{n-1}}\dfrac{S_{n}(y^{\prime}_{n-1})(y-y_{n-1})-(S_{n}(y_{n-1})(y-y^{\prime}_{n-1})}{y-y^{\prime}_{n-1}}P_{n-1}(y)\equiv 0
, or

-ac_{0}\displaystyle+\sum_{1}^{\infty}\dfrac{c_{n}C_{n}}{y^{\prime}_{n-1}-y_{n-1}}\{S_{n}(y^{\prime}_{n-1})P_{n}(y)-S_{n}(y_{n-1})P_{n-1}(y)\}\equiv 0
,

finally, by interpolating at
y=y_{0},y_{1},\dotsc
, the coefficients of
P_{0},P_{1};\dotsc

must vanish:

-ac_{0}-c_{1}C_{1}S_{1}(y_{0})/(y^{\prime}_{0}-y_{0})=0
,

\dfrac{c_{n}C_{n}}{y^{\prime}_{n-1}-y_{n-1}}S_{n}(y^{\prime}_{n-1})-\dfrac{c_{n+1}C_{n+1}}{y^{\prime}_{n}-y_{n}}S_{n+1}(y_{n})=0,n=1,2,\dotsc

See also
[
72
,
81
,
135
,
134
]
for interesting constructions and discussions
on factorial expansions.

\dfrac{c_{n+1}}{c_{n}}=\dfrac{C_{n}S_{n}(y^{\prime}_{n-1})(y^{\prime}_{n}-y_{n})}{C_{n+1}S_{n+1}(y_{n})(y^{\prime}_{n-1}-y_{n-1})}=\dfrac{C_{n}(y_{n}-y^{\prime}_{-1})Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{n-1})(1+a(x^{\prime}_{n}-x^{\prime}_{n-1})/2)(y^{\prime}_{n}-y_{n})}{C_{n+1}(y^{\prime}_{n-1}-y^{\prime}_{-1})Y_{2}(y_{n})(1-a(x_{n+1}-x_{n})(y^{\prime}_{n-1}-y_{n-1})}

\dfrac{C_{n}}{C_{n+1}}=\dfrac{(y_{-1}-y_{n-1})(x_{-1}-x^{\prime}_{n})}{(y_{-1}-y^{\prime}_{n})(x_{-1}-x_{n})}=\dfrac{(x^{\prime}_{0}-x^{\prime}_{n})(y^{\prime}_{-1}-y_{n-1})}{(x^{\prime}_{0}-x_{n})(y^{\prime}_{-1}-y^{\prime}_{n})}

from (
38
)

We recall (
7
)
F(x_{m},y_{n})=X_{2}(x_{m})(y_{n}-y_{m-1})(y_{n}-y_{m})=Y_{2}(y_{n})(x_{m}-x_{n})(x_{m}-x_{n+1})
,

and we use the second formula together with the little trick of
multiplying the numerator and the denominator by
y^{\prime}_{n-1}-y_{n}
,
so that

(y_{n-1}-y^{\prime}_{-1})(y_{n}-y^{\prime}_{-1})=Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{-1})(x_{n}-x^{\prime}_{-1})(x_{n}-x^{\prime}_{0})/X_{2}(x_{n}),\\
(y^{\prime}_{n}-y_{n})(y^{\prime}_{n-1}-y_{n})=(x^{\prime}_{n}-x_{n})(x^{\prime}_{n}-x_{n+1})Y_{2}(y_{n})/X_{2}(x^{\prime}_{n})
;

y^{\prime}_{n-1}-y^{\prime}_{-1})(y^{\prime}_{n}-y^{\prime}_{-1})=Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{-1})(x^{\prime}_{n}-x^{\prime}_{-1})(x^{\prime}_{n}-x^{\prime}_{0})/X_{2}(x^{\prime}_{n}),\\
(y^{\prime}_{n-1}-y_{n-1})(y^{\prime}_{n-1}-y_{n})=(x_{n}-x^{\prime}_{n-1})(x_{n}-x^{\prime}_{n})Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{n-1})/X_{2}(x_{n})
,
from (
7
), to have

\dfrac{c_{n+1}}{c_{n}}=\dfrac{(x_{n}-x^{\prime}_{-1})(1+a(x^{\prime}_{n}-x^{\prime}_{n-1})/2)(x_{n+1}-x^{\prime}_{n})}{(x^{\prime}_{n}-x^{\prime}_{-1})(1-a(x_{n+1}-x_{n})/2)(x_{n}-x^{\prime}_{n-1})}

(50)
