7.
Biorthogonal rational functions satisfying differential and difference equations.

7.1.
Laguerre and Hahn theories

Laguerre wrote a number of studies on continued fraction expansion of solutions of
differential equations. His final survey
[
82
]
written shortly before his death in 1886,
deals with the Jacobi continued fraction of a (possibly formal) power expansion about
\infty
:

f(x)=\sum_{1}^{\infty}c_{j}x^{-j}=\dfrac{1}{a_{1}x+b_{1}+\dfrac{1}{a_{2}x+b_{2}+\ddots}},

where the power expansion of the
n^{\text{th}}
approximant of the continued fraction has the
first
2n
terms matching with the expansion of
f
(
Padé
approximation). The
denominator of the
n^{\text{th}}
approximant is also the related
orthogonal polynomial

of degree
n
, see Brezinski’s History
[
21
, §5.2]
, Chihara
[
30
, chap. III, §4]
, Perron
[
109
, §67 (§30 in the 1957 edition)]
, Wall
[
136
, chap. XI]

Laguerre explores functions satisfying the linear differential equation of first order

Uf^{\prime}+Vf=W
with polynomials
U,V,W
, and has remarkable results, including linear
differential equations of second order satisfied by the orthogonal polynomials. In particular,
if the weight function
w
satisfies the homogeneous equation
Uw^{\prime}+Vw=0
on a support
S
,

f(x)=\int_{S}(x-t)^{-1}w(t)dt
has the right properties, and the orthogonal polynomials are
related to
w
on
S
, see also ”semi-classical orthogonal polynomials” by Hendriksen and van Rossum
[
60
]
.

For instance
f(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}(x-t)^{-1}e^{-t}dt
satisfies
xf^{\prime}(x)+xf(x)=1
, with

c_{j}=(j-1)!
, and the orthogonal polynomials are the … Laguerre polynomials, but much
more difficult cases are treated in
[
82
]
, such as an integral involving

\exp(1/x^{2})
on a contour
[
82
, §25-28]
, see Belmehdi and Ronveaux
[
14
,
15
]
for
other examples in modern notation. These difficult examples are NOT classical,they (probably) do not
lead to hypergeometric expansions.

Perron
[
109
, §76 (§43 in the 1957 edition)]
shows the Laguerre theory.

The Laguerre theory will be extended here to any difference calculus based on a biquadratic
polynomial
F
, including the differential limit case, various discretizations,
up to the elliptic lattice. The differential equation for
f
will be expanded to
a discrete
Riccati
equation, a very natural step when dealing with
continued fractions
[
37
,
73
]

W. Hahn
[
53
,
54
,
55
]
looks at the same situation from the more difficult inverse
point of view: what are the orthogonal polynomials satisfying linear differential equations of
fixed order and complexity of coefficients? He finds that the order can be as low as 4 or 2,
recovering the Laguerre theory, whence the name
Laguerre-Hahn
sometimes given to this theory
[
11
,
15
,
88
,
89
,
99
,
116
]
.

See
[
69
,
114
]
for recent surveys.

7.2.
More on biorthogonality, rational interpolation with free poles, orthogonal polynomials, and Padé approximation

7.2.1.
Biorthogonality.

Biorthogonality refers to two sequences of objects in vector spaces, possibly of different kind,
but able to interact through a bilinear form
\langle\ ,\ \rangle
:

\{\mathcal{A}_{n}\}
and
\{\mathcal{B}_{m}\}
are biorthogonal if
\langle\mathcal{A}_{n},\mathcal{B}_{m}\rangle=0,m\neq n
.

Let

\{\mathfrak{a}_{n},\mathfrak{b}_{m}\}
be sequences of independent elements
in the two vector spaces, and we try triangular combinations
\mathcal{A}_{n}=\sum_{0}^{n}\alpha_{j}^{(n)}\mathfrak{a}_{j},\mathcal{B}_{m}=\sum_{0}^{m}\beta_{j}^{(m)}\mathfrak{b}_{j}
.
The required combinations are found from
the triangular factorization of the matrix
[<\mathfrak{a}_{r},\mathfrak{b}_{s}>]
(Brezinski
[
22
]
, Davis
[
39
]
):

\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{0}^{(0)}&0&&&\\
\alpha_{0}^{(1)}&\alpha_{1}^{(1)}&0&&\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&0&\\
\alpha_{0}^{(n)}&\alpha_{1}^{(n)}&\dotsb&\alpha_{n}^{(n)}&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&&&\ddots\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\langle\mathfrak{a}_{0},\mathfrak{b}_{0}\rangle&\langle\mathfrak{a}_{0},\mathfrak{b}_{1}\rangle&\dotsb&\\
\langle\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathfrak{b}_{0}\rangle&\langle\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathfrak{b}_{1}\rangle&\dotsb&\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\\
\langle\mathfrak{a}_{n},\mathfrak{b}_{0}\rangle&\langle\mathfrak{a}_{n},\mathfrak{b}_{1}\rangle&\dotsb&\\
\vdots&\vdots&\\
\vdots&\vdots&\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{0}^{(0)}&\beta_{0}^{(1)}&\dotsb&\beta_{0}^{(m)}&\dotsb\\
0&\beta_{1}^{(1)}&\dotsb&\beta_{1}^{(m)}&\dotsb\\
&0&\ddots&\vdots&\\
&&0&\beta_{m}^{(m)}&\\
&&&0&\ddots\end{bmatrix}

is the diagonal matrix of the
\langle\mathcal{A}_{n},\mathcal{B}_{n}\rangle
s. Existence an unicity hold if
the determinants of the
[\langle\mathfrak{a}_{r},\mathfrak{b}_{s}\rangle]
s, for
r,s=0,\dotsc n
, for all
n
, do not vanish
[
22
,
39
]
.

The triangular factors above are the inverses of the corresponding factors of
the Gauss triangular factorization.

7.2.2.
Biorthogonal rational functions.

We consider a much narrower set made of rational functions,
with
\mathfrak{a}_{n}=1/(x-a_{n}),\mathfrak{b}_{n}=1/(x-b_{n})
.

Here,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
and
\mathcal{B}_{m}

are rational functions

\dfrac{\mathsf{A}_{n}(x)}{(x-a_{0})\dotsb(x-a_{n})}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \dfrac{\mathsf{B}_{m}(x)}{(x-b_{0})\dotsb(x-b_{m})},\ \ n,m=0,1,\dotsc

with
\mathsf{A}_{n}
and
\mathsf{B}_{m}
polynomials of degrees
n
and
m
,
and the bilinear form is actually a linear form
\mathscr{L}
acting on the product, often
given as an integral
\int_{S}f(t)g(t)d\mu(t)
, with some
measure
d\mu
(possibly formal),
\int_{S}\mathcal{A}_{n}(t)\mathcal{B}_{m}(t)d\mu(t)=0,m\neq n
. ¨

From the bilinearity conditions
\mathcal{A}_{n}
is orthogonal to any linear combination of the
\mathcal{B}_{m}
s,
m=0,\dotsc,m-1
, equivalent statements are

(1)

\mathsf{A}_{n}(t)
is orthogonal to all polynomials of degree
<j
with respect to

d\mu(t)/((t-a_{0})\dotsb(t-a_{n})(t-b_{0})\dotsb(t-b_{j-1}))
, for
j=1,\dotsc,n
,

(2)

\mathsf{A}_{n}(t)
is orthogonal to all polynomials of degree
<n
with respect to

d\mu(t)/((t-a_{0})\dotsb(t-a_{n})(t-b_{0})\dotsb(t-b_{n-1}))
.

For
\mathsf{B}_{m}
:

(1)

\mathsf{B}_{m}(t)
is orthogonal to all polynomials of degree
<j
with respect to

d\mu(t)/((t-a_{0})\dotsb(t-a_{j-1})(t-b_{0})\dotsb(t-b_{m}))
, for
j=1,\dotsc,m
,

(2)

\mathsf{B}_{m}(t)
is orthogonal to all polynomials of degree
<m
with respect to

d\mu(t)/((t-a_{0})\dotsb(t-a_{m-1})(t-b_{0})\dotsb(t-b_{m}))
.

This cries for
\mathsf{C}_{n}
of degree
n
orthogonal to all polynomials of smaller
degrees with respect to

d\mu(t)/((t-a_{0})\dotsb(t-a_{n})(t-b_{0})\dotsb(t-b_{n}))
.

Are there biorthogonal functions involving
\mathsf{C}_{n}
?

\begin{split}\mathsf{A}_{n}(t)&=\text{\ a\ constant\ times\ }\dfrac{\mathsf{C}_{n}(b_{n})\mathsf{A}_{n+1}(t)-\mathsf{A}_{n+1}(b_{n})\mathsf{C}_{n}(t)}{t-b_{n}},\\
\mathsf{B}_{m}(t)&=\text{\ a\ constant\ times\ }\dfrac{\mathsf{C}_{m}(a_{n})\mathsf{B}_{m+1}(t)-\mathsf{B}_{m+1}(a_{n})\mathsf{C}_{m}(t)}{t-a_{m}}.\end{split}

(51)

Indeed, we must check that the right-hand side of the first equation is orthogonal to all polynomials
p
of degree
<n
with respect to
d\mu(t)/((t-a_{0}\dotsb(t-a_{n})(t-b_{0})\dotsb(t-b_{n-1}))
. This works nicely with the
\mathsf{C}_{n}(t)
part, thanks to the division by
t-b_{n}
.
As for
\mathsf{A}_{n+1}(t)
, orthogonal to all polynomials of degree
\leqslant n
, including the
(t-a_{n+1})p(t)
polynomials, restoring the expected orthogonality property.

A similar proof holds for
\mathsf{B}_{m}(t)
. ∎

These identities give
\dfrac{\mathsf{C}_{n}(t)}{(t-a_{0})\dotsb(t-a_{n+1})}
and
\dfrac{\mathsf{C}_{n}(t)}{(t-b_{0})\dotsb(t-b_{n+1})}
in terms of
\mathcal{A}_{n},\mathcal{A}_{n+1},\mathcal{B}_{n},\mathcal{B}_{n+1}
, but this is perhaps not useful.

A similar identity for
\mathsf{C}_{n}
will be needed

\mathsf{C}_{n}(t)=\text{\ const.\ }\dfrac{\mathsf{A}_{n+1}(a_{n+1})\mathsf{C}_{n+1}(t)-\mathsf{C}_{n+1}(a_{n+1})\mathsf{A}_{n+1}(t)}{t-a_{n+1}}.

(52)

We check the orthogonality of the right-hand side to all polynomials
p
of degree
<n
with respect to
d\mu(t)/((t-a_{0})\dotsb(t-a_{n})(t-b_{0})\dotsb(t-b_{n}))
. This is easy for the
\mathsf{A}_{n+1}(t)
part thanks to the division by
t-a_{n+1}
. And
\mathsf{C}_{n+1}(t)
is orthogonal to all polynomials of degree
\leqslant n
, including the
(t-b_{n+1})p(t)
polynomials.

Remark that the identities just found are the
Christoffel-Darboux
equations for orthogonal polynomials
[
30
,
65
,
77
]
etc. See §
7.2.4
for more on orthogonal polynomials.

n=0:\mathcal{A}_{0}(x)=1/(x-a_{0}),\mathcal{B}_{0}(x)=1/(x-b_{0}),\mathsf{A}_{0}=\mathsf{B}_{0}=\mathsf{C}_{0}=1
.

n=1:\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
\alpha_{0}^{(1)}&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\int_{S}d\mu(t)/((t-a_{0})(t-b_{0}))&\int_{S}d\mu(t)/((t-a_{0})(t-b_{1}))\\
\int_{S}d\mu(t)/((t-a_{1})(t-b_{0}))&\int_{S}d\mu(t)/((t-a_{1})(t-b_{1}))\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&\beta_{0}^{(1)}\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\\
=\begin{bmatrix}\int_{S}d\mu(t)/((t-a_{0})(t-b_{0}))&0\\
0&\int_{S}d\mu(t)/((t-a_{1})(t-b_{1}))-\alpha_{0}^{(1)}\beta_{0}^{(1)}\int_{S}d\mu(t)/((t-a_{0})(t-b_{0}))\end{bmatrix}
,

with
\alpha_{0}^{(1)}=-\dfrac{\int_{S}d\mu(t)/((t-a_{1})(t-b_{0}))}{\int_{S}d\mu(t)/((t-a_{0})(t-b_{0}))},\beta_{0}^{(1)}=-\dfrac{\int_{S}d\mu(t)/((t-a_{0})(t-b_{1}))}{\int_{S}d\mu(t)/((t-a_{0})(t-b_{0}))}
.

So,
\mathcal{A}_{1}(x)=\dfrac{\alpha_{0}^{(1)}}{x-a_{0}}+\dfrac{1}{x-a_{1}}=\dfrac{\mathsf{A}_{1}(x)=(1+\alpha_{0}^{(1)})x-a_{0}-\alpha_{0}^{(1)}a_{1}}{(x-a_{0})(x-a_{1})}\\
=\dfrac{\displaystyle\int_{S}d\mu(t)\left(\dfrac{(a_{1}-a_{0})(x-t)}{(t-a_{0})(t-a_{1})(t-b_{0})}\right)}{(x-a_{0})(x-a_{1})\int_{S}d\mu(t)/((t-a_{0})(t-b_{0}))}

It figures:
x-c
is orthogonal to the constants with respect to some measure if
c
is the integral of
t
with respect to the same measure. Also,
\mathsf{C}_{1}(x)
is a constant times the integral of

(x-t)d\mu(t)/((t-a_{0})(t-a_{1})(t-b_{0})(t-b_{1}))
.

Early items: Rahman in 1981
[
111
]
considered
\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle=\mathscr{L}(fg)=\int_{S=\mathbb{Z}}f(t)g(t)d\mu(t)=\sum_{-\infty}^{\infty}w_{j}f(j)g(j),
where
w_{j}=\dfrac{\Gamma(j+c+c^{\prime})\Gamma(j+d+d^{\prime})}{\Gamma(j+c)\Gamma(j+d)}
for
j\in\mathbb{Z}
and with
c^{\prime}+d^{\prime}<-1
(so that
w_{j}
is bounded by const.
|j|^{c^{\prime}+d^{\prime}}
for large
|j|
). The rational functions
\mathcal{A}_{n}
and
\mathcal{B}_{m}
have poles at
-c,-c-1,\dotsc,-c-n
and
-d,-d-1,\dotsc,-d-m
, and are given as
{}_{3}F_{2}
hypergeometric expansions, there is also a
{}_{4}F_{3}
in the same paper. See also Rahman and Suslov
[
112
]
for
q-
lattices, and
Spiridonov and Zhedanov on true elliptic lattices,
[
123
,
124
,
125
]
, also Rosengren
[
118
]
.

7.2.3.
Rational interpolation with free poles.

We encountered in §
5.1
rational interpolation through expansions of products

c_{n}\frac{(x-x_{0})\dotsb(x-x_{n-1})}{(x-x^{\prime}_{0})\dotsb(x-x^{\prime}_{n-1})}
. The
values of the sum of the
m
first terms at
x=x_{0},\dotsc,x_{m}
will not change
with the next sums, so that this
m^{\text{th}}
sum is a rational interpolant
of degree
m
with
m
imposed poles
x^{\prime}_{0},\dotsc,x^{\prime}_{m-1}
. We consider now rational
interpolants with unknown denominators.

Proposition.

Let
f(z)=\mathscr{L}(1/(x-z))
or, in the integral setting
f(z)=\int_{S}d\mu(x)/(x-z)
, and let
w_{m,n}(t)=1/((t-a_{0})\dotsc(t-a_{m})(t-b_{0})\dotsc(t-b_{n})
,
the
numerators

\mathsf{A}_{m},\mathsf{B}_{m},\mathsf{C}_{m}
of particular biorthogonal rational functions, fulfilling, as see in §
7.2.2
above:

(1)

\mathsf{A}_{m}
orthogonal to all polynomials of degree
<m
with respect to
w_{m,m-1}(t)d\mu(t)
,

(2)

\mathsf{B}_{m}
orthogonal to all polynomials of degree
<m
with respect to
w_{m-1,m}(t)d\mu(t)
,

(3)

\mathsf{C}_{m}
orthogonal to all polynomials of degree
<m
with respect to
w_{m,m}(t)d\mu(t)
,

then,

\mathsf{A}_{m},\mathsf{B}_{m},\mathsf{C}_{m}
are the
denominators
of rational functions

(1)

\widetilde{\mathsf{A}}_{m}/\mathsf{A}_{m}
of degrees
m
and
m
interpolating
f
at
a_{0},\dotsc,a_{m},b_{0},\dotsc,b_{m-1}
,

(2)

\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{m}/\mathsf{B}_{m}
of degrees
m
and
m
interpolating
f
at
a_{0},\dotsc,a_{m-1},b_{0},\dotsc,b_{m}
,

(3)

\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{m}/\mathsf{C}_{m}
of degrees
m+1
and
m
interpolating
f
at
a_{0},\dotsc,a_{m},b_{0},\dotsc,b_{m}

Proof. First, note that
\mathsf{A}_{m}(z)f(z)=\int_{S}\mathsf{A}_{m}(t)d\mu(t)/(t-z)+
a polynomial of degree
<m
. Indeed,
\int_{S}\frac{\mathsf{A}_{m}(z)-\mathsf{A}_{m}(t)}{t-z}d\mu(t)
is a polynomial of degree
\leqslant m-1
in
z
.

We perform now successive polynomial interpolation of
\mathsf{A}_{m}f
at
a_{0},a_{1},\dotsc
, adding
terms of the appropriate Newton expansion
[a_{0},\dotsc,a_{j}]_{\mathsf{A}_{m}f}(z-a_{0})\dotsb(z-a_{j-1})
, where
[a_{0},\dotsc,a_{j}]_{\mathsf{A}_{m}f}
is the divided difference of
\mathsf{A}_{m}f
. The divided difference
of the part involving
\mathsf{A}_{m}(t)d\mu(t)/(t-z)
is

\int_{S}\mathsf{A}_{m}(t)d\mu(t)/((t-a_{0})\dotsb(t-a_{j}))
,
as seen in §
5.1
.

After
a_{m}
and interpolation of degree
m
, we have

[a_{0},\dotsc,a_{m},b_{0},\dotsc,b_{j}]_{\mathsf{A}_{m}f}(z-a_{0})\dotsb(z-a_{m})(z-b_{0})\dotsb(z-b_{j-1})
terms, the divided difference is now the integral of
\mathsf{A}_{m}(t)w_{m,j}(t)d\mu(t)=\mathsf{A}_{m}(t)w_{m,m-1}(t)p(t)d\mu(t)
, where
p(t)=(t-b_{j+1})\dotsb(t-b_{m-1})
has degree
m-j-1<m
, for
j=0,\dotsc,m-1
, so that these divided differences vanish thanks to
the orthogonality properties of
\mathsf{A}_{m}
. The polynomial of degree
\leqslant m-1
in
z
of above is no more involved here. The remainder starts with the first nonvanishing
divided difference times
(z-a_{0})\dotsb(z-a_{m})(z-b_{0})\dotsb(z-b_{m-1})
.

The proofs for
\mathsf{B}_{m}
and
\mathsf{C}_{m}
are similar. ∎

So,
\dfrac{\widetilde{\mathsf{A}}_{0}(x)}{\mathsf{A}_{0}(x)}=\dfrac{f(a_{0})}{1};\dfrac{\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{0}(x)}{\mathsf{C}_{0}(x)}=\dfrac{(b_{0}-a_{0})^{-1}[(x-a_{0})f(b_{0})-(x-b_{0})f(a_{0})]}{1};\\
\dfrac{\widetilde{A}_{1}(x)}{A_{1}(x)}=f(a_{0})+\dfrac{(f(a_{1})-f(a_{0}))(x-a_{0})}{(1+\alpha_{0}^{(1)})(x-a_{1})+a_{1}-a_{0}}
. Check the interpolation at
x=b_{0}
:

\mathsf{A}_{1}(b_{0})=(1+\alpha_{0}^{(1)})(b_{0}-a_{1})+a_{1}-a_{0}=\left(1-\dfrac{\int_{S}d\mu(t)/((t-a_{1})(t-b_{0}))}{\int_{S}d\mu(t)/((t-a_{0})(t-b_{0}))}\right)(b_{0}-a_{1})+a_{1}-a_{0}=\left(1-\dfrac{(b_{0}-a_{0})(f(b_{0})-f(a_{1}))}{(b_{0}-a_{1})(f(b_{0})-f(a_{0}))}\right)(b_{0}-a_{1})+a_{1}-a_{0}=\dfrac{(b_{0}-a_{0})(f(a_{1})-f(a_{0}))}{f(b_{0})-f(a_{0})}
.

Recurrence relations
for the
\mathsf{A}
s and the
\mathsf{C}
s.

Proposition.
Let the biorthogonal rational functions
\mathcal{A}_{m}
and
\mathcal{B}_{m}
be uniquely defined up to multiplication by constants of the numerators, then
there is a choice of these numerators, and coefficients
r_{0},r_{1},\dotsc
such that

\mathsf{A}_{m+1}(x)=\mathsf{C}_{m}(x)+r_{2m}(x-b_{m})\mathsf{A}_{m}(x),\widetilde{\mathsf{A}}_{m+1}(x)=\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{m}(x)+r_{2m}(x-b_{m})\widetilde{\mathsf{A}}_{m}(x),

(53a)

\mathsf{C}_{m+1}(x)=\mathsf{A}_{m+1}(x)+r_{2m+1}(x-a_{m+1})\mathsf{C}_{m}(x),\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{m+1}(x)=\widetilde{\mathsf{A}}_{m+1}(x)+r_{2m+1}(x-a_{m+1})\widetilde{\mathsf{A}}_{m}(x).

(53b)

Indeed, we performed polynomial interpolation of
\mathsf{A}_{m}f
at the
2m+2
points
a_{0},b_{0},\dotsc,a_{m},b_{m}
resulting in
\widetilde{\mathsf{A}}_{m}
of degree
m

followed by the first nonvanishing term involving
(x-a_{0})(x-b_{0})\dotsb(x-a_{m})(x-b_{m-1})
.

\mathsf{A}_{m}(x)f(x)-\widetilde{\mathsf{A}}_{m}(x)=\gamma_{m}(x-a_{0})\dotsb(x-a_{m})(x-b_{0})\dotsb(x-b_{m-1})+\dotsb
,

\mathsf{C}_{m}(x)f(x)-\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{m}(x)=\delta_{m}(x-a_{0})\dotsb(x-a_{m})(x-b_{0})\dotsb(x-b_{m})+\dotsb
,

so that the polynomials
X_{m}(x)=\mathsf{C}_{m}(x)+r_{2m}(x-b_{m})\mathsf{A}_{m}(x)
and
\widetilde{X}(x)=\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{m}(x)+r_{2m}(x-b_{m})\widetilde{\mathsf{A}}_{m}(x)
are polynomials of
degrees
m+1
achieving interpolation at
a_{0},\dotsc,a_{m},b_{0},\dotsc,b_{m}
when
r_{2m}=-\delta_{m}/\gamma_{m}
, see also the first equation of (
51
).

\mathsf{C}_{m}(x)f(x)-\widetilde{\mathsf{C}}_{m}(x)=\delta_{m}(x-a_{0})\dotsb(x-a_{m})(x-b_{0})\dotsb(x-b_{m})+\dotsb
,

\mathsf{A}_{m+1}(x)f(x)-\widetilde{\mathsf{A}}_{m+1}(x)=\gamma_{m+1}(x-a_{0})\dotsb(x-a_{m+1})(x-b_{0})\dotsb(x-b_{m})+\dotsb
,

proceeds in the same way, see also (
52
).

Let
P_{2n}
be a new definition of
\mathsf{A}_{n}
through multiplication by another constant, and the same with
P_{2n+1}
and
\mathsf{C}_{n}
, for
n=0,1,...
, so,

P_{n+1}(x)=P_{n}(x)+r_{n}\left(\left\{\begin{matrix}(x-a_{n/2})\text{\ if\ }n\text{\ is\ even}\\
(x-b_{(n-1)/2})\text{\ if\ }n\text{\ is\ odd}\end{matrix}\right.\right)P_{n-1}(x),n=0,1,\dotsc,P_{-1}=0
Ismail & Masson
[
66
, §3]
, Spiridonov & Zhedanov
[
123
,
124
]
,

f(x)-f(a_{0})=\dfrac{r_{0}(x-a_{0})}{1+\dfrac{r_{1}(x-b_{0})}{1+\dfrac{r_{2}(x-a_{1})}{1+\dfrac{r_{3}(x-b_{1})}{1+\ddots}}}}
Multipoint Padé: Goncar, López,
Zhedanov
[
47
,
85
,
86
,
145
]

With
N_{0},N_{1},\dotsc
the corresponding numerators, and
x_{2j}=a_{j},x_{2j+1}=b_{j},j=0,1,\dotsc

\dfrac{N_{0}}{P_{0}}=\dfrac{f(a_{0})}{1},\dfrac{N_{1}(x)}{P_{1}(x)}=\dfrac{f(a_{0})+r_{0}(x-x_{0})}{1},\dfrac{N_{2}(x)}{P_{2}(x)}=f(a_{0})+\dfrac{r_{0}(x-x_{0})}{1+r_{1}(x-x_{1})}
,

with
x_{0}=a_{0},x_{1}=b_{0},\dotsc,r_{0}=\frac{f(x_{1})}{x_{1}-x_{0}}
,

\dfrac{r_{0}(x-x_{0})}{1+\dfrac{r_{1}(x-x_{1})}{1+\dfrac{r_{2}(x-x_{2})}{1}}}=\dfrac{r_{0}(x-x_{0})[1+r_{2}(x-x_{2})]}{1+r_{1}(x-x_{1})+r_{2}(x-x_{2})}

Thiele
[
128
, §40]
builds rational interpolants to a function
f
at

x_{0},x_{1},\dotsc
as successive approximants of the continued fraction

f(x)-f(x_{0})=\dfrac{x-x_{0}}{\delta_{0}+\dfrac{x-x_{1}}{\delta_{1}+\ddots}}
, where
\delta_{0},\delta_{1},\dotsc

are related to
f(x_{0}),f(x_{1}),\dotsc
by a particular algorithm (
reciprocal differences

algorithm:
\delta_{0}=(x_{1}-x_{0})/(f(x_{1})-f(x_{0}))
, etc). If the relevant determinants and the
\delta-
coefficients do not vanish,
the
m^{\text{th}}
approximant of the continued fraction above is the unique
interpolation at
x_{0},\dotsc,x_{m}
of
f
by a polynomial of degree
n_{m}=
integer part of
(m+1)/2
over a polynomial of degree
d_{m}=
integer part of
m/2
.

See
[
28
,
29
,
43
]
, Peter Wynn’s
\rho-
algorithm
[
23
, §2.5.3]
,
see also
[
49
,
51
,
101
,
139
,
140
,
146
]
for existence, unicity, and numerical stability discussions.

Let
f(x_{0})=f(a_{0})=0
. Consider now the continued fraction

f(x)=\dfrac{r_{0}(x-x_{0})}{1+\dfrac{r_{1}(x-x_{1})}{1+\ddots}}

(54)

equivalent to Thiele’s fraction (
\delta_{0}=1/r_{0},\delta_{1}=r_{0}/r_{1},\delta_{2}=r_{1}/(r_{0}r_{2})
, etc.)

Reminding that
f(x_{0})=0
, the first steps are

\dfrac{N_{0}}{P_{0}}=\dfrac{0}{1},\dfrac{N_{1}(x)}{P_{1}(x)}=\dfrac{r_{0}(x-x_{0})}{1},\dfrac{N_{2}(x)}{P_{2}(x)}=\dfrac{r_{0}(x-x_{0})}{1+r_{1}(x-x_{1})}
,
with
r_{0}=\frac{f(x_{1})}{x_{1}-x_{0}}
,

r_{1}=\frac{1}{x_{2}-x_{1}}\left(\frac{(x_{2}-x_{0})f(x_{1})}{(x_{1}-x_{0})f(x_{2})}-1\right)=\frac{(x_{2}-x_{0})[f(x_{1})/(x_{1}-x_{0})-f(x_{2})/(x_{2}-x_{0})]}{(x_{2}-x_{1})f(x_{2})}

etc.,
and
N_{m}/P_{m}
interpolates at
x_{0},\dotsc,x_{m}
, but
N_{m}
and
P_{m}
are
polynomials of degrees
n_{m}
and
d_{m}
, the integer parts of
(m+1)/2
and
m/2
.

\begin{bmatrix}N_{m}(x)&N_{m+1}(x)\\
P_{m}(x)&P_{m+1}(x)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}N_{m-1}(x)&N_{m}(x)\\
P_{m-1}(x)&P_{m}(x)\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0&r_{m}(x-x_{m})\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}

(55)

A convenient prequel is
N_{-1}=1,P_{-1}=0
.

Casorati determinant: let
\mathfrak{C}_{m}(x)=N_{m-1}(x)P_{m}(x)-P_{m-1}(x)N_{m}(x)
,

then

\mathfrak{C}_{m+1}(x)=-r_{m}(x-x_{m})\mathfrak{C}_{m}(x)
,

\mathfrak{C}_{m}(x)=(-1)^{m}r_{0}\dotsb r_{m-1}(x-x_{0})\dotsb(x-x_{m-1})

(56)

f(x)=\dfrac{r_{0}(x-x_{0})}{1+\dfrac{\ddots}{1+\dfrac{r_{m-1}(x-x_{m-1})}{1+f_{m}(x)}}}=\dfrac{N_{m}(x)+N_{m-1}(x)f_{m}(x))}{P_{m}(x)+P_{m-1}(x)f_{m}(x)}
,

where
f_{0}=f
, and

f_{m}(x)=\dfrac{r_{m}(x-x_{m})}{1+\dfrac{r_{m+1}(x-x_{m+1})}{1+\ddots}}.

(57)

A very basic continued fraction identity (Perron etc. ) check the

m\to m+1
step:

\dfrac{N_{m}(x)+N_{m-1}(x)r_{m}(x-x_{m})/(1+f_{m+1}(x)))}{P_{m}(x)+P_{m-1}(x)r_{m}(x-x_{m})/(1+f_{m+1}(x)))}=\dfrac{N_{m+1}(x)+N_{m}(x)f_{m+1}(x)}{P_{m+1}(x)+P_{m}(x)f_{m+1}(x)}

Contraction:

P_{2m+2}(x)=\left(1+r_{2m}(x-x_{2m})+r_{2m+1}(x-x_{2m+1})\right)P_{2m}(x)-r_{2m-1}r_{2m}(x-x_{2m-1})(x-x_{2m})P_{2m-2}(x),

(58)

type II of Ismail & Masson
[
13
,
66
]
.

Koekoek et al
[
76
]
often give
xp_{n}(x)=p_{n+1}(x)+(A_{n}+C_{n})p_{n}(x)+A_{n-1}C_{n}p_{n-1}(x)
, we recognize
(A_{n},C_{n})
basically as
(r_{2m},r_{2m+1})

The
R_{I}
fractions of
[
66
]
are NOT
(
54
) but continued fractions based on recurence relations

X_{m+1}=(x-\alpha_{m})X_{m}-\lambda_{m}(x-x_{m})X_{m-1}
.

7.2.4.
Orthogonal polynomials.

Let all the
a_{n}
s and
b_{n}
s be a single value
x^{*}
,
we have from (
58
)
P_{2m+2}(x)/(x-x^{*})^{m+1}=(1/(x-x^{*})+r_{2m}+r_{2m+1})P_{2m}(x)/(x-x^{*})^{m}-r_{2m-1}r_{2m}P_{2m-2}(x)/(x-x^{*})^{m-1}
, so that
P_{2m}(x)/(x-x^{*})^{m}
is a
polynomial of degree
m
in
1/(x-x^{*})
satisfying the three-term recurrence relations of
orthogonal polynomials

(plain polynomials if
x^{*}=\infty
). See also
[
59
]
.

Let
z=1/(x-x^{*})
, (
54
) becomes

\dfrac{r_{0}/z}{1+\dfrac{r_{1}/z}{1+\dfrac{r2/z}{1+\dfrac{r3/z}{1+\ddots}}}}=\dfrac{r_{0}}{z+\dfrac{r_{1}}{1+\dfrac{r2}{z+\dfrac{r3}{1+\ddots}}}},

(59)

a
SITZ^{-1}
fraction
[
61
, §12.8]

[
109
, §67, 1957: §33]
, or a formal
S-
fraction. The true
S-
fractions, so called after Stieltjes, have
r_{j}>0

[
61
, §12.8]

[
136
, §28]
.

7.2.5.

Knuth
[
75
, §4.5.3, answers of §4.5.3, 16]

is delighted to report how the 24-year old Euler found the
r_{m}
s of the
expansion
f(x)=e^{x}-1=r_{0}x/(1+r_{1}x/(1+\ddots))
from
f^{\prime}(x)=f(x)+1
and
f(x)=r_{0}x/(1+f_{1}(x))\Rightarrow r_{0}=1
and
1/(1+f_{1}(x))-xf^{\prime}_{1}(x)/(1+f_{1}(x))^{2}=1+x/(1+f_{1}(x))
or
xf^{\prime}_{1}(x)=-f_{1}^{2}(x)-(1+x)f_{1}(x)-x
,
a
Riccati
differential equation, and that equations of the same complexity hold for

f_{2},f_{3}
, etc. see §
7.3.6
, see also Khovanskii
[
73
, chap.II, §9]
, Cretney
[
37
]
for the
role of Daniel Bernoulli.

7.2.6.
Linear case, the Pearson’s equation

.

Let
f
satisfy a
linear
first order difference equation on
\{x_{n},y_{n}\}
from a biquadratic
F(x,y)=0
,

A\mathcal{D}f=C\mathcal{M}f+D

(60)

(why replace
B
and
C
of (
45
) by
C
and
D
? A more general Riccati equation with the mysterious
B
will be needed later in (
65a
)),
and introduce a discrete measure
\{w_{j}\}
so that

f(x)=\sum_{j_{1}}^{j_{2}}w_{j}/(x^{\prime}_{j}-x)
. What are the residues
w_{j}
s if we want
(
60
) to hold with polynomial
A,C,D
?

At some
y=y_{n}
,

\displaystyle A(y_{n})(\mathcal{D}f)(y_{n})-C(y_{n})(\mathcal{M}f)(y_{n})\\
=\sum_{j=j_{1}}^{j_{2}}w_{j}\dfrac{A(y_{n})-C(y_{n})\left(x^{\prime}_{j}-(x_{n}+x_{n+1})/2\right)}{\underbrace{(x^{\prime}_{j}-x_{n})(x^{\prime}_{j}-x_{n+1})}_{\displaystyle F(x^{\prime}_{j},y_{n})/Y_{2}(y_{n})=(y_{n}-y^{\prime}_{j})(y_{n}-y^{\prime}_{j-1})X_{2}(x^{\prime}_{j})/Y_{2}(y_{n})}}

is a rational function of
y_{n}
(as
x_{n}+x_{n+1}
is, remind (
6
)) , it must be the polynomial
D(y_{n})
, so have vanishing residues at
y=y^{\prime}_{j_{1}-1},\dotsc,y^{\prime}_{j_{2}}
:

\begin{split}D(y)&=A(y)(\mathcal{D}f)(y)-C(y)(\mathcal{M}f)(y)\\
=\sum_{j=j_{1}-1}^{j_{2}}&\dfrac{w_{j+1}\dfrac{A(y)Y_{2}(y)-C(y)\left(x^{\prime}_{j+1}Y_{2}(y)+Y_{1}(y)/2\right)}{(y^{\prime}_{j}-y^{\prime}_{j+1})X_{2}(x^{\prime}_{j+1})}+w_{j}\dfrac{A(y)Y_{2}(y)-C(y)\left(x^{\prime}_{j}Y_{2}(y)+Y_{1}(y)/2\right)}{(y^{\prime}_{j}-y^{\prime}_{j-1})X_{2}(x^{\prime}_{j})}}{y-y^{\prime}_{j}},\end{split}

(61)

where
w_{j_{1}-1}=w_{j_{2}+1}=0
are defined. We now put
y=y^{\prime}_{j}
to appreciate
residues, seeing that
x^{\prime}_{j+1}Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{j})+Y_{1}(y^{\prime}_{j})/2=(x^{\prime}_{j+1}-x^{\prime}_{j})Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{j})/2,x^{\prime}_{j}Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{j})+Y_{1}(y^{\prime}_{j})/2=-(x^{\prime}_{j+1}-x^{\prime}_{j})Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{j})/2
, and the condition on
w_{j}
and

w_{j+1}
is

\dfrac{w_{j+1}}{(y^{\prime}_{j+1}-y^{\prime}_{j})X_{2}(x^{\prime}_{j+1})}\left(\dfrac{A(y^{\prime}_{j})}{x^{\prime}_{j+1}-x^{\prime}_{j}}-\dfrac{C(y^{\prime}_{j})}{2}\right)=\dfrac{w_{j}}{(y^{\prime}_{j}-y^{\prime}_{j-1})X_{2}(x^{\prime}_{j})}\left(\dfrac{A(y^{\prime}_{j})}{x^{\prime}_{j+1}-x^{\prime}_{j}}+\dfrac{C(y^{\prime}_{j})}{2}\right),j=j_{1},\dotsc,j_{2}-1,

(62)

make the
Pearson’s equations
for the weights
w_{j}
.

Romanovsky and Hildebrandt related Pearson’s equations for special probability weights
to orthogonal polynomials
5
5

5

The reference
[
62
]
was given by A. Ronveaux.

[
62
]

Remark that if
w_{j}
is considered as a function of
x^{\prime}_{j}
, (
62
) is

(A\mathcal{D}-C\mathcal{M})\dfrac{w}{X_{2}\Delta y}=0,

(63)

the homogeneous part of the
equation for
f
, and (
61
) is rewritten as
D(y)=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=j_{1}-1}^{j_{2}}(x^{\prime}_{j+1}-x^{\prime}_{j})\dfrac{\left[C(y)\left((x^{\prime}_{j}+x^{\prime}_{j+1})Y_{2}(y)+Y_{1}(y)\right)/2-A(y)Y_{2}(y)\right]\mathcal{D}\dfrac{w_{j}}{X_{2}\Delta y}+C(y)Y_{2}(y)\mathcal{M}\dfrac{w_{j}}{X_{2}\Delta y}}{y-y^{\prime}_{j}}

\begin{split}D(y)&=C(y)\sum_{j=j_{1}-1}^{j_{2}}(x^{\prime}_{j+1}-x^{\prime}_{j})\dfrac{\left((x^{\prime}_{j}+x^{\prime}_{j+1})(Y_{2}(y)-Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{j}))+Y_{1}(y)-Y_{1}(y^{\prime}_{j})\right)\mathcal{D}\dfrac{w_{j}}{X_{2}\Delta y}}{2(y-y^{\prime}_{j})}\\
&+Y_{2}(y)\sum_{j=j_{1}-1}^{j_{2}}(x^{\prime}_{j+1}-x^{\prime}_{j})\dfrac{(A(y^{\prime}_{j})-A(y))\mathcal{D}\dfrac{w_{j}}{X_{2}\Delta y}+(C(y)-C(y^{\prime}_{j}))\mathcal{M}\dfrac{w_{j}}{X_{2}\Delta y}}{y-y^{\prime}_{j}}\end{split}

(64)

7.2.7.
Remark.

From now on,
\{x_{n},y_{n}\}
and
\{x^{\prime}_{n},y^{\prime}_{n}\}
are successive points on a given biquadratic curve
F(x,y)=0
. There are no more independent sequences
\{a_{n}\},\{b_{n}\}
, and the interpolation set
a_{0},\dotsc,a_{m},b_{0},\dotsc,b_{m-1}

of §
7.2.3

is
x_{0},\dotsc,x_{2m}
. Then,
\mathsf{A}_{m}
is called
P_{2m}
here, together with
\mathsf{C}_{m}=P_{2m+1}
.

7.2.8.
The Hahn measure.

Let us return to the simplest discrete
lattice
\{x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},\dotsc\}
defined by
F(x,y)=(x-y)(x+h-y)
,
so that
x_{j}=x_{0}+jh,y_{j}=x_{0}+(j+1)h,x^{\prime}_{j}=x^{\prime}_{0}+jh,y^{\prime}_{j}=x^{\prime}_{0}+(j+1)h
,

Y_{2}(y)\equiv 1,Y_{1}(y)=h-2y,Y_{0}(y)=y(y-h),Q(y)\equiv h^{2},X_{2}(x)\equiv 1
.

Let
w_{j}=\dfrac{(\beta+1)\dotsb(\beta+j)(\alpha+1)\dotsb(\alpha+N-j-1)}{j!(N-1-j)!}=(-1)^{N-1}\binom{-\beta-1}{j}\binom{-\alpha-1}{N-j-1}
,
on
x^{\prime}_{0},\dotsc,x^{\prime}_{N-1}
.
[
76
, §9.5]

f(x)=\sum_{j_{1}}^{j_{2}}w_{j}/(x^{\prime}_{j}-x)=\mu_{0}/x+\mu_{1}/x^{2}+\dotsb
,

\mu_{0}=-\sum_{0}^{N-1}w_{j}=-(\alpha+\beta+2)\dotsb(\alpha+\beta+N)/(N-1)!,

[
76
, 9.5.2]
,
[
104
, 2.5.4]
, at
n=0
,

\mu_{1}=-\sum_{0}^{N-1}x^{\prime}_{j}w_{j}=x^{\prime}_{0}\mu_{0}-h(\beta+1)(\alpha+\beta+3)\dotsb(\alpha+\beta+N)/(N-2)!,

See that
w_{-1}=w_{N}=0
, and
(j+1)(\alpha+N-j-1)w_{j+1}=(\beta+j+1)(N-1-j)w_{j}
.
[
104
, §2.4.6]
which is our Pearson equation also

(j+1)(\alpha+N-j-1)\left[\dfrac{w_{j}+w_{j+1}}{2}+h\dfrac{w_{j+1}-w_{j}}{2h}\right]=(\beta+j+1)(N-1-j)\left[\dfrac{w_{j}+w_{j+1}}{2}-h\dfrac{w_{j+1}-w_{j}}{2h}\right]
.

so, we have
A(y)\mathcal{D}w(y)=C(y)\mathcal{M}w(y)
at
y=y^{\prime}_{0}+jh
,

A(y)=h[((y-y^{\prime}_{0})/h+1)(\alpha+N-(y-y^{\prime}_{0})/h-1)+(\beta+(y-y^{\prime}_{0})/h+1)(N-1-(y-y^{\prime}_{0})/h)]/2
,

C(y)=(\beta+(y-y^{\prime}_{0})/h+1)(N-1-(y-y^{\prime}_{0})/h)-((y-y^{\prime}_{0})/h+1)(\alpha+N-(y-y^{\prime}_{0})/h-1)=-(\alpha+\beta)((y-y^{\prime}_{0})/h+1)+N\beta
,
with
A(y)\mathcal{D}f(y)=C(y)\mathcal{M}f(y)+D(y)
,

D(y)
is given by (
64
), using here

\sum_{j=-1}^{N-1}(w_{j+1}-w_{j})=w_{N}-w_{-1}=0
,
and as
A(y)=-y^{2}/h+\dotsb
has degree 2, and
C(y)=-(\alpha+\beta)y/h+\dotsb
, as well as
f(x)=\mu_{0}/x+O(1/x^{2})
when
x
(and
y
)
\to\infty
, the constant
D(y)=(\alpha+\beta+1)\mu_{0}
remains.

7.3.
Riccati difference equation for
f

7.3.1.

Theorem.
Let
\{(x_{n},y_{n})\}
,
\{(x_{n+1},y_{n})\}
be points
on the biquadratic curve
F(x,y)=Y_{0}(y)+xY_{1}(y)+x^{2}Y_{2}(y)=0
, and
f
a function
defined on the
x_{n}
s, satisfying the
discrete Riccati
difference equation
at some
y=y_{n}
,

A(y_{n})(\mathcal{D}f)(y_{n})=B(y_{n})f(x_{n})f(x_{n+1})+C(y_{n})(\mathcal{M}f)(y_{n})+D(y_{n}),

(65a)

or, showing how
f(x_{n+1})
is related to
f(x_{n})
:

f(x_{n+1})=\dfrac{\left[\dfrac{A(y_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}+\dfrac{C(y_{n})}{2}\right]f(x_{n})+D(y_{n})}{-B(y_{n})f(x_{n})+\dfrac{A(y_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}-\dfrac{C(y_{n})}{2}},

(65b)

and suppose we also have

\dfrac{A(y_{-1})}{x_{0}-x_{-1}}+\dfrac{C(y_{-1})}{2}=0,\ \ \ D(y_{-1})=0,

(66)

showing that
f(x_{0})
does NOT depend on the value of
f(x_{-1})
, that is, that

x_{0}
is a
singular
point of the Riccati equation. The value of
f(x_{0})
happens to be 0.

In (
65a
),
A,\dotsc,D
are polynomials. If needed, these polynomials are multiplied
by a common factor so that
Y_{2}
is a factor of
B,C
, and
D
. Let
d
be then
the largest degree of
A,\dotsc,D
.

Let us consider now at some
y=y_{n}
,

A_{m}(y_{n})(\mathcal{D}f_{m})(y_{n})=B_{m}(y_{n})f_{m}(x_{n})f_{m}(x_{n+1})+C_{m}(y_{n})(\mathcal{M}f_{m})(y_{n})+D_{m}(y_{n}),\ \ m=0,1,\dotsc,

(67)

with
f_{0}=f,A_{0}=A,
etc., and

f_{m}(x)=\dfrac{r_{m}(x-x_{m})}{1+f_{m+1}(x)}
, as seen in (
57
).

Then,

(1)

With the rational functions
\Upsilon_{m}(y)=Y_{2}(y)/(y-y_{m-1})
if
m
is even;
1/(y-y_{m-1})
if
m
is odd,

\begin{split}A_{m+1}&=\Upsilon_{m}r_{m}\left[\dfrac{Y_{1}/2+x_{m}Y_{2}}{Y_{2}}A_{m}+\dfrac{Q}{4Y_{2}^{2}}C_{m}\right],\end{split}

(68a)

B_{m+1}=\Upsilon_{m}D_{m},

(68b)

C_{m+1}=\Upsilon_{m}\left[-r_{m}A_{m}+2D_{m}-r_{m}\dfrac{Y_{1}/2+x_{m}Y_{2}}{Y_{2}}C_{m}\right],

(68c)

D_{m+1}(y)=\Upsilon_{m}(y)\left[B_{m}(y)r_{m}^{2}\dfrac{F(x_{m},y)}{Y_{2}(y)}-r_{m}A_{m}(y)-r_{m}\dfrac{Y_{1}/2+x_{m}Y_{2}}{Y_{2}(y)}C_{m}(y)+D_{m}(y)\right]\\
=C_{m+1}(y)+\Upsilon_{m}(y)\left[B_{m}(y)r_{m}^{2}\dfrac{F(x_{m},y)}{Y_{2}(y)}-D_{m}(y)\right]

(68d)

(2)

The degrees of
A_{m},
etc. are
\leqslant d+1
,

(3)

Y_{2}
is a factor of
B_{m},C_{m}
, and
D_{m}
,

(4)

x_{m}
is a singular point of (
67
):

\dfrac{A_{m}(y_{m-1})}{x_{m}-x_{m-1}}+\dfrac{C_{m}(y_{m-1})}{2}=0,D_{m}(y_{m-1})=0,

(68e)

Sometimes
\widetilde{A}_{m}=A_{m}+\dfrac{Y_{1}/2+x_{m}Y_{2}}{Y_{2}}C_{m}
is preferred,
then,

A_{m+1}=\Upsilon_{m}r_{m}\left[\dfrac{Y_{1}/2+x_{m}Y_{2}}{Y_{2}}A_{m}+\dfrac{Q}{4Y_{2}^{2}}C_{m}\right]=\Upsilon_{m}r_{m}\left[\dfrac{Y_{1}/2+x_{m}Y_{2}}{Y_{2}}\widetilde{A}_{m}-\dfrac{F(x_{m},y)}{Y_{2}}C_{m}\right],

(69a)

\widetilde{A}_{m+1}=\Upsilon_{m}r_{m}\left[\dfrac{Y_{1}/2+x_{m}Y_{2}}{Y_{2}}\widetilde{A}_{m}-\dfrac{F(x_{m},y)}{Y_{2}}C_{m}+\dfrac{Y_{1}/2+x_{m+1}Y_{2}}{Y_{2}}\left[-\widetilde{A}_{m}+2D_{m}/r_{m}\right]\right]
, or

\widetilde{A}_{m+1}=\Upsilon_{m}r_{m}\left[(x_{m}-x_{m+1})\widetilde{A}_{m}-\dfrac{F(x_{m},y)}{Y_{2}}C_{m}+\dfrac{Y_{1}+2x_{m+1}Y_{2}}{r_{m}Y_{2}}D_{m}\right].

(69b)

7.3.2.

Proof.
We have (
67
) at
m=0
to begin with,
if (
67
) holds at some
m
, we adapt (
65b
) at this level
m
showing
how
f_{m}(x_{n+1})
is related to
f_{m}(x_{n})
:

f_{m}(x_{n+1})=\dfrac{\left[\dfrac{A_{m}(y_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}+\dfrac{C_{m}(y_{n})}{2}\right]f_{m}(x_{n})+D_{m}(y_{n})}{-B_{m}(y_{n})f_{m}(x_{n})+\dfrac{A_{m}(y_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}-\dfrac{C_{m}(y_{n})}{2}}.

(70)

Let

U_{m}(y_{n})=\dfrac{C_{m}(y_{n})}{2}+\dfrac{A_{m}(y_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}},V_{m}(y_{n})=\dfrac{C_{m}(y_{n})}{2}-\dfrac{A_{m}(y_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}
,

and we
perform the step
m\to m+1
by entering
f_{m}(x)=\dfrac{r_{m}(x-x_{m})}{1+f_{m+1}(x)}

in (
70
):

\dfrac{r_{m}(x_{n+1}-x_{m})}{1+f_{m+1}(x_{n+1})}=-\dfrac{U_{m}(y_{n})\dfrac{r_{m}(x_{n}-x_{m})}{1+f_{m+1}(x_{n})}+D_{m}(y_{n})}{B_{m}(y_{n})\dfrac{r_{m}(x_{n}-x_{m})}{1+f_{m+1}(x_{n})}+V_{m}(y_{n})}

and isolate

f_{m+1}(x_{n+1})=-1-r_{m}(x_{n+1}-x_{m})\dfrac{B_{m}(y_{n})r_{m}(x_{n}-x_{m})+V_{m}(y_{n})\left[1+f_{m+1}(x_{n})\right]}{U_{m}(y_{n})r_{m}(x_{n}-x_{m})+D_{m}(y_{n})\left[1+f_{m+1}(x_{n})\right]}

=-\dfrac{\left[r_{m}(x_{n+1}-x_{m})V_{m}(y_{n})+D_{m}(y_{n})\right]f_{m+1}(x_{n})+X}{D_{m}(y_{n})f_{m+1}(y_{n})+r_{m}(x_{n}-x_{m})U_{m}(y_{n})+D_{m}(y_{n})}

with
X=B_{m}(y_{n})r_{m}^{2}(x_{n}-x_{m})(x_{n+1}-x_{m})+r_{m}(x_{n}-x_{m})U_{m}(y_{n})+r_{m}(x_{n+1}-x_{m})V_{m}(y_{n})+D_{m}(y_{n})

must be
-\dfrac{U_{m+1}(y_{n})f_{m+1}(x_{n})+D_{m+1}(y_{n})}{B_{m+1}(y_{n})f_{m+1}(x_{n})+V_{m+1}(y_{n})}
, whence

\begin{split}U_{m+1}(y_{n})&=\Upsilon_{m}(y_{n})\left[r_{m}(x_{n+1}-x_{m})V_{m}(y_{n})+D_{m}(y_{n})\right]\\
D_{m+1}(y_{n})&\ \\
=\Upsilon_{m}(y_{n})&\left[B_{m}(y_{n})r_{m}^{2}(x_{n}-x_{m})(x_{n+1}-x_{m})+r_{m}(x_{n}-x_{m})U_{m}(y_{n})+r_{m}(x_{n+1}-x_{m})V_{m}(y_{n})+D_{m}(y_{n})\right]\\
B_{m+1}(y_{n})&=\Upsilon_{m}(y_{n})D_{m}(y_{n})\\
V_{m+1}(y_{n})&=\Upsilon_{m}(y_{n})\left[r_{m}(x_{n}-x_{m})U_{m}(y_{n})+D_{m}(y_{n})\right]\end{split}

(71)

We need only now the
x
s and
y
s to be on a biquadratic curve, by (
6
), (
9
),

x_{n}+x_{n+1}=-Y_{1}(y_{n})/Y_{2}(y_{n})
and
(x_{n+1}-x_{n})^{2}=Q(y_{n})/Y_{2}^{2}(y_{n})
are rational functions of
y_{n}
. We also have

F(x_{m},y_{n})=(x_{n}-x_{m})(x_{n+1}-x_{m})Y_{2}(y_{n})=X_{2}(x_{m})(y_{n}-y_{m})(y_{n}-y_{m-1})
,

We get (
68a
)-(
68d
) through
A_{m}(y_{n})/(x_{n+1}-x_{n})=(U_{m}(y_{n})-V_{m}(y_{n}))/2,C_{m}(y_{n})=U_{m}(y_{n})+V_{m}(y_{n})
,

\dfrac{C_{m+1}(y_{n})}{2}\pm\dfrac{A_{m+1}(y_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}=\Upsilon_{m}(y_{n})\left[\left(\left\{\begin{matrix}r_{m}(x_{n+1}-x_{m})\\
r_{m}(x_{n}-x_{m})\end{matrix}\right)\left[\dfrac{C_{m}(y_{n})}{2}\mp\dfrac{A_{m}(y_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}\right]+D_{m}(y_{n})\right)\right],

(72)

\Upsilon_{m}
is not yet known, it must be such that
A_{m+1}
etc. are polynomials,

and, from
f_{m+1}(x_{m+1})=0,f_{m}(x_{m+1})=r_{m}(x_{m+1}-x_{m})
, so,

r_{m}=\dfrac{D_{m}(y_{m})}{A_{m}(y_{m})-(x_{m+1}-x_{m})C_{m}(y_{m})/2}.

(73)

In the Padé case
F(x,y)=(x-y)^{2},x_{m}=0
, the condition is
f_{m}(x)\sim r_{m}x
near the origin, and the Riccati equation gives
r_{m}=D_{m}(0)/A_{m}(0)
as in (
73
), but if
A_{m}(0)=0
,

D_{m}(0)
vanishes too,
0=A_{m}(y)f^{\prime}_{m}(x)-B_{m}(y)f^{2}_{m}(x)-C_{m}(y)f_{m}(x)D_{m}(y)=A^{\prime}_{m}(0)r_{m}y-C_{m}(0)r_{m}y-D^{\prime}_{m}(0)y+O(y^{2})
, so,

r_{m}=\dfrac{D^{\prime}_{m}(0)}{A^{\prime}_{m}(0)-C_{m}(0)}.

(74)

Matrix form:

\dfrac{1}{\Upsilon_{m}(y_{n})}\begin{bmatrix}U_{m+1}&B_{m+1}\\
D_{m+1}&V_{m+1}\end{bmatrix}=\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}D_{m}&V_{m}\\
V_{m+1}/\Upsilon_{m}(y_{n})&r_{m}(x_{n}-x_{0})B_{m}+V_{m}\end{bmatrix}}_{\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
r_{m}(x_{n}-x_{0})&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}U_{m}&B_{m}\\
D_{m}&V_{m}\end{bmatrix}}\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
r_{m}(x_{n+1}-x_{0})&0\end{bmatrix}
, or

\begin{bmatrix}\dfrac{C_{m+1}(y_{n})}{2}+\dfrac{A_{m+1}(y_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}&B_{m+1}(y_{n})\\
D_{m+1}(y_{n})&\dfrac{C_{m+1}(y_{n})}{2}-\dfrac{A_{m+1}(y_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}\end{bmatrix}\\
=\Upsilon_{m}(y_{n})\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
r_{m}(x_{n}-x_{m})&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\dfrac{C_{m}(y_{n})}{2}+\dfrac{A_{m}(y_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}&B_{m}(y_{n})\\
D_{m}(y_{n})&\dfrac{C_{m}(y_{n})}{2}-\dfrac{A_{m}(y_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
r_{m}(x_{n+1}-x_{m})&0\end{bmatrix}

(75)

Note the determinant of (
75
):
\dfrac{C^{2}_{m+1}(y_{n})}{4}-\dfrac{A^{2}_{m+1}(y_{n})}{(x_{n+1}-x_{n})^{2}}-B_{m+1}(y_{n})D_{m+1}(y_{n})\\
=\Upsilon^{2}_{m}(y_{n})\underbrace{(x_{n}-x_{m})(x_{n+1}-x_{m})}_{F(x_{m},y_{n})=X_{2}(x_{m})(y_{n}-y_{m-1})(y_{n}-y_{m})}\left[\dfrac{C^{2}_{m}(y_{n})}{4}-\dfrac{A_{m}^{2}(y_{n})}{(x_{n+1}-x_{n})^{2}}-B_{m}(y_{n})D_{m}(y_{n})\right]

\dfrac{C^{2}_{m}}{4}-\dfrac{A^{2}_{m}}{Q/Y_{2}^{2}}-B_{m}D_{m}=\Upsilon^{2}_{0}\dotsb\Upsilon^{2}_{m-1}\dfrac{X_{2}(x_{0})\dotsb X_{2}(x_{m-1})(y-y_{-1})(y-y_{0})^{2}\dotsb(y-y_{m-2})^{2}(y-y_{m-1})}{X_{2}(x^{\prime}_{0})\dotsb X_{2}(x^{\prime}_{m-1})(y-y^{\prime}_{-1})(y-y^{\prime}_{0})^{2}\dotsb(y-y^{\prime}_{m-2})^{2}(y-y^{\prime}_{m-1})}\\
\times\left[\dfrac{C^{2}_{0}}{4}-\dfrac{A^{2}_{0}}{Q/Y_{2}^{2}}-B_{0}D_{0}\right]

We will also need the contraction of two steps in one

\begin{bmatrix}\dfrac{C_{m+2}(y_{n})}{2}+\dfrac{A_{m+2}(y_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}&B_{m+2}(y_{n})\\
D_{m+2}(y_{n})&\dfrac{C_{m+2}(y_{n})}{2}-\dfrac{A_{m+2}(y_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}\end{bmatrix}=\Upsilon_{m}(y_{n})\Upsilon_{m+1}(y_{n})\begin{bmatrix}r_{m}(x_{n}-x_{m})&1\\
r_{m}(x_{n}-x_{m})&(x_{n}-x_{m+1})r_{m+1}+1\end{bmatrix}\\
\times\begin{bmatrix}\dfrac{C_{m}(y_{n})}{2}+\dfrac{A_{m}(y_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}&B_{m}(y_{n})\\
D_{m}(y_{n})&\dfrac{C_{m}(y_{n})}{2}-\dfrac{A_{m}(y_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}(x_{n+1}-x_{m+1})r_{m+1}+1&1\\
r_{m}(x_{n+1}-x_{m})&r_{m}(x_{n+1}-x_{m})\end{bmatrix}

(76)

We proceed now with the proof, supposing (
68e
) at level
m

(1)

y-y_{m-1}
is a common factor to the right-hand sides of (
68a
)-(
68d
)
before the multiplication by
\Upsilon
: the part in square brackets of (
77a
) has

\widetilde{A}_{m}
vanishing at
y=y_{m-1}
, and so does
F(x_{m},y)
; obvious for the
B_{m+1}
line, as
D_{m}
does the job; and the same for
C_{m+1}
using
\widetilde{A}_{m}
and
D_{m}
in (
68c
); finally,
\widetilde{A}_{m}
,
F(x_{m},y)
and
D_{m}
do the job in (
68d
).

Proof that (
68e
) still holds at
m+1
:

From (
73
),

r_{m}=\dfrac{D_{m}(y_{m})}{A_{m}(y_{m})-(x_{m+1}-x_{m})C_{m}(y_{m})/2=\widetilde{A}_{m}(y_{m})}
,
and (
69b
),
\widetilde{A}_{m+1}(y_{m})=\Upsilon_{m}(y_{m})r_{m}\left[(x_{m}-x_{m+1})\widetilde{A}_{m}(y_{m})-\dfrac{F(x_{m},y_{m})}{Y_{2}}C_{m}(y_{m})\right.\\
\left.+\dfrac{Y_{1}(y_{m})+2x_{m+1}Y_{2}(y_{m})=(x_{m+1}-x_{m})Y_{2}}{r_{m}Y_{2}}D_{m}(y_{m})\right]=0
,

and (
68d
),
D_{m+1}(y_{m})=\Upsilon_{m}(y_{m})\left[B_{m}(y_{m})r_{m}^{2}\dfrac{F(x_{m},y_{m})}{Y_{2}(y_{m})}-r_{m}\widetilde{A}_{m}(y_{m})+D_{m}(y_{m})\right]=0
.

A much shorter, perhaps too short proof:

from
0=f_{m}(x_{m})=r_{m}(x_{m}-x_{m}=0)/(1+f_{m+1}(x_{m}))
,
f_{m+1}(x_{m})=0/0
is indefinite,
and
f_{m+1}(x_{m+1})=0
for any
f_{m+1}(x_{m})
, (
68e
) follows at
m+1
.

(2)

The degrees of
A_{m}
etc.
\leqslant d
when
m
is even, and
Y_{2}
being a factor of
\Upsilon_{m}
,
A_{m+1}
etc. are of course polynomials (of degrees
\leqslant d+1
),
and
Y_{2}
is a factor of
B_{m+1},C_{m+1},D_{m+1}
.

Are
A_{m+2}
etc. then still polynomials?
We look at (
76
) leading to

\begin{split}A_{m+2}&=\Upsilon_{m}\Upsilon_{m+1}r_{m+1}\left[r_{m}\dfrac{Y_{0}+(x_{m}+x_{m+1})Y_{1}/2+x_{m}x_{m+1}Y_{2}}{Y_{2}}A_{m}+\dfrac{Q}{4Y_{2}^{2}}[r_{m}(x_{m+1}-x_{m})C_{m}+2D_{m}]\right],\end{split}

(77a)

B_{m+2}=-\Upsilon_{m}\Upsilon_{m+1}r_{m}\left[A_{m}-\dfrac{F(x_{m},y)r_{m}B_{m}-(Y_{1}/2+x_{m})C_{m}}{Y_{2}}-D_{m}/r_{m}\right],

(77b)

C_{m+2}=\Upsilon_{m}\Upsilon_{m+1}r_{m}\left[[(x_{m+1}-x_{m})r_{m+1}-2]A_{m}+2r_{m}\dfrac{F(x_{m},y)}{Y_{2}}B_{m}\right.\\
\left.+\dfrac{r_{m+1}[Y_{0}+(x_{m}+x_{m+1})Y_{1}/2+x_{m}x_{m+1}Y_{2}]-Y1-2x_{m}Y_{2}}{Y_{2}}C_{m}-\dfrac{(Y_{1}+2x_{m+1}Y_{2})r_{m+1}-2Y_{2}}{Y_{2}}D_{m}/r_{m}\right],

(77c)

D_{m+2}=\Upsilon_{m}\Upsilon_{m+1}r_{m}\left[((x_{m+1}-x_{m})r_{m+1}-1)A_{m}+r_{m}\dfrac{F(x_{m},y)}{Y_{2}}B_{m}\right.\\
\left.+\dfrac{[Y_{0}+(x_{m}+x_{m+1})Y_{1}/2+x_{m}x_{m+1}Y_{2}]r_{m+1}-Y_{1}/2-x_{m}Y_{2}}{Y_{2}}C_{m}\right.\\
\left.+r_{m+1}\dfrac{r_{m+1}F(x_{m+1},y)-Y_{1}-2x_{m+1}Y_{2}+Y_{2}/r_{m+1}}{Y_{2}}D_{m}/r_{m}\right]\\
=C_{m+2}(y)+\Upsilon_{m}\Upsilon_{m+1}\left[r_{m}A_{m}-r_{m}^{2}\dfrac{F(x_{m},y)}{Y_{2}}B_{m}+r_{m}\dfrac{Y_{1}/2+x_{m}Y_{2}}{Y_{2}}C_{m}-\dfrac{r_{m+1}^{2}F(x_{m+1},y)+Y_{2}}{Y_{2}}D_{m}\right]

(77d)

and use
\Upsilon_{m}(y)\Upsilon_{m+1}(y)=\dfrac{Y_{2}(y)}{(y-y_{m-1})(y-y_{m})}
.

∎

7.3.3.
Consequences on the interpolant
N_{m}/P_{m}
.

A(y_{n})\left(\mathcal{D}\dfrac{N_{m}}{P_{m}}\right)(y_{n})-B(y_{n})\dfrac{N_{m}(x_{n})N_{m}(x_{n+1})}{P_{m}(x_{n})P_{m}(x_{n+1})}-C(y_{n})\left(\mathcal{M}\dfrac{N_{m}}{P_{m}}f\right)(y_{n})-D(y_{n})

is a rational function of
y_{n}
vanishing when
n
is such that
N_{m}/P_{m}
interpolates
f
at
x_{n}

and
x_{n+1}
, so for
n=0,\dotsc,m-1
, and we have after multiplication by
P_{m}(x_{n})P_{m}(x_{n+1})
,

A(y_{n})\dfrac{N_{m}(x_{n+1})P_{m}(x_{n})-N_{m}(x_{m})P_{m}(x_{n+1}}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}-B(y_{n})N_{m}(x_{n})N_{m}(x_{n+1})\\
-C(y_{n})\dfrac{N_{m}(x_{n+1})P_{m}(x_{n})+N_{m}(x_{m})P_{m}(x_{n+1}}{2}-D(y_{n})P_{m}(x_{n})P_{m}(x_{n+1})\\
=\Theta_{m}(y_{n})(y_{n}-y_{0})\dotsb(y_{n}-y_{m-1})

(78)

(almost Laguerre’s notation!
[
82
]
) where
\Theta_{m}
is a polynomial whose degree
is not larger than the degrees of
A,B,
etc.

Casorati:
A(y_{n})\dfrac{P_{m+1}(x_{n+1})N_{m}(x_{n+1})P_{m}(x_{n})-P_{m+1}(x_{n+1})N_{m}(x_{m})P_{m}(x_{n+1})-P_{m}(x_{n+1})N_{m+1}(x_{n+1})P_{m+1}(x_{n})+P_{m}(x_{n+1})N_{m+1}(x_{n})P_{m+1}(x_{n+1})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}-B(y_{n})N_{m}(x_{n})N_{m}(x_{n+1})-C(y_{n})\dfrac{N_{m}(x_{n+1})P_{m}(x_{n})+N_{m}(x_{m})P_{m}(x_{n+1}}{2}-D(y_{n})P_{m}(x_{n})P_{m}(x_{n+1})\\
=\Theta_{m}(y_{n})(y_{n}-y_{0})\dotsb(y_{n}-y_{m-1})

f(x)=\dfrac{r_{0}(x-x_{0})}{1+\dfrac{\ddots}{1+\dfrac{r_{m-1}(x-x_{m-1})}{1+f_{m}(x)}}}=\dfrac{N_{m}(x)+N_{m-1}(x)f_{m}(x)}{P_{m}(x)+P_{m-1}(x)f_{m}(x)}
,

where
f_{0}=f
, and
f_{m}(x)=\dfrac{r_{m}(x-x_{m})}{1+\dfrac{r_{m+1}(x-x_{m+1})}{1+\ddots}}
.

A very basic continued fraction identity (Perron etc. ) check the

m\to m+1
step:

\dfrac{N_{m}(x)+N_{m-1}(x)r_{m}(x-x_{m})/(1+f_{m+1}(x)))}{P_{m}(x)+P_{m-1}(x)r_{m}(x-x_{m})/(1+f_{m+1}(x)))}=\dfrac{N_{m+1}(x)+N_{m}(x)f_{m+1}(x)}{P_{m+1}(x)+P_{m}(x)f_{m+1}(x)}

7.3.4.
Orthogonal polynomials.

As seen in §
7.2.4
,
if all the
x_{n}
are themselves a fixed point
x^{*}
(possible only in the zerogenus case, see §
3.4
),
P_{2m}(x)/(x-x^{*})^{m}
is a polynomial of degree
m
in
1/(x-x^{*})
.

Things become trickier when
x^{*}=\infty
, we then replace in continued fractions and recurrence relations
r_{m}(x-x^{*})
vanishing at
x=x^{*}
, by
r_{m}/x
vanishing when
x\to\infty
.

Note first that
f(x)\sim r/x
when
x\to\infty\Rightarrow\mathcal{D}f\sim-\dfrac{r}{x_{n}x_{n+1}}=-\dfrac{rY_{2}(y^{*})}{Y_{0}(y^{*})},\mathcal{M}f\sim r\left(\dfrac{1}{x_{n}}+\dfrac{1}{x_{n+1}}\right)/2=-\dfrac{rY_{1}(y^{*})}{2Y_{0}(y^{*})},0=A\mathcal{D}f-Bf(x_{n})f(x_{n+1})-C\mathcal{M}f-D\sim\dfrac{-rA(y^{*})Y_{2}(y^{*})-r^{2}B(y^{*})Y_{2}(y^{*})+rY_{1}(y^{*})C(y^{*})/2}{Y_{0}(y^{*})}-D(y^{*})
.

As
y^{*}
is a root of
Y_{2}(y)=0
, an equation for
r
remains, solved by
r=\dfrac{2Y_{0}(y^{*})D(y^{*})}{Y_{1}(y^{*})C(y^{*})}
.

It may happen that
y^{*}=\infty
, then,
r=\lim_{y\to\infty}\dfrac{Y_{0}(y)D(y)}{-A(y)Y_{2}(y)+Y_{1}(y)C(y)/2}
if degree
(B)<
degree
(A)
.

We now work the equations for
A_{m}
etc. in the new situation, as in
[
89
, §3]
, with the present notations, enter
f_{m}(x)=\dfrac{r_{m}/x}{1+f_{m+1}(x)}
in (
70
),
and one adapts (
68a
-
68d
) as

\begin{split}A_{m+1}(y_{n})&=(x_{n+1}-x_{n})(U_{m+1}(y_{n})-V_{m+1}(y_{n}))/2\\
=\Upsilon_{m}(y_{n})r_{m}&\left[-\left(Y_{0}(y_{n})\left(\dfrac{1}{x_{n}}+\dfrac{1}{x_{n+1}}\right)=-Y_{1}(y_{n})\right)A_{m}(y_{n})/2\right.\\
\left.-\left(Y_{0}(y_{n})\left(\dfrac{(x_{n+1}-x_{n})^{2}}{x_{n}x_{n+1}}\right)=\dfrac{Q(y_{n})}{4Y_{2}(y_{n}}\right)C_{m}(y_{n})\right],\end{split}

(79a)

B_{m+1}=\Upsilon_{m}Y_{0}D_{m},

(79b)

C_{m+1}(y_{n})=U_{m+1}(y_{n})+V_{m+1}(y_{n})\\
=\Upsilon_{m}(y_{n})\left[r_{m}\left(Y_{0}(y_{n})\left(\dfrac{1/x_{n}-1/x_{n+1}}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}\right)=Y_{2}(y_{n})\right)A_{m}(y_{n})\right.\\
\left.+r_{m}\left(Y_{0}(y_{n})\left(\dfrac{1}{x_{n}}+\dfrac{1}{x_{n+1}}\right)=-Y_{1}(y_{n})\right)C_{m}(y_{n})/2+2Y_{0}(y_{n})D_{m}(y_{n})\right],

(79c)

D_{m+1}(y)=\Upsilon_{m}(y)\left[B_{m}(y)r_{m}^{2}Y_{2}(y)+r_{m}Y_{2}(y)A_{m}(y)-r_{m}Y_{1}(y)C_{m}(y)/2+Y_{0}(y)D_{m}(y)\right]\\
=C_{m+1}(y)+\Upsilon_{m}(y)\left[B_{m}(y)r_{m}^{2}Y_{2}(y)-Y_{0}(y)D_{m}(y)\right]

(79d)

We now look for simple cases.

7.3.5.
Hermite and Freud polynomials.

Let
f(x)=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\dfrac{t^{\alpha}\exp(-t^{s})dt}{x^{-1}-t}\sim\mu_{0}x+\mu_{1}x^{2}+\dotsb
(the series is divergent asymptotic,
\mu_{p}=s^{-1}\Gamma((\alpha+p+1)/s),\mu_{p+s}=((\alpha+p+1)/s)\mu_{p}
), which
satisfies
x^{s+1}f^{\prime}(x)=-s(\mu_{0}x+\dotsb+\mu_{s-1}x^{s})+(s-\alpha x^{s})f(x)
.
Here,
x_{m}=x^{*}=0
,
F(x,y)=(x-y)^{2},Y_{1}(y)=-2y,Q(y)\equiv 0
,

(1)

When
s=1
and
\alpha=-1/2
,
\Upsilon_{m}(y)=-1/(r_{m}y),A_{m}(y)=y^{2},C_{m}(y)=1+y/2,D_{m}(y)=-r_{m}y
, and, when
m>0,B_{m}=1,r_{m}=-m/2
, the latter from (
74
). We then recover the Hermite polynomials, actually,
P_{m}(y)=(-iy^{1/2}/2)^{m}H_{m}(iy^{-1/2})
.

[
3
, 22.5.38-41]
. Other cases will not always be so easy.

(2)

When
s=2
and
\alpha=-1/2
, there is no elementary closed form. In terms of the
r_{m}
s:
\Upsilon_{m}(y)=-1/(r_{m}y),A_{m}(y)=y^{3}
, and, when
m>0,B_{m}=2-(m/(2r_{m})-r_{m+1})y,C_{m}(y)=2-4r_{m}y+y^{2}/2,D_{m}(y)=-2r_{m}y(1-(r_{m}+r_{m+1})y)
, with
r_{1}(r_{1}+r_{2})=1/4,r_{m}(r_{m-1}+r_{m}+r_{m+1})=m/4,m=2,3,\dotsc
, the simplest Freud or discrete Painlevé equations

[
15
,
34
,
45
,
91
,
132
]
.

7.3.6.
Exponential function: end of §
7.2.5

Euler’s example of the exponential function:
f(x)=f_{0}(x)=e^{x}-1
, we consider the same
F
as before,

f^{\prime}=f+1,x_{m}-y_{m}=0,x_{n}=x_{n+1}=x,f(x)=x/(1+f_{1}(x))\Rightarrow xf^{\prime}_{1}=-f_{1}^{2}-(1+x)f_{1}-x,xf^{\prime}_{2}=-2f_{2}^{2}+(x-2)f_{2}+x/2,\dotsc,\Upsilon=1/x,A_{m}(x)=x,B_{m}=-m,C_{m}(x)=(-1)^{m}x-m,D_{m}(x)=(m+1)r_{m}x,r_{m}=(-1)^{m}/(2m+1+(-1)^{m}),m=1,2,\dotsc

[
38
, 11.1.3]
.

Same function interpolated at
0,h,2h,\dotsc
:
F(x,t)=(y-x)(y-x-h),x_{n}=nh,y_{n}=(n+1)h,(f(x+h)-f(x))/h=a((f(x)+f(x+h))/2+1)
, with
f(x_{0})=0,a=(2/h)\tanh(h/2)
if we want

f(x)=e^{x-x_{0}}-1
.

A_{0}(y)=y-y_{-1}=y-x_{0},B_{0}(y)\equiv 0,C_{0}(y)=D_{0}(y)=a(y-y_{-1})
, as we must have (
66
),
D_{0}(y_{-1})=0,r_{0}=\dfrac{a}{1-ah/2}
.

Then,
\Upsilon_{m}(y)=1/(y-x_{m}),A_{2m}(y)=y-x_{0}-mh,B_{2m}=-2m,C_{2m}(y)=a(y-x_{0})-2m(1+ah),D_{2m}(y)=a((1+ah/2)(y-x_{0})-2mh)/2,r_{2m}=a(1+ah/2)/(4m+2-ah);A_{2m+1}(y)=y-x_{0}-(m+1/2)h-ah^{2}/4,B_{2m+1}=ah/2-2m-1,C_{2m+1}(y)=-a(y-x0)-(2m+1)(1-ah)+ah/2,D_{2m+1}(y)=a((m+1)(1-ah/2)(y-x_{0}-(2m+1)h)/(ah/2-2m-1),r_{2m+1}=-a(1-ah/2)/(4m+2-ah)
.
Rational interpolations to the exponential function have been given by Nörlund
[
105
, §243]
, by A. Iserles
[
64
, Thm 4]

looking for numerical solvers of differential equations. Milne-Thomson
[
102
, §5.9]
uses confluent
interpolation
x_{0}=x_{1}=\dotsb=0
and recovers Padé approximation to the exponential function.

See also

(1+\alpha)^{x}=1+\dfrac{\alpha x}{1+\dfrac{\alpha(1-x)}{2+\dfrac{\alpha(1+x)}{3+\dfrac{\alpha(2-x)}{\ddots+\dfrac{\alpha(k\pm x)}{a_{k}+\ddots}}}}}

(80)

a_{k}=2
or
2k+1
. Already in Thiele (1909)!

7.3.7.
The Hahn function.

We recall §
7.2.8

F(x,y)=(x-y)(x+h-y),Y_{2}(y)\equiv 1,Y_{1}(y)=h-2y,Y_{0}(y)=y(y-h),Q(y)\equiv h^{2},X_{2}(x)\equiv 1
and
f(x)=\sum_{0}^{N-1}w_{j}/(x^{\prime}_{j}-x)
with

w_{j}=\dfrac{(\beta+1)\dotsb(\beta+j)(\alpha+1)\dotsb(\alpha+N-j-1)}{j!(N-1-j)!}=(-1)^{N-1}\binom{-\beta-1}{j}\binom{-\alpha-1}{N-j-1}
,

on
x^{\prime}_{0},x^{\prime}_{1}=x^{\prime}_{0}+h,\dotsc,x^{\prime}_{N-1}

satisfying
(j+1)(\alpha+N-j-1)w_{j+1}=(\beta+j+1)(N-1-j)w_{j}
.
The
polynomials
A,C,D
have been given in §
7.2.8
, we also need

U(y)=C(y)/2+A(y)/h=(\beta+j+1)(N-1-j)=-(y-y^{\prime}_{-1}+\beta h)(y-y^{\prime}_{-1}-Nh)/h^{2},\\
V(y)=C(y)/2-A(y)/h=-(j+1)(\alpha+N-j-1)=(y-y^{\prime}_{-1})(y-y^{\prime}_{-1}-\alpha h-Nh)/h^{2}

Here are two
(A_{m},B_{m},C_{m},D_{m})
different solutions corresponding to two different
rational approximations to the
same
function
f
:

1.
Orthogonal polynomials.

We expand
f
about
\infty
as
f(x)=\mu_{0}/x+\mu_{1}/x^{2}+\dotsc=\dfrac{r_{0}/x}{1+\dfrac{r_{1}/x}{1+\dfrac{r_{2}/x}{1+\ddots}}}=\dfrac{r_{0}}{x+\dfrac{r_{1}}{1+\dfrac{r_{2}}{x+\ddots}}}

and get the Hahn orthogonal polynomials
[
76
, §9.5]

[
65
, §6.2]
,

r_{0}=\mu_{0}=-(\alpha+\beta+2)\dotsb(\alpha+\beta+N)/(N-1)!,

r_{1}=-\mu_{1}/\mu_{0}=-x^{\prime}_{0}-h(N-1)(\beta+1)/(\alpha+\beta+2)
.

With
x^{\prime}_{0}=0
(and
y^{\prime}_{0}=h
),
A_{0}(y)=h[y(\alpha+N-y/h)/h+(\beta+y/h)(N-y/h)]/2,C_{0}(y)=(\beta+y/h)(N-y/h)-y(\alpha+N-y/h)/h=-(\alpha+\beta)y/h+N\beta,D_{0}=(\alpha+\beta+1)\mu_{0}/h

With
\Upsilon_{m}(y)
replaced by
y(y-h)\Upsilon_{m}(y)
,
(
79a
)-(
79d
) become

A_{m+1}(y)=\Upsilon_{m}(y)\left[r_{m}[(h-2y)A_{m}(y)/2-h^{2}C_{m}(y)/4\right],B_{m+1}=y(y-h)\Upsilon_{m}(y)D_{m}(y),\\
C_{m+1}(y)=\Upsilon_{m}(y_{n})\left[r_{m}A_{m}(y)+r_{m}(2y-h)C_{m}(y)/2+2y(y-h)D_{m}(y)\right],\\
D_{m+1}(y)=\Upsilon_{m}(y)\left[B_{m}(y)r_{m}^{2}+r_{m}A_{m}(y)+r_{m}(2y-h)C_{m}(y)/2+y(y-h)D_{m}(y)\right]=C_{m+1}(y)+\Upsilon_{m}(y)\left[B_{m}(y)r_{m}^{2}-y(y-h)D_{m}(y)\right]
, solved at
m>0
by

\Upsilon_{2m}(y)=\dfrac{1}{r_{2m}y},r_{2m}=-\dfrac{2mh(\alpha+m)(\alpha+\beta+N+m)}{(\alpha+\beta+2m)(\alpha+\beta+2m+1)},\\
V_{2m}(y)=y(y-(\alpha+N+m)h)/h^{2},B_{2m}(y)=y(N-m)(\beta+m),\\
C_{2m}(y)=-(\alpha+\beta+2m)y/h+m(\alpha+\beta+m)-\beta N,D_{2m}=-\dfrac{m(\alpha+m)(\alpha+\beta+N+m)}{\alpha+\beta+2m}
;

\Upsilon_{2m-1}(y)=\dfrac{1}{r_{2m-1}(y-h)},r_{2m-1}=-\dfrac{(N-m)h(\beta+m)(\alpha+\beta+m)}{(\alpha+\beta+2m-1)(\alpha+\beta+2m)},\\
U_{2m-1}(y)=(y-h)(y+(\beta+m-N)h)/h^{2},B_{2m-1}(y)=(y-h)(\alpha+\beta+2m-1)/h,\\
C_{2m-1}(y)=(\alpha+\beta+2m-1)y/h-m\alpha-(m+1)\beta-m(m+1)+(\beta+1)N,\\
D_{2m-1}=-\dfrac{(\beta+m)(\alpha+\beta+m)(N-m)}{\alpha+\beta+2m-1}

A_{m}
has not been written, only
U_{m}
or
V_{m}
, which is easiest.

If
h\to 0,N\to\infty
, such that
Nh\to 1
, we expect to recover a special case of the continued fraction of
Gauss (
2
) and the Jacobi orthogonal polynomials. Indeed, we recover
r_{2m}=\dfrac{m(m+q-1)}{(2m+p-1)(2m+p)},\\
r_{2m-1}=\dfrac{(m+p-1)(m+p-q)}{(2m+p-2)(2m+p-1)}
from
[

, § 22.2.2, § 22.7.2]
with

.

2.
Biorthogonal rational functions.

Instead of Padé approximation about
x=\infty
, we interpolate the same
f
(up to subtraction by a constant)
at
x_{0},x_{1}=x_{0}+h,\dotsc
, with
x_{0}=x^{\prime}_{0}+j^{\prime}_{0}h
, where
j^{\prime}_{0}=-\beta
, which is a singular point of the difference equation,

(j+1)(\alpha+N-j-1)f(x+h)-\overbrace{(\beta+j+1)}^{\displaystyle 1+(x-x_{0})/h}(N-1-j)f(x)=D(y)=(\alpha+\beta+1)\mu_{0}/h
at
x=x^{\prime}_{0}+jh=x_{0}+(\beta+j)h
,
j=-\beta,-\beta+1,\dotsc

for
f
, as
f(x_{0})
does not depend on
f(x_{-1})
,
the value of
f(x_{0})
must be
(\alpha+\beta+1)\mu_{0}/(\beta(\alpha+\beta+N)h)

evaluated at
j=j^{\prime}_{0}-1=-\beta-1
. Starting the interpolation at a singular point
also allows simplifications in the construction of expansions, see section

As the present theory starts with
f_{0}(x_{0})=0
, we take
f_{0}(x)=f(x)-f(x_{0})
, and
the new
D_{0}
is

A(y)(\mathcal{D}f_{0})(y)-C(y)(\mathcal{M}f_{0})(y)=D_{0}(y)=-(\alpha+\beta+1)\mu_{0}/h+f(x_{0})C(y)=f(x_{0})[-\beta(\alpha+\beta+N)+(\beta+j+1)(N-1-j)-(j+1)(\alpha+N-j-1)]=-f(x_{0})(\beta+j+1)(\alpha+\beta)=-f(x_{0})(\alpha+\beta)(y-y_{-1})/h
.

Note that the degrees of the polynomials
A_{0}=A,C_{0}=C,D_{0}
are
2,1,1
.
We also have
r_{0}=\dfrac{D_{0}(y_{0})}{A_{0}(y_{0})-hC_{0}(y_{0})/2}=\dfrac{-f(x_{0})(\alpha+\beta)}{h(-\beta+1)(\alpha+\beta+N-1)}
.

We proceed with (
68a
-
68d
) from
m=0
and with
\Upsilon_{0}(y)=1/(y-y_{-1})=1/((\beta+j+1)h)
:

(
71
) with

\Upsilon_{m}(y)=1/(r_{m}(y-y_{m-1}))
and, for

,

U_{2m}(y)=(y-y_{2m-1}))(y-y^{\prime}_{-1}-(N-m)h)/h^{2}B_{2m}=3m(m-\beta)-2mN-m\alpha+(\alpha+\beta+N)\beta,C_{2m}(y)=-(\alpha+\beta)(y-y^{\prime}_{-1})/h-2mN+3m^{2}+(N-m)\beta+m\alpha,D_{2m}(y)=m(\alpha+m)(N-m)(y-y_{2m-1})/(hB_{2m})
,

r_{2m}=-m(\alpha+m)(N-m)/(hB_{2m}B_{2m+1}),

(81)

U_{2m-1}(y)=(y-y_{2m-2}))(y-y^{\prime}_{-1}-(\alpha+\beta+N-m)h)/h^{2}B_{2m-1}=-3m^{2}+3m-1-(2m-1)N-m\alpha-(3m-1)\beta-(\alpha+\beta+N)\beta,C_{2m-1}(y)=-(\alpha+\beta-1)(y-y^{\prime}_{-1})/h+(2m-1)N-(N-5m+1)\beta+(3m-1)(\alpha-m)-2\beta(\alpha+\beta),D_{2m-1}(y)=-(\beta-m)(\alpha+\beta-m)(\alpha+\beta-m+N)(y-y_{2m-2})/(hB_{2m-1})
,

(82)

Starting the interpolation at a generic
x_{0}
instead of

should be hardly
more difficult, the degrees of the polynomials

, etc. are simply a little bit higher than
before, but the coefficients of these polynomials soon become intractable, even
when
\alpha=\beta=0
, we have
w_{j+1}=w_{j}
,

f(x_{n})=\sum_{0}^{N-1}(x^{\prime}_{j}-x_{n})^{-1}=\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}(x^{\prime}_{0}-x_{0}-(j+n)h)^{-1}
,
deciding to put the poles at

to be closer to the

f(x_{n+1})-f(x_{n})=\dfrac{1}{x^{\prime}_{0}-x_{0}-(n+N)h}-\dfrac{1}{x^{\prime}_{0}-x_{0}-nh}=\dfrac{hD_{0}(y)=Nh(z+1)}{A_{0}(y)=h^{3}(z+1)(z+s)(z+s+N)}
,
z=y/h=x/h+1,s=-x^{\prime}_{0}/h
. No further calculations are shown here.

Tsujimoto, Vinet, and Zhedanov, have built in
[

]
a biorthogonal set

of rational

;

with poles at

, where

. The numerators

satisfy difference equations and recurrence relations involving
P_{2m+2}(x),P_{2m}(x),P_{2m-2}(x)
,
[
130
, §6]
, where one recognizes the R-

type recurrence relation (

), with

a
polynomial in
m
(depending on the evenness of

) divided by
(N-\beta-m)(N-\beta-m+1)
.

This recurrence relation is not the same as what has been done here in (

-
82
) , the authors of
[

]
did consider
two separate sets of poles
\alpha,\alpha+1,\dotsc
and
\gamma,\gamma-1,\dotsc
,
whereas only a single set
\{x_{0},x_{1},\dotsc
has been studied here.

Next subsection considers
N\to\infty
where complexity remains bounded.

7.3.8.
The Psi function.

We interpolate on
x_{0},x_{1}=x_{0}+1,x_{2}=x_{0}+2,\dotsc
the function
f(x)=\Psi(x-x^{\prime}_{0})-\Psi(x_{0}-x^{\prime}_{0})
,
where the Psi (
\Psi
) function has been briefly discussed in §
6.1
,
so we have
f(x_{0})=0,f(x_{1})=1/(x_{0}-x^{\prime}_{0}),f(x_{2})=1/(x_{0}-x^{\prime}_{0})+1/(x_{1}-x^{\prime}_{0})
, etc.
We interpolate a meromorphic function of poles
x^{\prime}_{0},x^{\prime}_{0}-1,x^{\prime}_{0}-2,\dotsc
with the
first interpolation point not related to any singular point, as wished at the end of the preceding subsection

The first steps

f(x)=\dfrac{(x-x_{0})/(x_{0}-x^{\prime}_{0})}{1+\dfrac{((x-x_{1})/(2(x_{0}-x^{\prime}_{0})+1)}{1+\ddots}}
.

One finds
\mathcal{D}f(y_{n})=\dfrac{f(x_{n+1})-f(x_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}=1}=\dfrac{1}{x_{n}-x^{\prime}_{0}=y_{n}-1-x^{\prime}_{0}}
, so

A_{0}(y)=(y-x^{\prime}_{0}-1),B_{0}=C_{0}=0,D_{0}=1,r_{0}=\dfrac{D_{0}(y_{0})}{A_{0}(y_{0})-C_{0}(y_{0})/2}=\dfrac{1}{y_{0}-x^{\prime}_{0}-1},

With the shorthand
z=y-y_{0}=y-x_{0}-1,s=x_{0}-x^{\prime}_{0}
,
we proceed with (
68a
-
68d
) at
m=1,2,\dotsc
, find exact divisions with
\Upsilon_{m}(y)=1/(y-y_{0}-m+1)=1/(z-m+1)
and get

for even index
>0
,
A_{2m}(y)=z^{2}+(s-m+1)z-(m-1)s-m^{2}/2,B_{2m}=C_{2m}=-2m(s+3m/2-1),D_{2m}(y)=m(s+m-1)(z-2m+1)/(2s+3m-2),r_{2m}=m(s+m-1)/[(2s+3m-2)((2m+1)s+m(3m+1))]
,

for odd index,
A_{2m+1}(y)=z^{2}+(s-m+1/2)z-(m-1/2)s-m(m+1)/2,B_{2m+1}=-(2m+1)s-m(3m+1),C_{2m+1}(y)=z-(2m+1)s-3m(m+1),D_{2m+1}(y)=(m+1)^{2}(s+m)(z-2m)/((2m+1)s+m(3m+1)),r_{2m+1}=(m+1)(s+m)/[(2s+3m+1)((2m+1)s+m(3m+1))]
.

The example of §
1.4
is made with
s=1
.

7.3.9.
Full elliptic lattice, the classical case.

The simplest instances of families of classical special functions have explicit formulas for recurrence relations, differential and difference equations etc. So, the Gauss continued fraction (
2
)
has parameters
r_{m}=
a polynomial in
m
(depending on the evenness of
m
, so actually a polynomial in
m
and
(-1)^{m}
) over
(\gamma+m-1)(\gamma+m)
.
Other simple examples have been given in §
1.4
, §
7.3.5
, §
7.3.6
, Hahn function and polynomials in §
7.3.7
,
Koekoek et al
[
76
, §14.1]
present the most elaborate recurrence relations of orthogonal polynomials in terms of parameters
A_{m},C_{m}
easily recognized as our
r_{2m+1},r_{2m}
of (
58
), and have
r_{m}=
a polynomial of degree 4 in
q^{m/2}
over
(1-abcdq^{m-2})(1-abcdq^{m-1})
.

For biorthogonal rational functions,
Rahman
[
111
]
has
r_{m}=am+b+c(-1)^{m}
, Masson
[
97
]
has
r_{m}=
a polynomial of degree 8 (amazing!) in
q^{m/2}
over
q^{m}(1-sq^{m-2})(1-sq^{m-1})
.

On fully elliptic grids,
Spiridonov ad Zhedanov gave in
[
123
,
124
,
125
]

r_{m}
as
a product of 7 factors
[m+a_{j}+b_{j}(-1)^{m}]
(theta functions) over
[2m+s][2m+1+s][m+c+d(-1)^{m}]

[
123
, pp. 384-385]
. See
[
122
]
for further developments.

The degrees of
A_{m},\dotsc,D_{m}
must be at least
d=3
on a genera1 elliptic grid,
as
Y_{2}
, normally of degree 2, is a factor of
B_{m}
etc., and
y-y_{m-1}
is a factor of
D_{m}
from (
68e
). Even if
Y_{2}
has degree
<2
, the polynomial
Q
, needed in (
68a
), has degree
\geqslant 3
in the genus
>0
case, from §
3.4
, as
Q
must have 4 simple zeros (a polynomial
Q
of degree
<3
has a multiple zero at
\infty
).

Unfortunately, no explicit example of
A_{m}
etc. has been found here on a genera1 elliptic grid.

7.4.
Difference relations and equations

7.4.1.

Theorem.
Let
\{(x_{n},y_{n})\}
,
\{(x_{n+1},y_{n})\}
be points
on the biquadratic curve

F(x,y)=Y_{0}(y)+xY_{1}(y)+x^{2}Y_{2}(y)=0
, and
f
a function
defined on the
x_{n}
s, satisfying the
discrete Riccati
difference equation
(
65a
)

A(y_{n})(\mathcal{D}f)(y_{n})=B(y_{n})f(x_{n})f(x_{n+1})+C(y_{n})(\mathcal{M}f)(y_{n})+D(y_{n})
, with
f(x_{0})=0
, and we consider these rational interpolants to
f

\dfrac{N_{m}(x)}{P_{m}(x)}=\dfrac{r_{0}(x-x_{0})}{1+\dfrac{\ddots}{1+r_{m-1}(x-x_{m-1})}}
, for
m=0,1,\dotsc

Then,
N_{m}
and
P_{m}
satisfy linear difference equations of order
\leqslant 4
with coefficients depending on
A_{m},\dotsc,D_{m}
defined in (
67
),

P_{m}
satisfies a linear difference equation of order
\leqslant 2
if
B(y)\equiv 0
.

Proof.

We recall
U_{m}(y_{n})=\dfrac{C_{m}(y_{n})}{2}+\dfrac{A_{m}(y_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}},V_{m}(y_{n})=\dfrac{C_{m}(y_{n})}{2}-\dfrac{A_{m}(y_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}
,
and rewrite (
68a
)-(
68d
) as

\begin{bmatrix}B_{m+1}(y_{n})\\
U_{m+1}(y_{n})\\
V_{m+1}(y_{n})\\
D_{m+1}(y_{n})\end{bmatrix}=\Upsilon_{m}(y_{n})\begin{bmatrix}0&0&0&1\\
0&0&r_{m}(x_{n+1}-x_{m})&1\\
0&r_{m}(x_{n}-x_{m})&0&1\\
r_{m}^{2}(x_{n}-x_{m})(x_{n+1}-x_{m})&r_{m}(x_{n}-x_{m})&r_{m}(x_{n+1}-x_{m})&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}B_{m}(y_{n})\\
U_{m}(y_{n})\\
V_{m}(y_{n})\\
D_{m}(y_{n})\end{bmatrix}

which is exactly the recurrence relation of products
u(x_{n+1})v(x_{n})
, where
u_{m+1}(x)=u_{m}(x)+r_{m}(x-x_{m})u_{m-1}(x)
(resp.
v
),the recurrence relations of
N_{m}
and
P_{m}
,
for example
u_{m+1}(x_{n+1})v_{m}(x_{n})=u_{m}(x_{n+1})v_{m}(x_{n})+r_{m}(x_{n+1}-x_{m})u_{m-1}(x_{n+1})v_{m}(x_{n})
etc. and we look at various instances of

u_{m-1}(x_{n+1})v_{m-1}(x_{n}),u_{m}(x_{n+1})v_{m-1}(x_{n}),u_{m-1}(x_{n+1}v_{m}(x_{n}),u_{m}(x_{n+1})v_{m}(x_{n})
at
m=0
:

\begin{matrix}N_{-1}N_{-1}=1&N_{0}N_{-1}=0&N_{-1}N_{0}=0&N_{0}N_{0}=0\\
P_{-1}N_{-1}=0&P_{0}N_{-1}=1&P_{-1}N_{0}=0&P_{0}N_{0}=0\\
N_{-1}P_{-1}=0&N_{0}P_{-1}=0&N_{-1}P_{0}=1&N_{0}P_{0}=0\\
P_{-1}P_{-1}=0&P_{0}P_{-1}=0&P_{-1}P_{0}=0&P_{0}P_{0}=1\end{matrix}
,

and

\begin{split}&\begin{bmatrix}B_{m}(y_{n})\\
U_{m}(y_{n})\\
V_{m}(y_{n})\\
D_{m}(y_{n})\end{bmatrix}=\Upsilon_{0}(y_{n})\dotsb\Upsilon_{m-1}(y_{n})\\
&\times\begin{bmatrix}N_{m-1}(x_{n+1})N_{m-1}(x_{n})&P_{m-1}(x_{n+1})N_{m-1}(x_{n})&N_{m-1}(x_{n+1})P_{m-1}(x_{n})&P_{m-1}(x_{n+1})P_{m-1}(x_{n})\\
N_{m}(x_{n+1})N_{m-1}(x_{n})&P_{m}(x_{n+1})N_{m-1}(x_{n})&N_{m}(x_{n+1})P_{m-1}(x_{n})&P_{m}(x_{n+1})P_{m-1}(x_{n})\\
N_{m-1}(x_{n+1})N_{m}(x_{n})&P_{m-1}(x_{n+1})N_{m}(x_{n})&N_{m-1}(x_{n+1})P_{m}(x_{n})&P_{m-1}(x_{n+1})P_{m}(x_{n})\\
N_{m}(x_{n+1})N_{m}(x_{n})&P_{m}(x_{n+1})N_{m}(x_{n})&N_{m}(x_{n+1})P_{m}(x_{n})&P_{m}(x_{n+1})P_{m}(x_{n})\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}B(y_{n})\\
U(y_{n})\\
V(y_{n})\\
D(y_{n})\end{bmatrix}\end{split}

(83)

We recognize that
D_{m}
is basically the Laguerre’s
\Theta_{m}
of (
78
).
These identities are not meant to give
B_{m}
etc.,which we already have
in (
68a
)-(
68d
), in terms of
P_{m},N_{m}
,etc. but exactly
the opposite is true, we will get difference relations and equations for
P_{m},N_{m}
precisely. But before that, another way to have (
83
):

\begin{split}0&=A(y_{n})(\mathcal{D}f)(y_{n})-B(y_{n})f(x_{n})f(x_{n+1})-C(y_{n})(\mathcal{M}f)(y_{n})-D(y_{n})\\
&=\left[\dfrac{A(y_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}-\dfrac{C(y_{n})}{2}\right]\dfrac{N_{m}(x_{n+1})+N_{m-1}(x_{n+1})f_{m}(x_{n+1})}{P_{m}(x_{n+1})+P_{m-1}(x_{n+1})f_{m}(x_{n+1})}\\
&\ \ -\left[\dfrac{A(y_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}+\dfrac{C(y_{n})}{2}\right]\dfrac{N_{m}(x_{n})+N_{m-1}(x_{n})f_{m}(x_{n})}{P_{m}(x_{n})+P_{m-1}(x_{n})f_{m}(x_{n})}\\
-B(y_{n})&\dfrac{N_{m}(x_{n})+N_{m-1}(x_{n})f_{m}(x_{n})}{P_{m}(x_{n})+P_{m-1}(x_{n})f_{m}(x_{n})}\dfrac{N_{m}(x_{n+1})+N_{m-1}(x_{n+1})f_{m}(x_{n+1})}{P_{m}(x_{n+1})+P_{m-1}(x_{n+1})f_{m}(x_{n+1})}-D(y_{n})\end{split}

(84)

Multiply the denominators,

0=-V(y_{n})[N_{m-1}(x_{n+1})P_{m}(x_{n})f_{m}(x_{n+1})+N_{m}(x_{n+1})P_{m-1}(x_{n})f_{m}(x_{n})+N_{m-1}(x_{n+1})P_{m-1}(x_{n})f_{m}(x_{n})f_{m}(x_{n+1})+N_{m}(x_{n+1})P_{m}(x_{n})]\\
-U(y_{n})[N_{m}(x_{n})P_{m-1}(x_{n+1})f_{m}(x_{n+1})+N_{m-1}(x_{n})P_{m}(x_{n+1})f_{m}(x_{n})+N_{m-1}(x_{n})P_{m-1}(x_{n+1})f_{m}(x_{n})f_{m}(x_{n+1})+N_{m}(x_{n})P_{m}(x_{n+1})]\\
-B(y_{n})[N_{m-1}(x_{n+1})N_{m}(x_{n})f_{m}(x_{n+1})+N_{m-1}(x_{n})N_{m}(x_{n+1})f_{m}(x_{n})+N_{m-1}(x_{n+1})N_{m-1}(x_{n})f_{m}(x_{n})f_{m}(x_{n+1})+N_{m}(x_{n+1})N_{m}(x_{n})]\\
-D(y_{n})[P_{m-1}(x_{n+1})P_{m}(x_{n})f_{m}(x_{n+1})+P_{m-1}(x_{n})P_{m}(x_{n+1})f_{m}(x_{n})+P_{m-1}(x_{n+1})P_{m-1}(x_{n})f_{m}(x_{n})f_{m}(x_{n+1})+P_{m}(x_{n+1})P_{m}(x_{n})]

must be
\left(-V_{m}(y_{n})=\dfrac{A_{m}(y_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}-\dfrac{C_{m}(y_{n})}{2}\right)f_{m}(x_{n+1})-\left(U_{m}(y_{n})=\dfrac{A_{m}(y_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}+\dfrac{C_{m}(y_{n})}{2}\right)f_{m}(x_{n})-B_{m}(y_{n})f_{m}(x_{n})f_{m}(x_{n+1})-D_{m}(y_{n})=0
,

and one recovers (the second part of) (
83
).

In (
83
), take
B_{m}(y_{n})P_{m}(x_{n+1}-U_{m}(y_{n})P_{m-1}(x_{n+1})
:

\dfrac{(-1)^{m}\Upsilon_{0}(y_{n})\dotsb\Upsilon_{m-1}(y_{n})}{r_{0}\dotsb r_{m-1}}

\times\{(N_{m-1}(x_{n+1})P_{m}(x_{n+1})-N_{m}(x_{n+1})P_{m-1}(_{n+1}))N_{m-1}(x_{n})B(y_{n})\\
+(N_{m-1}(x_{n+1})P_{m}(x_{n+1})-N_{m}(x_{n+1})P_{m-1}(x_{n+1}))P_{m-1}(x_{n})V(y_{n})\}

remains, where one recognizes the Casorati product (
56
)

\begin{split}&\begin{bmatrix}B_{m}(y_{n})P_{m}(x_{n+1})-U_{m}(y_{n})P_{m-1}(x_{n+1})\\
B_{m}(y_{n})P_{m}(x_{n})-V_{m}(y_{n})P_{m-1}(x_{n})\\
U_{m}(y_{n})N_{m}(x_{n})-D_{m}(y_{n})N_{m-1}(x_{n})\\
V_{m}(y_{n})N_{m}(x_{n+1})-D_{m}(y_{n})N_{m-1}(x_{n+1})\end{bmatrix}=(-1)^{m}r_{0}\dotsb r_{m-1}\Upsilon_{0}(y_{n})\dotsb\Upsilon_{m-1}(y_{n})\\
\times&\begin{bmatrix}(x_{n+1}-x_{0})\dotsb(x_{n+1}-x_{m-1})\{B(y_{n})N_{m-1}(x_{n})+V(y_{n})P_{m-1}(x_{n})\}\\
(x_{n}-x_{0})\dotsb(x_{n}-x_{m-1})\{B(y_{n})N_{m-1}(x_{n+1})+U(y_{n})P_{m-1}(x_{n+1})\}\\
(x_{n}-x_{0})\dotsb(x_{n}-x_{m-1})\{-V(y_{n})N_{m}(x_{n+1})-D(y_{n})P_{m}(x_{n+1})\}\\
(x_{n+1}-x_{0})\dotsb(x_{n+1}-x_{m-1})\{-U(y_{n})N_{m}(x_{n})-D(y_{n})P_{m}(x_{n})\}\end{bmatrix}\end{split}

(85)

Such relations between
\phi_{m}
, differences of
\phi_{m}
and values of
\phi_{m\pm 1}
are sometimes called differential or difference relations,
sometimes raising and lowering operators relations, sometimes structure relations
[
72
,
79
,
80
,
96
,
98
]
.

Let
\chi_{m}(x_{n})=\Upsilon_{0}(y_{n})\dotsb\Upsilon_{m-1}(y_{n})(x_{n}-x_{0})\dotsb(x_{n}-x_{m-1})

=\dfrac{(Y_{2}(y_{n}))^{d_{m}}(x_{n}-x_{0})\dotsb(x_{n}-x_{m-1})}{(y_{n}-y_{-1})\dotsb(y_{n}-y_{m-2})}
, and

\chi^{+}_{m}(x_{n+1})=\Upsilon_{0}(y_{n})\dotsb\Upsilon_{m-1}(y_{n})(x_{n+1}-x_{0})\dotsb(x_{n+1}-x_{m-1})

=\dfrac{(Y_{2}(y_{n}))^{d_{m}}(x_{n+1}-x_{0})\dotsb(x_{n+1}-x_{m-1})}{(y_{n}-y_{-1})\dotsb(y_{n}-y_{m-2})}
.

(
85
) is a first order vector difference equation relating

[P_{m-1},P_{m},N_{m-1},N_{m}]
at
x_{n}
and
x_{n+1}
:

\begin{split}&\begin{bmatrix}-U_{m}(y_{n})&B_{m}(y_{n})&0&0\\
-\chi_{m}(x_{n})U(y_{n})&0&-\chi_{m}(x_{n})B(y_{n})&0\\
0&\chi_{m}(x_{n})D(y_{n})&0&\chi_{m}(x_{n})V(y_{n})\\
0&0&-D_{m}(y_{n})&V_{m}(y_{n})\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}P_{m-1}(x_{n+1})\\
P_{m}(x_{n+1})\\
N_{m-1}(x_{n+1})\\
N_{m}(x_{n+1})\end{bmatrix}\\
=&\begin{bmatrix}\chi^{+}_{m}(x_{n+1})V(y_{n})&0&\chi^{+}_{m}(x_{n+1})B(y_{n})&0\\
V_{m}(y_{n})&-B_{m}(y_{n})&0&0\\
0&0&D_{m}(y_{n})&-U_{m}(y_{n})\\
0&-\chi^{+}_{m}(x_{n+1})D(y_{n})&0&-\chi^{+}_{m}(x_{n+1})U(y_{n})\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}P_{m-1}(x_{n})\\
P_{m}(x_{n})\\
N_{m-1}(x_{n})\\
N_{m}(x_{n})\end{bmatrix}\end{split}

(86)

which can be condensed as a
scalar
fourth-order difference
equation for, say,
P_{m}

[
11
,
116
]

whose solutions are products of solutions of second order differential or difference eq:

[
67
, p.446]
,
[
44
, §4]

Finally, when
B(y)\equiv 0
,

-U_{m}(y_{n})P_{m-1}(x_{n+1})+B_{m}(y_{n})P_{m}(x_{n+1})=\chi^{+}_{m}(x_{n+1})V(y_{n})P_{m-1}(x_{n})
,

-\chi_{m}(x_{n})U(y_{n})P_{m-1}(x_{n+1})=V_{m}(y_{n})P_{m-1}(x_{n})-B_{m}(y_{n})P_{m}(x_{n})

scalar eq: put
m\to m+1,n\to n-1
, eliminate
P_{m+1}(x_{n})
from

P_{m+1}(x_{n})=\dfrac{U_{m+1}(y_{n-1})P_{m}(x_{n})+\chi^{+}_{m+1}(x_{n})V(y_{n-1})P_{m}(x_{n-1})}{B_{m+1}(y_{n-1})}

=\dfrac{\chi_{m+1}(x_{n})U(y_{n})P_{m}(x_{n+1})+V_{m+1}(y_{n})P_{m}(x_{n})}{B_{m+1}(y_{n})}

to have

\chi_{m+1}(x_{n})U(y_{n})B_{m+1}(y_{n-1})P_{m}(x_{n+1})+[V_{m+1}(y_{n})B_{m+1}(y_{n-1})-U_{m+1}(y_{n-1})B_{m+1}(y_{n})]P_{m}(x_{n})\\
-\chi^{+}_{m+1}(x_{n})V(y_{n-1})B_{m+1}(y_{n})P_{m}(x_{n-1})=0
,

which is a scalar linear second order difference equation. ∎

See §
9
for more on second order difference equations.

Ratios
\dfrac{(x_{n}-x_{0})\dotsb(x_{n}-x_{m-1})}{(y_{n}-y_{-1})\dotsb(y_{n}-y_{m-2})}
entering
\chi_{m}(x_{n})

and similar ones for
\chi^{+}_{m}(x_{n+1})
simplify nicely for simple grids,
where
(x_{n}-x_{j})/(y_{n}-y_{j-1})
appears as a function of
j
over the same function of
j-1
:

x_{n}=n,y_{n}=n+1/2,\chi_{m}=n(n-1)\dotsb(n-m+1)/((n+1)n(n-1)\dotsb(n-m+2))=(n-m+1)/(n+1)
,

x_{n}=q^{n},y_{n}=q^{n+1/2},(x_{n}-x_{j})/(y_{n}-y_{j-1})=(q^{n}-q^{j})/(q^{n+1/2}-q^{j-1/2})=q^{-1/2}(q^{n}-q^{j})/(q^{n}-q^{j-1})
, and the product
=q^{-m/2}(q^{n}-q^{m-1})/(q^{n}-q^{-1})
,

even
x_{n}=\sin(nh+\theta),y_{n}=\sin((n+1/2)h+\theta)
gives
(x_{n}-x_{j})/(y_{n}-y_{j-1})=[\sin((n-j)h/2)\cos((n+j)h/2+\theta)]/[\sin((n-j+1)h/2)\cos((n+j)h/2+\theta)]
and

\sin((n-m+1)h/2+\theta)/\sin((n+1)h/2+\theta)
remains.

8.
The adjoint operator.

8.1.
Definitions

Let
g
be defined on at least a sequence
\{y_{n}\}
, then, the
adjoint operator to
\mathcal{D}
is defined as

(\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}g)(x_{n})=-\dfrac{g(y_{n})-g(y_{n-1})}{y_{n}-y_{n-1}}

(87)

at
x=x_{n}
.

One has

\sum_{0}^{N-1}f(x_{n})(\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}g)(x_{n})(y_{n}-y_{n-1})=\sum_{0}^{N-1}(\mathcal{D}f)(y_{n})\ g(y_{n})(x_{n+1}-x_{n})-f(x_{0})g(y_{-1})+f(x_{N})g(y_{N-1})
.

Indeeed, the left-handed side sum is rearranged as

\sum_{0}^{N-1}(f(x_{n+1}-f(x_{n}))g(y_{n})\ \ \ f(x_{0})g(y_{-1})-f(x_{N})g(y_{N-1})\\
=\sum_{0}^{N-1}(\mathcal{D}f)(y_{n})\ g(y_{n})(x_{n+1}-x_{n})\ \ \ f(x_{0})g(y_{-1})-f(x_{N})g(y_{N-1})
,

showing a discrete integration by parts, also called Abel’s summation rule, as noted in (
1
) in §
1.1
.

The difference elements
y_{n}-y_{n-1}
at
x_{n}
, and
x_{n+1}-x_{n}
at
y_{n}

are not as crazy as they seem:

d_{q}x=c(q^{n}-q^{n+1})
in Jackson
[
76
, §1.15 ]
.

Milne-Thomson
[
102
, §12.6]
and Nörlund
[
105
, p. 288]
give
the adjoint equation of
\alpha_{n}v_{n+d}+\beta_{n}v_{n+d-1}+\dotsb+\omega_{n}v_{n}=0
to be

\alpha_{n}u_{n}+\beta_{n+1}u_{n+1}+\dotsb+\omega_{n+d}u_{n+d}=0
(equation for
a multiplier). Rearranging
\sum u_{n}(\alpha_{n}v_{n+d}+\dotsb+\omega_{n}v_{n})

yields
\sum v_{n}(\alpha_{n-d}u_{n-d}+\dotsb+\omega_{n}u_{n})
, showing the
adjoint operator. In particular,
the adjoint of
\Delta
is
-\nabla
.

We now come to a simple fraction case,

\left(\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}\dfrac{1}{y-A}\right)(x)=\dfrac{X_{2}(x)}{Y_{2}(A)(x-x_{\kappa})(x-x_{\kappa+1})}

(88)

where
\kappa
is such that
A=y_{\kappa}
.

Indeed,

\dfrac{1/(y_{n}-A)-1/(y_{n-1}-A)}{y_{n}-y_{n-1}}=\dfrac{-1}{(y_{n}-A)(y_{n-1}-A)}=\dfrac{-X_{2}(x_{n})}{F(x_{n},A=y_{\kappa})=Y_{2}(A)(x-x_{\kappa})(x-x_{\kappa+1})}
.

Naturally enough, we define

(\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}g)(x_{n})=\dfrac{g(y_{n})+g(y_{n-1})}{2}\ \ \ \text{at\ }x=x_{n}.

(89)

Note that the operator
\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}
just defined in (
89
) is NOT
the adjoint of
\mathcal{M}
with respect to the bilinear form associated to

\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}
: if
\mathscr{M}
is the adjoint to
\mathcal{M}

with respect to the same bilinear form, one must have

\langle f(x),(\mathscr{M}g)(x)\rangle=\sum_{n}(y_{n}-y_{n-1})f(x_{n})(\mathscr{M}g)(x_{n})=\langle(\mathcal{M}f)(y),g(y)\rangle=\sum_{n}(x_{n+1}-x_{n})(f(x_{n})+f(x_{n+1})g(y_{n})/2
rearranged as

\sum(y_{n}-y_{n-1})f(x_{n})\dfrac{(x_{n+1}-x_{n})g(y_{n})+(x_{n}-x_{n-1})g(y_{n-1})}{2(y_{n}-y_{n-1})}
,

so, the ”true” adjoint of
\mathcal{M}
is
(\mathscr{M}g)(x_{n})=\dfrac{(x_{n+1}-x_{n})g(y_{n})+(x_{n}-x_{n-1})g(y_{n-1})}{2(y_{n}-y_{n-1})}
, will not be used here.

8.2.
Adjoint operators applied to products

We now transpose §
5.3.1

Theorem.

Let
\{(x_{n},y_{n})\},\{(x^{\prime}_{n},y^{\prime}_{n})\}
be two elliptic lattices on the same

F-
curve, then

\begin{split}\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}\dfrac{(y-y_{r})\dotsb(y-y_{r+m-1})}{(y-y^{\prime}_{s})\dotsb(y-y^{\prime}_{s+m-1})}&=C^{\dagger}_{m,r,s}X_{2}(x)\dfrac{(x-x_{r+1})\dotsb(x-x_{r+m-1})}{(x-x^{\prime}_{s})\dotsb(x-x^{\prime}_{s+m})},\\
\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}\dfrac{(y-y_{r})\dotsb(y-y_{r+m-1})}{(y-y^{\prime}_{s})\dotsb(y-y^{\prime}_{s+m-1})}&=D_{m,r,s}^{\dagger}(x)\dfrac{(x-x_{r+1})\dotsb(x-x_{r+m-1})}{(x-x^{\prime}_{s})\dotsb(x-x^{\prime}_{s+m})},\end{split}

(90)

where
C_{m,r,s}^{\dagger}
is a constant, and
D_{m,r,s}^{\dagger}
is a second degree polynomial.

Indeed, let
Q_{m}(y)=\dfrac{(y-y_{r})\dotsb(y-y_{r+m-1})}{(y-y^{\prime}_{s})\dotsb(y-y^{\prime}_{k+m-1})}
.
the operators
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}
applied to each simple fraction

1/(y-y^{\prime}_{j})
yield fractions divided by
(x-x^{\prime}_{j})(x-x^{\prime}_{j+1})
by
(
88
),
the results
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}Q_{m}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}Q_{m}
are therefore rational functions of degree
m+1
and poles

x^{\prime}_{s},\dotsc,x^{\prime}_{s+m}
.

We now look at some
x=x_{n}
involving the values of
Q_{m}
at
y_{n-1}

and
y_{n}
. We see that

y_{n-1}-y_{r}
and
y_{n}-y_{r+1}
both vanish at
x=x_{r+1}
, etc.
up to
y_{n-1}-y_{r+m-2}
and
y_{n}-y_{r+m-1}
both vanishing
at
x=x_{r+m-1}
,
so that
Q_{m}(y_{n-1})
and
Q_{m}(y_{n})
both vanish at

y_{n-1}=y_{r},\dotsc,y_{r+m-2}
and

y_{n}=y_{r+1},\dotsc,y_{r+m-1}
, corresponding to
x=x_{r+1},\dotsc,x_{r+m-1}
.

The first part of (
90
) then follows from (
88
).

For
\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}
, we still have a rational function of degree

m+1
with the same
m-1
zeros, whence the factor
D_{m,r,s}^{\dagger}(x)
.

Examples
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}\dfrac{y-y_{r}}{y-y^{\prime}_{s}}=\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}\dfrac{y^{\prime}_{s}-y_{r}}{y-y^{\prime}_{s}}
at
x_{n}=\dfrac{y^{\prime}_{s}-y_{r}}{(y_{n-1}-y^{\prime}_{s})(y_{n}-y^{\prime}_{s})}=\dfrac{(y^{\prime}_{s}-y_{r})X_{2}(x_{n})}{F(x_{n},y^{\prime}_{s})=Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{s})(x_{n}-x^{\prime}_{s})(x_{n}-x^{\prime}_{s+1})}
, so,

C_{1,r,s}^{\dagger}=\dfrac{y^{\prime}_{s}-y_{r}}{Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{s})}=\dfrac{(x_{r}-x^{\prime}_{s})(x_{r}-x^{\prime}_{s+1})}{X_{2}(x_{r})(y^{\prime}_{s}-y_{r-1})}.

(91)

2\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}\dfrac{y-y_{r}}{y-y^{\prime}_{s}}
at
x_{n}
is
\dfrac{y_{n}-y_{r}}{y_{n}-y^{\prime}_{s}}+\dfrac{y_{n-1}-y_{r}}{y_{n-1}-y^{\prime}_{s}}=\dfrac{2y_{n}y_{n-1}-(y_{r}+y^{\prime}_{s})(y_{n}+y_{n-1})+2y_{r}y^{\prime}_{s}}{(y_{n}-y^{\prime}_{s})(y_{n-1}-y^{\prime}_{s})=F(x_{n},y^{\prime}_{s})/X_{2}(x_{n})}\\
=\dfrac{2X_{0}(x_{n})+(y_{r}+y^{\prime}_{s})X_{1}(x_{n})+2y_{r}y^{\prime}_{s}X_{2}(x_{n})}{Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{s})(x_{n}-x^{\prime}_{s})(x_{n}-x^{\prime}_{s+1})}
, and

D_{1,r,s}^{\dagger}(x)=\dfrac{X_{0}(x)+(y_{r}+y^{\prime}_{s})X_{1}(x)/2+y_{r}y^{\prime}_{s}X_{2}(x)}{Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{s})}

(92)

The constant
C_{m,r,s}^{\dagger}
is found through particular values of
x_{n}
,
involving
y_{n-1}-y_{r}
and
y_{n}-y_{r}
the latter only vanishing at
x=x_{r}
, so

\begin{split}C_{m,r,s}^{\dagger}&=\dfrac{(x_{r}-x^{\prime}_{s})\dotsb(x_{r}-x^{\prime}_{s+m})(y_{r-1}-y_{r})\dotsb(y_{r-1}-y_{r+m-1})}{X_{2}(x_{r})(x_{r}-x_{r+1})\dotsb(x_{r}-x_{r+m-1})(y_{r}-y_{r-1})(y_{r-1}-y^{\prime}_{s})\dotsb(y_{r-1}-y^{\prime}_{s+m-1})}\\
&D_{m,r,s}^{\dagger}(x_{r})=(y_{r}-y_{r-1})C_{m,r,s}^{\dagger}X_{2}(x_{r})/2,\end{split}

(93a)

and
y_{n-1}-y_{r+m-1}
and
y_{n}-y_{r+m-1}
the first only vanishing
at
n=r+m
:

\begin{split}C_{m,r,s}^{\dagger}&=-\dfrac{(x_{r+m}-x^{\prime}_{s})\dotsb(x_{r+m}-x^{\prime}_{s+m})(y_{r+m}-y_{r})\dotsb(y_{r+m}-y_{r+m-1})}{X_{2}(x_{r+m})(x_{r+m}-x_{r+1})\dotsb(x_{r+m}-x_{r+m-1})(y_{r+m}-y_{r+m-1})(y_{r+m}-y^{\prime}_{s})\dotsb(y_{r+m}-x^{\prime}_{s+m-1})}\\
&D_{m,r,s}^{\dagger}(x_{r+m})=-(y_{r+m}-y_{r+m-1})C_{m,r,s}^{\dagger}X_{2}(x_{r+m})/2,\end{split}

(93b)

for
m=1,2,\dotsc

Through residues at
x=x^{\prime}_{s}
, involving
1/(y_{n}-y^{\prime}_{s})=\infty
, while
1/(y_{n-1}-y^{\prime}_{s})=1/(y^{\prime}_{s-1}-y^{\prime}_{s})
remains bounded,
replacing
(x-x^{\prime}_{s})/(y-y^{\prime}_{s})
by
dx/dy=-(y^{\prime}_{s}-y^{\prime}_{s-1})X_{2}(x^{\prime}_{s})/((x^{\prime}_{s}-x^{\prime}_{s+1})Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{s}))

of the tangent at
(x^{\prime}_{s+1},y^{\prime}_{s})
from (
8
)

C_{m,r,s}^{\dagger}=-\dfrac{(y^{\prime}_{s}-y_{r})\dotsb(y^{\prime}_{s}-y_{r+m-1})(x^{\prime}_{s}-x^{\prime}_{s+1})\dotsb(x^{\prime}_{s}-x^{\prime}_{s+m})}{(y^{\prime}_{s}-y^{\prime}_{s+1})\dotsb(y^{\prime}_{s}-y^{\prime}_{s+m-1})(y^{\prime}_{s}-y^{\prime}_{s-1})(x^{\prime}_{s}-x_{r+1})\dotsb(x^{\prime}_{s}-x_{r+m-1})X_{2}(x^{\prime}_{s})}\;\dfrac{dx}{dy}

\begin{split}C_{m,r,s}^{\dagger}&=\dfrac{(y^{\prime}_{s}-y_{r})\dotsb(y^{\prime}_{s}-y_{r+m-1})(x^{\prime}_{s}-x^{\prime}_{s+2})\dotsb(x^{\prime}_{s}-x^{\prime}_{s+m})}{(y^{\prime}_{s}-y^{\prime}_{s+1})\dotsb(y^{\prime}_{s}-y^{\prime}_{s+m-1})(x^{\prime}_{s}-x_{r+1})\dotsb(x^{\prime}_{s}-x_{r+m-1})Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{s})}\\
&D_{m,r,s}^{\dagger}(x^{\prime}_{s})=C_{m,r,s}^{\dagger}(y^{\prime}_{s}-y^{\prime}_{s-1})X_{2}(x^{\prime}_{s})/2,\end{split}

(93c)

and finally at

,

C_{m,r,s}^{\dagger}=\dfrac{(y^{\prime}_{s+m-1}-y_{r})\dotsb(y^{\prime}_{s+m-1}-y_{r+m-1})(x^{\prime}_{s+m}-x^{\prime}_{s})\dotsb(x^{\prime}_{s+m}-x^{\prime}_{s+m-1})}{(y^{\prime}_{s+m-1}-y^{\prime}_{s})\dotsb(y^{\prime}_{s+m-1}-y^{\prime}_{s+m-1})(y^{\prime}_{s+m}-y^{\prime}_{s+m-1})(x^{\prime}_{s+m}-x_{r+1})\dotsb(x^{\prime}_{s+m}-x_{r+m-1})X_{2}(x^{\prime}_{s+m})}\;\dfrac{dx}{dy}

\begin{split}C_{m,r,s}^{\dagger}&=-\dfrac{(y^{\prime}_{s+m-1}-y_{r})\dotsb(y^{\prime}_{s+m-1}-y_{r+m-1})(x^{\prime}_{s+m}-x^{\prime}_{s})\dotsb(x^{\prime}_{s+m}-x^{\prime}_{s+m-2})}{(y^{\prime}_{s+m-1}-y^{\prime}_{s})\dotsb(y^{\prime}_{s+m-1}-y^{\prime}_{s+m-1})(x^{\prime}_{s+m}-x_{r+1})\dotsb(x^{\prime}_{s+m}-x_{r+m-1})Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{s+m-1})}\\
&D_{m,r,s}^{\dagger}(x^{\prime}_{s+m})=C_{m,r,s}^{\dagger}(y^{\prime}_{s+m}-y^{\prime}_{s+m-1})X_{2}(x^{\prime}_{s+m})/2,\end{split}

(93d)

remark from (

-

)
\dfrac{C_{m+1,r,s}^{\dagger}}{C_{m,r,s}^{\dagger}}=\dfrac{(x_{r}-x^{\prime}_{s+m+1})(y_{r-1}-y_{r+m})}{(x_{r}-x_{r+m})(y_{r-1}-y^{\prime}_{s+m})}=\dfrac{(x^{\prime}_{s}-x^{\prime}_{s+m+1})(y^{\prime}_{s}-y_{r+m})}{(x^{\prime}_{s}-x_{r+m})(y^{\prime}_{s}-y^{\prime}_{s+m})}
transposing (

) .

9.
Second order difference operators.

9.1.
Definitions and examples

A linear difference operator of the second order must involve

f(x_{n})
and the two neighboring values
f(x_{n\pm 1})

\alpha_{n}f(x_{n+1})+\beta_{n}f(x_{n})+\gamma_{n}f(x_{n-1})

the operator
\mathcal{S}
extending the second derivative operator must
vanish on the constants,
\alpha_{n}+\beta_{n}+\gamma_{n}=0
, so

\mathcal{S}f(x_{n})=\alpha_{n}(f(x_{n+1})-f(x_{n}))+\gamma_{n}(f(x_{n-1})-f(x_{n}))

remains. Should the operator vanish on
x
,

\alpha_{n}(x_{n+1}-x_{n})+\gamma_{n}(x_{n-1}-x_{n})=0
, whence the
operator

\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}\mathcal{D}f(x_{n})=-\left(\dfrac{f(x_{n+1})-f(x_{n})}{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}-\dfrac{f(x_{n})-f(x_{n-1})}{x_{n}-x_{n-1}}\right)/(y_{n}-y_{n-1})
, see also §
9.4.3
.

But
f(x)=x
is a special case of a rational function of first degree,we
consider a family of operators, each of them vanishing on

(\alpha^{\prime}x+\beta^{\prime})/(\gamma^{\prime}x+\delta^{\prime})
for some
(\alpha^{\prime},\dotsc,\delta^{\prime})
, then

\alpha_{n}\dfrac{x_{n+1}-x_{n}}{(x_{n+1}-t)(x_{n}-t)}+\gamma_{n}\dfrac{x_{n-1}-x_{n}}{(x_{n}-t)(x_{n-1}-t)}=0
, where
t=-\delta^{\prime}/\gamma^{\prime}
, so, using

(x_{n+1}-t)(x_{n}-t)=F(t,y_{n})/Y_{2}(y_{n})=X_{2}(x_{\kappa})(y_{n}-y_{\kappa})(y_{n}-y_{\kappa-1})/Y_{2}(y_{n})
,
where
t=x_{\kappa}
for some
\kappa
,

\alpha_{n}=\dfrac{(y_{n}-y_{\kappa})(y_{n}-y_{\kappa-1})}{Y_{2}(y_{n})(x_{n+1}-x_{n})}
,

\gamma_{n}=\dfrac{(y_{n-1}-y_{\kappa})(y_{n-1}-y_{\kappa-1})}{Y_{2}(y_{n-1})(x_{n}-x_{n-1})}
, up to multiplication by constants. We will use

\mathcal{S}f(x)=\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}\left(\dfrac{(y-y_{\kappa})(y-y_{\kappa-1})}{Y_{2}(y)}\right)\mathcal{D}f(x).

Applying this
\mathcal{S}
to the product
P_{m}(x)=\dfrac{(x-x_{0})\dotsb(x-x_{m-1})}{(x-x^{\prime}_{0})\dotsb(x-x^{\prime}_{m-1})}
yields interesting results. From (
34
)

\mathcal{S}P_{m}(x)=\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}\dfrac{(y-y_{\kappa})(y-y_{\kappa-1})}{Y_{2}(y)}C_{m,0,0}Y_{2}(y)\dfrac{(y-y_{0})\dotsb(y-y_{m-2})}{(y-y^{\prime}_{-1})\dotsb(y-y^{\prime}_{m-1})}
. As seen before, a big simplification occurs when

y_{\kappa}=y^{\prime}_{0}
, then,

\mathcal{S}P_{m}(x)=\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}C_{m,0,0}\dfrac{(y-y_{0})\dotsb(y-y_{m-2})}{(y-y^{\prime}_{1})\dotsb(y-y^{\prime}_{m-1})}

Finally,

\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}\left(\dfrac{(y-y^{\prime}_{0})(y-y^{\prime}_{-1})}{Y_{2}(y)}\right)\mathcal{D}\dfrac{(x-x_{0})\dotsb(x-x_{m-1})}{(x-x^{\prime}_{0})\dotsb(x-x^{\prime}_{m-1})}=C_{m,0,0}C^{\dagger}_{m-1,0,1}X_{2}(x)\dfrac{(x-x_{1})\dotsb(x-x_{m-2})}{(x-x^{\prime}_{1})\dotsb(x-x^{\prime}_{m})},

(94)

and we have table
6
, from (
90
)

f(x_{n-1})\times

f(x_{n})\times

f(x_{n+1})\times

(\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}w(y)\mathcal{D}f)(x_{n})
