9.2.
Adjoint operators

We defined in §
8.1
the adjoint of
\mathcal{D}
with respect to the
bilinear form
\sum_{k}f(x_{k})g(x_{k})\Delta y_{k-1}
. Keeping this bilinear form,
the adjoint of a general linear second order operator

\mathcal{A}f(x_{n})=\alpha_{n}f(x_{n+1})+\beta_{n}f(x_{n})+\gamma_{n}f(x_{n-1})
is such that

\sum_{n}\Delta y_{n-1}(\alpha_{n}f(x_{n+1})+\beta_{n}f(x_{n})+\gamma_{n}f(x_{n-1}))g(x_{n})
is reordered as

\sum_{n}f(x_{n})[g(x_{n-1})\alpha_{n-1}\Delta y_{n-2}+(x_{n})\beta_{n}\Delta y_{n-1}+g(x_{n+1})\gamma_{n+1}\Delta y_{n}]

so, the adjoint is

\mathcal{A}^{\dagger}g(x_{n})=\dfrac{g(x_{n-1})\alpha_{n-1}\Delta y_{n-2}+g(x_{n})\beta_{n}\Delta y_{n-1}+g(x_{n+1})\gamma_{n+1}\Delta y_{n}}{\Delta y_{n-1}}
.

For instance, the adjoint of
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}w(y)\mathcal{D}
is, from
table
6
,

\dfrac{-g(x_{n-1})w(y_{n-1})/\Delta x_{n-1}+g(x_{n})(w(y_{n-1})/\Delta x_{-1}+w(y_{n})/\Delta x_{n})-g(x_{n+1})w(y_{n})/\Delta x_{n}}{\Delta y_{n-1}}

at
g(x_{n})
, and we recover the same operator!

Definition.
Let
f
and
g
be defined on at least one lattice
\{x_{n}\}
. The
operator
\mathcal{A}
is selfadjoint
with respect to the bilinear form
\langle.,.\rangle
if

\langle f\mathcal{A}g\rangle
is symmetric in
f
and
g
.

with respect to the bilinear form
\sum_{n}\omega_{n}f(x_{n})g(x_{n})
,
one must look at

\sum_{n}(y_{n}-y_{n-1})f(x_{n})(\mathcal{A}g)(x_{n})

With the matrix formulation

\langle f\mathcal{A}g\rangle\\
=[\dotsb,f(x_{n-1},f(x_{n}),f(x_{n+1}),\dotsb]\begin{bmatrix}\ddots&&&&\\
&\omega_{n-1}\beta_{n-1}&\omega_{n-1}\alpha_{n-1}&&\\
&\omega_{n}\gamma_{n}&\omega_{n}\beta_{n}&\omega_{n}\alpha_{n}&\\
&&\omega_{n+1}\gamma_{n+1}&\omega_{n+1}\beta_{n+1}&\\
&&&&\ddots\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\vdots\\
g(x_{n-1})\\
g(x_{n})\\
g(x_{n+1})\\
\vdots\end{bmatrix}

indeed symmetric
f
and
g
if the matrix is symmetric,
\omega_{n}\alpha_{n}=\omega_{n+1}\gamma_{n+1}

We also check the selfadjointness of
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}(w(y)\mathcal{D})

on the matrix form
\alpha_{n}\Delta y_{n-1}=\gamma_{n+1}\Delta y_{n}=w(y_{n})/\Delta x_{n}
from table
6
.

For operators involving
\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}
, which is not the true adjoint of
\mathcal{M}
,
it is better to check
by the book
: let
\mathcal{L}=a(x)(\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}+b(x)\mathcal{D}^{\dagger})(\mathcal{M}+c(y)\mathcal{D})
,
then, with
h(y)=(\mathcal{M}g)(y)+c(y)(\mathcal{D}g)(y)
,

\langle f,\mathcal{L}g\rangle=\sum_{n}\omega_{n}f(x_{n})a(x_{n})((\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}+b(x)\mathcal{D}^{\dagger})h)(x_{n})\\
=\sum_{n}\omega_{n}f(x_{n})a(x_{n})\left[\left(\dfrac{1}{2}-\dfrac{b(x_{n})}{y_{n}-y_{n-1}}\right)h(y_{n})+\left(\dfrac{1}{2}+\dfrac{b(x_{n})}{y_{n}-y_{n-1}}\right)h(y_{n-1})\right]\\
=\sum_{n}h(y_{n})\left[\omega_{n+1}f(x_{n+1})a(x_{n+1})\left(\dfrac{1}{2}+\dfrac{b(x_{n+1})}{y_{n+1}-y_{n}}\right)+\omega_{n}f(x_{n})a(x_{n})\left(\dfrac{1}{2}-\dfrac{b(x_{n})}{y_{n}-y_{n-1}}\right)\right]\\
=\sum_{n}[\mathcal{M}g)(y_{n})+c(y_{n})(\mathcal{D}g)(y_{n})]\left[(\mathcal{M}a\omega f)(y_{n})+(x_{n+1}-x_{n})\left(\mathcal{D}\dfrac{\omega fab}{y_{n}-y_{n-1}}\right)(y_{n})\right]

9.3.
Hypergeometric difference equation and expansion

An elliptic hypergeometric expansion is a (formal) sum
\sum_{n}R_{1}R_{2}\dots R_{n}
,
where
R_{n}
is a rational function
R(x_{n},y_{n},x^{\prime}_{n},y^{\prime}_{n},\dotsc,x_{n}^{(r)},y_{n}^{(r)})
,
and where
(x_{n},y_{n}),\dotsc,(x_{n}^{(r)},y_{n}^{(r)})
are sequences of points on the
same biquadratic curve
F(x,y)=0
.

9.4.
Some classical hypergeometric expansions

9.4.1.
The expansion of
{}_{2}F_{1}
from the differential equation

Let
\mu(x)f^{\prime\prime}(x)+\nu(x)f^{\prime}(x)-\lambda f(x)=0
where
\mu,\nu,\lambda
are polynomials of
degrees
2,1,0
.

Put a (formal) Taylor expansion
f(x)=\sum_{0}^{\infty}c_{n}(x-a)^{n}
in the equation
to get

[\mu(a)+\mu^{\prime}(a)(x-a)+\mu^{\prime\prime}(a)(x-a)^{2}/2]\sum_{0}^{\infty}n(n-1)c_{n}(x-a)^{n-2}+[\nu(a)+\nu^{\prime}(a)(x-a)]\sum_{0}^{\infty}nc_{n}(x-a)^{n-1}-\lambda\sum_{0}^{\infty}c_{n}(x-a)^{n}=0
, so, a three-term recurrence relation
linking
c_{n}
,
c_{n+1}
and
c_{n+2}

\mu(a)(n+2)(n+1)c_{n+2}+\mu^{\prime}(a)(n+1)nc_{n+1}+\mu^{\prime\prime}(a)n(n-1)c_{n}/2+\nu(a)(n+1)c_{n+1}+\nu^{\prime}(a)nc_{n}-\lambda c_{n}=0
,
n=0,1,\dotsc
follows.

A big simplification occurs if
x=a
is a root of
\mu(x)=0
(singular
point), then

c_{n+1}=\dfrac{\lambda-n\nu^{\prime}(a)-n(n-1)\mu^{\prime\prime}(a)/2}{(n+1)(n\mu^{\prime}(a)+\nu(a))}c_{n}
, so,

c_{n}=\dfrac{\Gamma(\alpha+n)\Gamma(\beta+n)}{n!\Gamma(\gamma+n)}(-\mu^{\prime\prime}(a)/(2\mu^{\prime}(a)))^{n}c_{0}
,
where
-\alpha
and
-\beta
are the
n-
roots of
\lambda-n\nu^{\prime}(a)-n(n-1)\mu^{\prime\prime}(a)/2=0
, and where
\gamma=\nu(a)/\mu^{\prime}(a)
,

leading to a hypergeometric
[
141
, 10.3, 10.7]

f(x)={}_{2}F_{1}(\alpha,\beta;\gamma;-\mu^{\prime}(a)(x-a)/(2\mu^{\prime}(a))

Another technique:

Derivate to get
\mu(x)f^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)+(\mu^{\prime}(x)+\nu(x))f^{\prime\prime}(x)+(\nu^{\prime}-\lambda)f^{\prime}(x)=0
has the same form
with same degrees!

The equation for
f^{(n)}(x)
has the same
\mu(x)
, and
\nu_{n}(x)=n\mu^{\prime}(x)+\nu(x)
,
and the new constant coefficient
\lambda_{n+1}=\lambda_{n}-\nu^{\prime}_{n}=\lambda_{n}-n\mu^{\prime\prime}-\nu^{\prime}
, or
\lambda_{n}=-n(n-1)\mu^{\prime\prime}/2-n\nu^{\prime}+\lambda

If
x=a
is a singular point
(\mu(a)=0)
with, say,
f(a)=1
, we have

f^{(n+1)}(a)/f^{(n)}(a)=-\lambda_{n}/\nu_{n}(a)=[n(n-1)\mu^{\prime\prime}/2+n\nu^{\prime}-\lambda]/(n\mu^{\prime}(a)+\nu(a))
leading to the same
Taylor expansion about
a
.

Darboux-Bäcklund
[
84
]

9.4.2.
Discrete equation on the lattice
\{x_{n}=x_{0}+nh\}
.

\mu(x)\Delta\nabla f(x)/h^{2}+\nu(x)(\Delta+\nabla)f(x)/(2h)-\lambda f(x)=0

[

,

]

with degrees
2,1,0

with the operators
\Delta
and
\nabla
of table

p.
1

note that
\Delta\nabla f(x)=\nabla\Delta f(x)=f(x+h)-2f(x)+f(x-h)

we now put
f(x)=\sum_{0}^{\infty}c_{n}(x-a)(x-a-h)\dotsb(x-a-(n-1)h)
,

then
\Delta f(x)=\sum nhc_{n}(x-a)\dotsb(x-a-(n-2)h)
,

\nabla f(x)=\sum nhc_{n}(x-a-h)\dotsb(x-a-(n-1)h)
,

\Delta\nabla f(x)=\sum n(n-1)h^{2}c_{n}(x-a-h)\dotsb(x-a-(n-2)h)

0=\sum\{n(n-1)\mu(x)+n\nu(x)(x-a-(n-1)h)/2\}c_{n}(x-a-h)\dotsb(x-a-(n-2)h)+\sum\{n\nu(x)/2-\lambda(x-a-(n-1)h)\}c_{n}(x-a)\dotsb(x-a-(n-2)h)

we find a combination of products
(x-a-h)(x-a-2h)\dotsb(x-a-mh)
,
m=0,1,\dotsc
, each product involving
c_{m+2},c_{m+1}
and
c_{m}
.

A big simplification now occurs if
x=a
is a root of
\mu(x)-h\nu(x)/2=0
, then,
let
\mu(x)-h\nu(x)/2=\mu^{\prime\prime}(x-a)(x-b)/2
and we have

0=\sum n(n-1)\mu^{\prime\prime}c_{n}(x-a)\dotsb(x-a-(n-2)h)(x-b=x-a-(n-1)h+a-b+(n-1)h)/2+\sum nc_{n}(x-a)\dotsb(x-a-(n-2)h)(\nu(x)=\nu(a+(n-1)h)+\nu^{\prime}(x-a-(n-1)h))-\lambda\sum c_{n}(x-a)\dotsb(x-a-(n-1)h)
and

n(n-1)\mu^{\prime\prime}c_{n}/2+(n+1)n\mu^{\prime\prime}(a-b+nh)c_{n+1}/2+n\nu^{\prime}c_{n}+(n+1)\nu(a+nh)c_{n+1}-\lambda c_{n}=0

follows, or

c_{n+1}=\dfrac{\lambda-n\nu^{\prime}-n(n-1)\mu^{\prime\prime}/2}{(n+1)[n(a-b+nh)\mu^{\prime\prime}/2+\nu(a+nh)]}c_{n}

gives
{}_{3}F_{2}

[
104
, §2.2.1, 2.7.3.3]

[
108
, §18.20]

[
76
, chap. 5 & 9]

with the
3-)
term recurrence rel.

(\mu(x)/h^{2}-\nu(x)/(2h))f(x-h)-(2\mu(x)/h^{2}+\lambda)f(x)+(\mu(x)/h^{2}+\nu(x)/(2h))f(x+h)=0
,
we see that the condition
\mu(a)-h\nu(a)/2=0
shows that
x=a
is a singular point,
that
f(a+h)
is not a linear combination of
f(a-h)
and
f(a)

but depends only on
f(a):f(a+h)=\dfrac{\lambda h^{2}+2\mu(a)}{\mu(a)+h\nu(a)/2}f(a)=\dfrac{\lambda h+\nu(a)}{\nu(a)}f(a)

other way: apply
\Delta
and see that we have an equation of the same form
for
\Delta f

\Delta(\mu\Delta\nabla)f/h^{2}+\Delta(\nu(\Delta+\nabla))f/(2h)-\lambda\Delta f=0

we need
\Delta(uv)(x)=u(x+h)v(x+h)-u(x)v(x)=v(x+h)\Delta u(x)+u(x)\Delta v(x)

\mu(x+h)\Delta^{2}\nabla f/h^{2}+(\Delta\mu)\Delta\nabla f/h^{2}+\nu(x+h)(\Delta^{2}+\Delta\nabla)f/(2h)+(\Delta\nu)(\Delta+\nabla)f/(2h)-\lambda\Delta f=0

\{\mu(x+h)\Delta\nabla/h^{2}+(\Delta\mu)\nabla/h^{2}+\nu(x+h)(\Delta+\nabla)/(2h)+(\Delta\nu)(2-\nabla)/(2h)-\lambda\}\Delta f=0

using
\nabla-\Delta=-\Delta\nabla
,

\{\mu(x+h)\Delta\nabla/h^{2}+(\Delta(\mu-h\nu/2))(\Delta+\nabla-\Delta\nabla)/(2h^{2})+\nu(x+h)(\Delta+\nabla)/(2h)+(\Delta\nu)/h-\lambda\}\Delta f=0

using
\nabla=(\nabla+\Delta)/2-\Delta\nabla/2

so,
\mu_{\text{new}}=\mu(x+h)-\Delta(\mu-h\nu/2)/2=\mu+\Delta(\mu+h\nu/2)/2
,

\nu_{\text{new}}=\Delta(\mu-h\nu/2)/h+\nu(x+h)=\nu(x+h)+\Delta\mu^{\prime\prime}(x-a)(x-b)/(2h)=\nu(x+h)+\mu^{\prime\prime}(x-(a+b-h)/2)

\lambda_{\text{new}}=\lambda-\Delta\nu/h=\lambda-\nu^{\prime}

note that
\mu-h\nu/2
is unchanged:
x=a
remains a singular point of the new equation. After
n
steps,
\nu_{n+1}(x)=\nu_{n}(x+h)+\mu^{\prime\prime}(x+nh-(a+b-h)/2)
, so,

\nu_{n}(x)=\nu(x+nh)+n\mu^{\prime\prime}(x-(a+b-h)/2)+n(n-1)\mu^{\prime\prime}h/2=\nu(x+nh)+n\mu^{\prime\prime}(x-(a+b-nh)/2)
,

\lambda_{n+1}=\lambda_{n}-\nu^{\prime}_{n}=\lambda_{n}-\nu^{\prime}-n\mu^{\prime\prime}
, so

\lambda_{n}=\lambda-n\nu^{\prime}-n(n-1)\mu^{\prime\prime}/2
, and

\dfrac{\Delta^{n+1}f(a)}{\Delta^{n}f(a)}=\dfrac{\lambda_{n}}{\nu_{n}(a)}=\dfrac{\lambda-n\nu^{\prime}-n(n-1)\mu^{\prime\prime}/2}{\nu(a+nh)+n\mu^{\prime\prime}(a-b+nh)/2}
is confirmed.

9.4.3.
q-
Sturm-Liouville difference equation.

We follow Marcellán and Medem
[
95
]
, and consider
\mathcal{L}=\mu\Theta\Theta^{*}+\nu\Theta^{*}-\lambda
, with
operators
\Theta f(x)=(f(qx)-f(x))/(qx-x),\Theta^{*}f(x)=(f(x/q)-f(x))/(x/q-x)
, and
\mu,\nu
are polynomials of degrees
\leqslant 2,1
.

When
\mathcal{L}f
is written as a combination of
f(qx),f(x),f(x/q)
, we have

\mu(x)f(qx)/(x-qx)^{2}
and
q[\mu(x)+(x-qx)\nu(x)]f(x/q)/(x-qx)^{2}
,
and the zeros of
\mu
and
\mu(x)+(x-qx)\nu(x))
, singular points of
\mathcal{L}
, are involved in the support
of the relevant orthogonality measure
[
95
, eq. (2.6), table 3.1]
.

9.4.4.
ANSUW difference equation.

Askey and Wilson
[
8
, eq. (5.16)]
find the equation
D_{q}[w_{1}^{\prime}x)D_{q}p_{n}(x)]+\lambda_{n}w_{2}(x)p_{n}(x)=0

for their orthogonal polynomials
p_{n}
, where
D_{q}f(x)=\dfrac{f(q^{1/2}x)-f(q^{-1/2}x)}{q^{1/2}x-q^{-1/2}x}
.

Nikiforov et al.
[
104
, eq. (3.1.
5)]
find
\tilde{\sigma}[x(s)]\dfrac{\Delta}{\Delta x(s-1/2)}\left[\dfrac{\nabla f(x(s))}{\nabla x(s)}\right]+\dfrac{\tilde{\tau}[x(s)]}{2}\left[\dfrac{\Delta f(x(s))}{\Delta x(s)}+\dfrac{\nabla f(x(s))}{\nabla x(s)}\right]+\lambda f(x(s))=0

on the NSU lattice
x(s)
of table
1
.

We recognize the
\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}
, and
\mathcal{M}
operators
of the present sudy.

See also
[
10
,
72
,
81
,
134
,
135
]
for interesting constructions and discussions.

9.5.
Second order elliptic difference equation

We introduce a set of second order equations allowing hypergeometric expansions
[
117
]
.

Theorem.

Let
\{x_{n},y_{n}\}
and
\{x^{\prime}_{n},y^{\prime}_{n}\}
,

n\in\mathbb{Z}
, be two
elliptic sequences on the same biquadratic curve
F(x,y)=X_{0}(x)+X_{1}(x)y+X_{2}(x)y^{2}=Y_{0}(y)+xY_{1}(y)+x^{2}Y_{2}(y)=0
, and
let
\mathcal{L}
be the second order difference operator

\mathcal{L}=\dfrac{1}{x-x^{\prime}_{1}}\left(\dfrac{\mu(x)}{X_{2}(x)}\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}+\nu(x)\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}\right)\left(\dfrac{(y-y^{\prime}_{-1})(y-y^{\prime}_{0})}{Y_{2}(y)}\mathcal{D}\right)\\
-\lambda\left(\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}+\dfrac{y^{\prime}_{0}-y^{\prime}_{-1}}{2}\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}\right)\left(\mathcal{M}-\dfrac{R(y)}{Y_{2}(y)}\mathcal{D}\right),

(95)

where
\mu
and
\nu
are polynomials of degrees 3 and 1,

\lambda
is a constant, and
R
is the polynomial

R(y)=\dfrac{(y-y_{-1})Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{-1})(x^{\prime}_{0}-x^{\prime}_{-1})+(y-y^{\prime}_{-1})Y_{2}(y_{-1})(x_{0}-x_{-1})}{2(y^{\prime}_{-1}-y_{-1})}
.

Moreover,

\begin{split}2\mu(x_{0})-(y_{0}-y_{-1})X_{2}(x_{0})\nu(x_{0})&=0,\\
2\mu(x^{\prime}_{1})+(y^{\prime}_{1}-y^{\prime}_{0})X_{2}(x^{\prime}_{1})\nu(x^{\prime}_{1})&=0.\end{split}

(96)

The equation
\mathcal{L}\phi=0
has then a solution
with an interpolating expansion

\phi(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{n}\dfrac{(x-x_{0})\dotsb(x-x_{n-1})}{(x-x^{\prime}_{0})\dotsb(x-x^{\prime}_{n-1})}

(97)

where
c_{0}=f(x_{0})

may be chosen at will, and with

c_{n}=c_{0}(x_{n-1}-x^{\prime}_{n})\dfrac{\zeta_{0}\dotsb\zeta_{n-1}}{\alpha_{0}\dotsb\alpha_{n-1}},n=1,2,\dotsc

with

\alpha_{n}=\left(-\dfrac{\mu(x_{n})}{(y_{n}-y_{n-1})X_{2}(x_{n})}+\dfrac{\nu(x_{n})}{2}\right)\dfrac{(y_{n}-y^{\prime}_{-1})(y_{n}-y^{\prime}_{0})}{(x_{n+1}-x_{n})(x_{n}-x^{\prime}_{1})Y_{2}(y_{n})}\\
-\dfrac{\lambda}{2}\left[\left(1-\dfrac{y^{\prime}_{0}-y^{\prime}_{-1}}{y_{n}-y_{n-1}}\right)\left(\dfrac{1}{2}-\dfrac{R(y_{n})}{(x_{n+1}-x_{n})Y_{2}(y_{n})}\right)\right],

(98)

\left\{\begin{split}\zeta_{0}&=\dfrac{\lambda}{(x_{0}-x^{\prime}_{1})(x_{1}-x_{0})},\\
\zeta_{1}&=\dfrac{(x_{0}-x^{\prime}_{1})(x_{2}-x^{\prime}_{1})}{(x_{1}-x^{\prime}_{2})(x_{2}-x_{1})}\left(\dfrac{(x_{1}-x_{0})(x_{2}-x^{\prime}_{0})(\alpha_{0}-\beta_{1})}{(x_{1}-x^{\prime}_{0})(x_{2}-x_{0})}-\alpha_{1}\right),\\
\zeta_{n}&=\gamma^{\prime}_{n}Z_{n},n=2,3\dotsc\end{split}\right.,

(99)

\gamma^{\prime}_{n}=\left(-\dfrac{\mu(x^{\prime}_{n})}{(y^{\prime}_{n}-y^{\prime}_{n-1})X_{2}(x^{\prime}_{n})}-\dfrac{\nu(x^{\prime}_{n})}{2}\right)\dfrac{(y^{\prime}_{n-1}-y^{\prime}_{-1})(y^{\prime}_{n-1}-y^{\prime}_{0})}{(x^{\prime}_{n}-x^{\prime}_{n-1})(x^{\prime}_{n}-x^{\prime}_{1})Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{n-1})}\\
-\dfrac{\lambda}{2}\left[\left(1+\dfrac{y^{\prime}_{0}-y^{\prime}_{-1}}{y^{\prime}_{n}-y^{\prime}_{n-1}}\right)\left(\dfrac{1}{2}+\dfrac{R(y^{\prime}_{n-1})}{(x^{\prime}_{n}-x^{\prime}_{n-1})Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{n-1})}\right)\right],

(100)

The formula for
\beta_{1}
and
Z_{n}
will be given later on, and

R
is here the
R_{0,0}
of (
42
).

Note that
R
achieves the linear interpolation of
R(y_{-1})=-(x_{0}-x_{-1})Y_{2}(y_{-1})/2,R(y^{\prime}_{-1})=(x^{\prime}_{0}-x^{\prime}_{-1})Y_{2}(y^{\prime}_{-1})/2
,

Z_{n}
does not depend on
\lambda
, all the
\zeta_{n}
s are first degree polynomials in
\lambda
.

The first part of
\mathcal{L}
generalizes the terms

a(x)d^{2}/dx^{2}+b(x)d/dx
of the original differential hypergeometric
operator. The second part corresponds to the last constant term.
Why not finish simply with
-\lambda
times the identity operator?
This obvious choice does not lead to a workable ratio
c_{n+1}/c_{n}

(the author tried). One hopes that a less awkward construction
will be found someday.

We start the proof by writing
\mathcal{L}\phi=0
in (
95
) as a recurrence relation
at
x_{n}
, according to (
87
) and (
89
),

\alpha_{n}\phi(x_{n+1})+\beta_{n}\phi(x_{n})+\gamma_{n}\phi(x_{n-1})=0,

(101)

(\mathcal{L}\phi)(x_{n})=\dfrac{1}{x-x^{\prime}_{1}}\left[\left(\dfrac{\mu(x_{n})}{(y_{n}-y_{n-1})X_{2}(x_{n})}+\dfrac{\nu(x_{n})}{2}\right)\dfrac{(y_{n-1}-y^{\prime}_{-1})(y_{n-1}-y^{\prime}_{0})(\mathcal{D}\phi)(y_{n-1})}{Y_{2}(y_{n-1})}\right.\\
\left.\ \ \ \ \ \ +\left(\dfrac{-\mu(x_{n})}{(y_{n}-y_{n-1})X_{2}(x_{n})}+\dfrac{\nu(x_{n})}{2}\right)\dfrac{(y_{n}-y^{\prime}_{-1})(y_{n}-y^{\prime}_{0})(\mathcal{D}\phi)(y_{n})}{Y_{2}(y_{n})}\right]

-\dfrac{\lambda}{2}\left[\left(1+\dfrac{y^{\prime}_{0}-y^{\prime}_{-1}}{y_{n}-y_{n-1}}\right)\left(\mathcal{M}\phi-\dfrac{R\mathcal{D}\phi}{Y_{2}}\right)(y_{n-1})+\left(1-\dfrac{y^{\prime}_{0}-y^{\prime}_{-1}}{y_{n}-y_{n-1}}\right)\left(\mathcal{M}\phi-\dfrac{R\mathcal{D}\phi}{Y_{2}}\right)(y_{n})\right]
,

and we extract the coefficients of
\phi(x_{n+1})
ad
\phi(x_{n-1})
from
(\mathcal{D}\phi)(y_{n})
and
(\mathcal{M}\phi)(y_{n})
, giving

\alpha_{n}
of (
98
),

\gamma_{n}=-\left(\dfrac{\mu(x_{n})}{(y_{n}-y_{n-1})X_{2}(x_{n})}+\dfrac{\nu(x_{n})}{2}\right)\dfrac{(y_{n-1}-y^{\prime}_{-1})(y_{n-1}-y^{\prime}_{0})}{(x_{n}-x_{n-1})(x_{n}-x^{\prime}_{1})Y_{2}(y_{n-1})}\\
-\dfrac{\lambda}{2}\left[\left(1+\dfrac{y^{\prime}_{0}-y^{\prime}_{-1}}{y_{n}-y_{n-1}}\right)\left(\dfrac{1}{2}+\dfrac{R(y_{n-1})}{(x_{n}-x_{n-1})Y_{2}(y_{n-1})}\right)\right]

(102)

,

and

\alpha_{n}+\beta_{n}+\gamma_{n}=\mathcal{L}1=-\lambda.

(103)

In matrix form

\begin{split}\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{0}&\alpha_{0}&&\\

&\gamma_{2}&\beta_{2}&\alpha_{2}\\

\phi(x_{0}),\phi(x_{1}),\dotsc
,

One has

,etc.: each

is a polynomial in

.

Each

depends on the two earlier values of
\phi
, but

,

\phi(x_{2})=-\dfrac{\beta_{1}\phi(x_{1})+\gamma_{1}\phi(x_{0})}{\alpha_{1}}=\phi(x_{0})\dfrac{\beta_{1}\beta_{0}-\gamma_{1}\alpha_{0}}{\alpha_{0}\alpha_{1}}=\phi(x_{0})\dfrac{-\beta_{1}\lambda+\lambda\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}\alpha_{0}}{\alpha_{0}\alpha_{1}}
. Each

, and so is

\dfrac{c_{1}}{c_{0}}=\dfrac{x_{1}-x^{\prime}_{0}}{x_{1}-x_{0}}\left[\dfrac{\phi(x_{1})}{\phi(x_{0})}-1\right]=\dfrac{(x_{1}-x^{\prime}_{0})\lambda}{(x_{1}-x_{0})\alpha_{0}}
,
we shall also need the next step

\dfrac{c_{2}}{c_{1}}=\dfrac{\dfrac{(x_{2}-x^{\prime}_{0})(x_{2}-x^{\prime}_{1})}{(x_{2}-x_{0})(x_{2}-x_{1})}\left(\dfrac{f(x_{2})}{f(x_{0})}-1-\dfrac{c_{1}(x_{2}-x_{0})}{c_{0}(x_{2}-x^{\prime}_{0})}\right)}{c_{1}/c_{0}}\\
=\dfrac{(x_{2}-x^{\prime}_{0})(x_{2}-x^{\prime}_{1})}{(x_{2}-x_{0})(x_{2}-x_{1})}\left((x_{1}-x_{0})\dfrac{-\beta_{1}\lambda+\lambda\alpha_{0}}{(x_{1}-x^{\prime}_{0})\lambda\alpha_{1}}-\dfrac{x_{2}-x_{0}}{x_{2}-x^{\prime}_{0}}\right)=\dfrac{x_{2}-x^{\prime}_{1}}{x_{2}-x_{1}}\left(\dfrac{(x_{1}-x_{0})(x_{2}-x^{\prime}_{0})(\alpha_{0}-\beta_{1})}{(x_{1}-x^{\prime}_{0})(x_{2}-x_{0})\alpha_{1}}-1\right)

whence
\zeta_{0}
and

9.5.1.

Lemma.

let
P_{m}(x)=\dfrac{(x-x_{0})\dotsb(x-x_{m-1})}{(x-x^{\prime}_{0})\dotsb(x-x^{\prime}_{m-1})}

Then,

\mathcal{L}P_{m}(x)\\
=\dfrac{1}{x-x^{\prime}_{1}}\left(\dfrac{\mu(x)}{X_{2}(x)}\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}+\nu(x)\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}\right)\left(\dfrac{(y-y^{\prime}_{-1})(y-y^{\prime}_{0})}{Y_{2}(y)}C_{m,0,0}Y_{2}(y)\dfrac{(y-y_{0})\dotsb(y-y_{m-2})}{(y-y^{\prime}_{-1})\dotsb(y-y^{\prime}_{m-1})}\right)\\
-\lambda\left(\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}+\dfrac{y^{\prime}_{0}-y^{\prime}_{-1}}{2X_{2}(x)}\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}\right)\rho_{m,0,0}C_{m,0,0}\dfrac{(y-y_{-1})\dotsb(y-y_{m-2})}{(y-y^{\prime}_{0})\dotsb(y-y^{\prime}_{m-1})}

9.5.3.
Biorthogonality of eigenfunctions

\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{0}&\alpha_{0}&&\\
\gamma_{1}&\beta_{1}&\alpha_{1}&\\
&\gamma_{2}&\beta_{2}&\alpha_{2}\\
&&\ddots&\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\phi(x_{0})\\
\phi(x_{1})\\
\phi(x_{2})\\
\vdots\end{bmatrix}

Recall from (
104
) that
\mathsf{A}\mathbf{\phi}+\lambda\mathsf{B}\mathbf{\phi}=0
, where
\mathbf{\phi}
is the column
vector of
\phi(x_{0}),f(x_{1}),
etc.

Consider now a row vector
\mathsf{g}=[g_{0},g_{1},\dotsc]
such that
\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{A}+\lambda\mathsf{B})=0
, i.e.,

g_{1}=-\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\gamma_{1}}g_{0},g_{2}=\dfrac{\beta_{0}\beta_{1}-\alpha_{0}\gamma_{1}}{\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}}g_{0},\dotsc

Is
g_{n}
the value of some function at
x_{n}
?

When
\lambda
is such that
\zeta_{r}=0
, let
\lambda=\lambda_{r}
, the equations
(
101
) are solved by
\phi(x)=\phi_{r}(x)
, a rational function of
degree
r
. So, for any
\mathsf{g}
,
\mathsf{gA}\mathbf{\phi}_{r}+\lambda_{r}\mathsf{gB}\mathbf{\phi}_{r}=0
.

Let
\phi_{r}
and
\phi_{s}
be associated to
\lambda_{r}
and
\lambda_{s}
:

g_{1}^{(s)}=-\dfrac{\beta_{0}(\lambda_{s})}{\gamma_{1}(\lambda_{s})}
, etc.

g^{(s)}(\mathsf{A}+\lambda_{r}\mathsf{B})\phi_{r}=0,g^{(s)}(\mathsf{A}+\lambda_{s}\mathsf{B})\phi_{r}=0
, so,
g^{(s)}\mathsf{B}f_{r}=0
,
if
\lambda_{r}\neq\kappa_{s}
, which is the biorthogonality relation
of left and right eigenvectors (Wilkinson
[
142
, eq. (3.6)]
).

Elliptic hypergeometric functions are usually defined as

\theta(u)=2\sum_{0}^{\infty}(-1)^{m}p^{(m+1/2)^{2}}\sin((2m+1)u)=2p^{1/4}\sin u\prod_{m=1}^{\infty}(1-2p^{2m}\cos 2u+p^{4m})(1-p^{2m})\\

]
.

The theta functions realize a kind of the fudamental theorm of algebra for
elliptic functions, they take into account the zeros and poles of such functions.

[
141
, p. 474]
an elliptic function of the variable
u
, with given zeros and poles in a fundamental parallelogram,
must be a product of ratios
\dfrac{\theta(\pi(u-\text{zero})/\text{period})}{\theta(\pi(u-\text{pole})/\text{period})}
, where ”period” is a period of the elliptic function,
and where
\tau=-i\log p/\pi
is the ratio of two periods.

As seen on §,
x_{n}
is the simplest posible elliptic function of
u=nh
, it is a bivalent
function in any fundamental parallelogram. As we always encounter differences of
the form
x_{n}-x_{m}
, one of the zeros is of course
n=m
, we have a factor

\theta(\pi(n-m)h/(4K))=\theta(\pi(n-m)/n_{\text{period}})
.
As
{\text{sn}}(2K-u)={\text{sn}}(u)

[
3
, 16.8]
etc.
{\text{sn}}(nh+g)={\text{sn}}(mh+g)
at
nh+g=2K-mh-g
,
the other factor is
\theta(\pi((n+m)h+2g-2K)/(4K))=\theta(\pi(n+m)/n_{\text{period}}+\pi g/(2K)-\pi/2)
.

We take again the example of §
3.2
, interpolate
n
to see that
x_{n}=x_{1}
occurs
at
n\approx 3.2
which should be
2(K-g)/h-1=-5.895
from
4K=6.9713,g=-0.1275
, and
h=-0.7641
, to which we subtract
n_{\text{period}}=4K/h=-9.1234
to have 3.228.

\xi_{n}
(as well as
x_{n}

nh+g=\pm K
, giving
n=2.114)

=sn(nh+g)

where

arcsn
(\xi_{0}=(\alpha x_{0}+\beta)/(1+\gamma x_{0}))

Let ”
\mathfrak{t}
” the notation for
\mathfrak{t}(x,y)=\theta\left(\dfrac{\pi(x-y)}{4K}\right)\theta_{2}\left(\dfrac{\pi(x+y)}{4K}\right)
.

The constant is independent of

(it is always good to know what constants depend on),
but still depends on

, and

and

x_{n}-x_{m}=C\dfrac{\mathfrak{t}(nh+g,mh+g)}{\mathfrak{t}(mh+g,\mathfrak{a})\mathfrak{t}(nh+g,\mathfrak{a})},

where

.

11.
Scholium.

We saw in §
9.5
a second order elliptic
8
8

8

and any special case: differential, Hahn,
q-
theory, etc. §
9.4
.
difference equation solved by a hypergeometric expansion

\phi(x)=\sum_{0}^{\infty}c_{n}\dfrac{(x-x_{0})\dotsb(x-x_{n-1})}{(x-x^{\prime}_{0})\dotsb(x-x^{\prime}_{n-1})},

where
c_{n+1}/c_{n}
is a rational function of
x_{n},y_{n},x^{\prime}_{n},y^{\prime}_{n}
and
some of their neighbors
x_{n\pm 1}
etc.

For some particular values of a parameter
\lambda
, the expansion of
\phi=\phi_{m}
is reduced to its
m
first terms, so that
\phi_{m}

is a rational function of poles
x^{\prime}_{0},\dotsc,x^{\prime}_{m-1}
.

Do the
\phi_{m}
s enter some orthogonality or biorthogonality scheme? Things are not clear.

Do we have a recurrence relation connecting
\phi_{m},\phi_{m+1},\phi_{m-1}
? I found none.

On the other hand, we found in §
7.2.3
and §
7.2.7
polynomials
\mathsf{A}_{m}
satisfying recurrence relations, as well as biorthogonality conditions

\sum_{j}w_{j}\dfrac{\mathsf{A}_{m}(x^{\prime}_{j})}{(x^{\prime}_{j}-x_{0})(x^{\prime}_{j}-x_{2})\dotsb(x^{\prime}_{j}-x_{2m})}\dfrac{\mathsf{B}_{n}(x^{\prime}_{j})}{(x^{\prime}_{j}-x_{1})(x^{\prime}_{j}-x_{3})\dotsb(x^{\prime}_{j}-x_{2n+1})}=0,n\neq m,

with the weights
w_{j}
satisfying the Pearson’s relations (
62
)

\dfrac{w_{j+1}}{(y^{\prime}_{j+1}-y^{\prime}_{j})X_{2}(x^{\prime}_{j+1})}\left(\dfrac{A(y^{\prime}_{j})}{x^{\prime}_{j+1}-x^{\prime}_{j}}-\dfrac{C(y^{\prime}_{j})}{2}\right)=\dfrac{w_{j}}{(y^{\prime}_{j}-y^{\prime}_{j-1})X_{2}(x^{\prime}_{j})}\left(\dfrac{A(y^{\prime}_{j})}{x^{\prime}_{j+1}-x^{\prime}_{j}}+\dfrac{C(y^{\prime}_{j})}{2}\right)

,
with given polynomial
A
and
C
.

We established for these
\mathsf{A}_{m}
s the existence of second order elliptic difference equations, as extension of Laguerre’s theory
§
7.4.1
.

Do these two families of second order elliptic difference equations have a non empty intersection? The matter will perhaps be settled in the future.
